Complex spinorial forms, Brinkmann four-manifolds, and self-dual bundle gerbes
Alejandro Gil-Garc \́mathbf{i} a, C. S. Shahbazi

Abstract
. We develop the differential theory of complex spinorial forms associated with irreducible complex spinors across all dimensions and signatures. This framework enables the study of constrained parallelicity conditions for irreducible complex spinors by reformulating them as equivalent differential systems for exterior forms within a prescribed semi-algebraic body of the Kähler-Atiyah bundle. To illustrate this approach, we first apply it to the spin-c Killing spinor equation in low dimensions, refining existing results by relaxing standard assumptions of simply connectedness and completeness. Then, we proceed to apply our framework to supersymmetry conditions in supergravity, and we prove that every quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity belongs to an explicit four-parameter family of geodesically complete, globally hyperbolic gyratonic Brinkmann waves with spherical wave fronts. Finally, we study the quasi-supersymmetric solutions of six-dimensional minimal supergravity, defined by a system that couples a self-dual curving on a bundle gerbe to a Lorentzian metric with an irreducible chiral spinor parallel under a metric connection with totally skew-symmetric torsion given by the curvature of the aforementioned curving. Along the way, we prove that a Lorentzian six-manifold admits a skew-torsion parallel spinor with an integrable screen bundle only if it admits a foliation whose leaves are locally conformally Kähler complex surfaces. Keywords: complex spinors, spinorial forms, bundle gerbes, Kundt and Brinkmann space-times, supersymmetry, supergravity MSC2020: Primary 53C27; Secondary 53C50, 83C35, 83C60, 83E50



1.
Introduction

The main goal of this article is to develop a geometric framework that enables the study of any constrained parallelicity condition for an irreducible complex spinor in terms of an equivalent differential system for an algebraically constrained differential form, which is interpreted as the algebraic square of the original constrained parallel spinor
[
3
,
23
,
30
]
. The development of this general framework, to which we refer as the
theory of spinorial forms
, is inspired by the
method of bilinears

[
55
,
22
,
25
,
39
]
, see also
[
47
,
56
]
, which emerged in the supergravity literature as a formalism for classifying and studying supersymmetric solutions. In line with its physical origin, we expect the theory of spinorial forms to be especially relevant in the following cases:

•

Generally, to study parallel spinors in pseudo-Riemannian signatures and in cases where the representation theory of the spin-c group is yet to be established.

•

To study moduli spaces of solutions to parallelicity conditions for which the metric together with the spinor are both variables, which is the common situation.

•

To study evolution problems involving spinorial equations for which the metric evolves non-trivially, and in particular to Cauchy problems for parallel spinors and supersymmetric configurations in Lorentzian signature, as illustrated in
[
44
,
45
,
51
]
for real spinors of real type.

The reason why the theory of spinorial forms is potentially especially relevant for these cases is that it is a priori
blind
to representation theory, and instead resorts to brute algebraic force. On the other hand, the theory of spinorial forms eliminates any explicit dependence on the choice of spinor bundle, and therefore removes at once any complication associated with dealing with bundles that formally depend on the choice of variable underlying metric
[
4
]
. This hints at the relevance of the theory of spinorial forms in the study of moduli spaces of spinorial parallelicity conditions.

A
real version
of the framework of
complex
spinorial forms presented here was developed for irreducible real spinors of real type in
[
12
,
50
]
, which therefore restricts the signature
(p,q)
of the underlying pseudo-Riemannian metric to
p-q\equiv_{8}0,1,2
. Compared with the real case considered in
opere citato
, the complex case presents new challenges; in particular, it involves two distinct notions of the square of a spinor, as already explained in
[
23
]
. On the other hand, whereas the complex case considered in this article covers every dimension and signature, the real cases considered in
[
12
,
50
]
are restricted to the aforementioned signatures to guarantee that the corresponding irreducible Clifford module is surjective. The real irreducible case of quaternionic type remains, therefore, open.

Outline and main results

In Section
2
, we review the algebraic theory of the square of an irreducible spinor as developed in
[
23
]
, highlighting the existence of two natural squares associated with an irreducible complex spinor, which we call the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares. They are obtained, as their name indicates, through the squaring construction associated with a choice of admissible Hermitian or complex-bilinear pairings, respectively.

In Section
3
, we introduce some geometric preliminaries and define the notion of constrained parallel spinor that we will use throughout the paper, which consists of a section
\eta
of a bundle of irreducible complex spinors
S
over a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
satisfying
\mathcal{D}\eta=0
and
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)=0
for a connection
\mathcal{D}
on
S
and an endomorphism
\mathcal{Q}
of
S
.

In Sections
4
and
5
, we develop the differential theory for constrained parallel spinors through their Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares, respectively, hence establishing the theory of complex spinorial forms. Our main result characterizes constrained parallel spinors in terms of a necessary set of differential equations for their Hermitian squares, or an equivalent differential system for their complex-bilinear squares.

We summarize the results of these two sections in the following abbreviated theorems:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold of even dimension
d
and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given, where
W
is a vector bundle over
M
. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
only if there exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold of odd dimension
d
and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given, where
W
is a vector bundle over
M
. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
only if there exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

In the next two theorems,
\mathcal{L}
is a complex line bundle over
M
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold of even dimension
d
and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given, where
W
is a vector bundle over
M
. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold of odd dimension
d
and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given, where
W
is a vector bundle over
M
. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

We briefly explain the notation used in these theorems. The number
d\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the real dimension of the manifold
M
, while the numbers
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denote the symmetry and adjoint type of the admissible bilinear pairing, respectively. Throughout the article, we will use
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
. We denote by
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
the (complexified) Kähler-Atiyah bundle of
(M,g)
when
d
is even and by
(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\vee)
the (complexified) truncated Kähler-Atiyah bundle of
(M,g)
when
d
is odd. The symbol
\alpha^{(0)}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
denotes the degree zero component of the complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. The map
\pi
is the parity automorphism and
\tau
is the reversion anti-automorphism. They act by
\pi(\alpha)=(-1)^{k}
and
\tau(\alpha)=\smash{(-1)^{\binom{k}{2}}}\alpha
for every
k
-form
\alpha
. Finally,
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
is just a complex number of unit norm.

The
strongly
spin-c condition is included in order to guarantee that every spin-c structure on
(M,g)
reduces to its identity component. We refer the reader to Theorems
4.6
,
4.10
,
5.6
, and
5.10
for more details and for an expanded version of the previous theorems. It is important to recall that the formalism of spinorial forms laid out in the previous theorems establishes a correspondence between spinorial forms and pairs
(g,\eta)
, rather than simply a correspondence between spinorial forms and
bare
spinors
\eta
, hinting at why this formalism is well-adapted to study evolution and moduli problems. The metric is naturally a variable of the system: explicit on one side and implicitly determined on the other.

The remaining sections of the article are devoted to applying the theory of complex spinorial forms established in the previous theorems to three different classes of parallelicity conditions.

In Section
6
, we apply the theory of spinorial forms to complex spin-c Killing spinors in low dimensions
[
42
]
. The main goal is to illustrate the inner workings of the formalism and show how it readily recovers well-known results in the field. Among the several results recovered in this section, we highlight the following:

Corollary 1.5
.

A three-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
admits a real spin-c Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
if and only if it is an
\alpha
-Sasakian manifold with
\alpha=2\lambda
.

Hence, we recover the three-dimensional case of
[
42
, Thm. 4.1]
without assuming either simply connectedness or completeness. This illustrates the applicability of the formalism for studying the global topological and geometric structure of pseudo-Riemannian manifolds that carry parallel spinors. In particular, it is reasonable to expect that it can be used to obtain global geometric and topological results in other related types of Killing spinors studied recently in the literature
[
24
,
38
,
8
]
.

In Section
7
we apply the framework of spinorial forms to the supersymmetry conditions of a particularly elegant supergravity theory, namely Freedman’s minimal gauged four-dimensional supergravity. The bosonic sector of this theory reduces to the Einstein-Maxwell theory with a positive cosmological constant, and hence has as variables pairs of the form
(g,A)
, where
g
is a Lorentzian metric on
M
and
A
is a connection on a principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle over the underlying four-manifold
M
. The supersymmetry conditions of the theory are given in Equation (
15
). The first supersymmetry condition requires the existence of a chiral complex spinor
\eta
parallel with respect to a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(3,1)
-connection constructed from
g
and
A
. The second equation requires that the action of the curvature of
A
on
\eta
via Clifford multiplication is proportional to the identity, and therefore is a Lorentzian analog of an instanton condition for
A
. The main result of this section is given in Theorem
7.19
, which we summarize here as follows:

Theorem 1.6
.

Every standard stationary quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity is isometric to a Lorentzian four-manifold
(M,g)
of the form:

M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{2}\,,\qquad g=-(R^{-2}\psi^{2}+\mathfrak{c})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot(\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi)+h\,,

where
(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})
are the Cartesian coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
h
is the round metric on
S^{2}
of radius
R
,
\mathfrak{c}
is a real constant, and
\psi
is a first eigenfunction of the Laplacian on
(S^{2},h)
, that is,
\Delta_{h}\psi=2R^{-2}\psi
. Furthermore, every such standard stationary quasi-supersymmetric solution is geodesically complete and globally hyperbolic.

The notion of
quasi-supersymmetric
solution of a supergravity theory is a novel addition of this article, and arises as a natural consequence of having two distinct squares of a given irreducible complex spinor, namely the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares. Roughly speaking, a solution to a supergravity theory is said to be
quasi-supersymmetric
if there exists an irreducible complex spinor whose Hermitian square satisfies the differential system induced by the supersymmetry conditions of the theory via Theorem
1.1
or Theorem
1.2
, depending on the dimension of the underlying manifold. This provides a first-order integrability condition for supergravity solutions that is a priori weaker than supersymmetry and may therefore be useful for exploring supergravity solutions beyond supersymmetry. It would be very interesting to compare the notion of quasi-supersymmetry proposed in this article with the supersymmetry-breaking deformation introduced in
[
37
]
for pure spinors in type II supergravity.

In Section
8
, we apply the theory of spinorial forms to irreducible chiral complex spinors parallel under a metric connection with totally skew-symmetric torsion, to which we refer as skew-torsion parallel spinors. Our main result in this section is Theorem
8.10
, which proves that a spin Lorentzian six-manifold admits a skew-torsion parallel spinor with an integrable screen bundle only if it admits a foliation with locally conformally Kähler leaves. The notion of skew-torsion parallel spinor on a six-dimensional Lorentzian manifold is fundamental in ten-dimensional and six-dimensional supergravity and is one of the main motivations for their study.

In Section
9
we apply the theory of spinorial forms to minimal six-dimensional supergravity and its quasi-supersymmetric solutions. This supergravity theory can be formulated on a bundle gerbe with a connective structure, as we do in this article, or on an exact Courant algebroid, as proposed in
[
20
,
21
]
. From the bundle gerbe perspective, the bosonic sector of the theory has as variables a Lorentzian metric and a curving. On the other hand, the supersymmetry condition of the theory requires the existence of a skew-torsion parallel spinor with respect to a metric connection with totally skew-symmetric torsion given by the curvature of the given curving. The general theory of its supersymmetric solutions was pioneered in
[
25
]
, see also
[
9
]
, where the local isomorphism type of these solutions was determined in terms of a minimal set of differential equations in an adapted coordinate system. In particular, it was apparent that the class of supersymmetric solutions of six-dimensional minimal supergravity is vast and rich. In this article, we focus on the reduction of the general bosonic sector of the theory to the wave-front of a standard Kundt space-time, which in particular implies reducing the underlying bundle gerbe and its curvings. Our main result in this section is the complete decoupled reduced system, which we present as a purely four-dimensional differential system in Equation (
48
) for a Riemannian metric, a curving, and a function, whose solutions yield a large class of non-twisting solutions of six-dimensional minimal supergravity of infinite topological types. This reduced system, although different, belongs to the class of generalized Ricci soliton systems
[
21
]
in the Einstein frame
[
6
, Lemma 2.24]
, suggesting appropriate techniques for studying its solutions
[
54
,
52
,
53
]
. In Corollary
9.27
, we apply the results of Section
8
to show that these solutions are generically not quasi-supersymmetric, and hence non-supersymmetric. It would be very interesting to understand the relation between the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares used in this article, with the results of
[
13
]
, which also involves the Hermitian square of the supersymmetry parameter.

Finally, we recall that in our conventions, the Laplacian on functions on a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is defined as follows:

\Delta_{g}f:=\nabla^{g*}\mathrm{d}f:=-\mathrm{Tr}_{g}(\nabla^{g}\mathrm{d}f)\,,\qquad f\in C^{\infty}(M).

In Riemannian signature, this corresponds to the
positive
Laplacian.

.

v^{2}=h(v,v)

2.
Algebraic complex spinorial forms

In this section we recall the algebraic characterization of the
squares
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
for an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
associated to a real quadratic vector space
(V,h)
of dimension
d
and signature
(p,q)
, where
p
is the number of
pluses
and
q
is the number of
minuses
. We closely follow the exposition of
[
23
]
, to which we refer for more details. Throughout the paper we will use
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
.

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space. A
Hermitian pairing

\Scr H
on
\Sigma
is a non-degenerate sesquilinear pairing
\Scr H\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
which is anti-linear in the second entry and satisfies
\Scr H(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\overline{\Scr H(\eta_{2},\eta_{1})}
for all
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. We do not assume that
\Scr H
is necessarily positive-definite. The
Hermitian square maps
of such a
(\Sigma,\Scr H)
are the quadratic maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
defined by:

\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}(\eta):=\kappa\,\eta\otimes\Scr H(-,\eta),\quad\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1).

Let
\Scr B\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
be a
complex-bilinear pairing
. We assume
\Scr B
to be either symmetric or skew-symmetric and we say that
\Scr B
has
symmetry type

\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if
\Scr B(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\sigma\Scr B(\eta_{2},\eta_{1})
. The
complex-bilinear square map
of such a
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
is the quadratic map
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
defined by:

\mathcal{E}(\eta):=\eta\otimes\Scr B(-,\eta).

Note that in contrast with the Hermitian case, any multiplicative factor included in the definition of
\mathcal{E}
can be reabsorbed by a complex homothety of
\eta
, and therefore is irrelevant for our purposes.

2.1.
Spinorial forms in even dimensions

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic real vector space of even dimension
d
. We will use the
Kähler-Atiyah model

(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
for the universal Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
of the quadratic vector space
(V^{*},h^{*})
dual to
(V,h)
. We briefly recall this model in Appendix
A
. Let
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
be an irreducible complex representation of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
on a complex vector space
\Sigma
, whence
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma=2^{\smash{\frac{d}{2}}}
. Consider the complexification
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}):=\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
of the real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Since
d
is even, the map:

\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras. Composing
\gamma
with the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism:

\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})

gives an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras which we denote by:

(1)

\Psi_{\gamma}:=\gamma\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to(\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),\circ).

To define the spinor square maps we combine the isomorphism
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
together with the squaring maps
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
introduced before as quadratic maps associated to a choice of complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
and a choice of Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
on
\Sigma
, respectively. For our purposes, we cannot use arbitrary non-degenerate complex-bilinear or Hermitian pairings on
\Sigma
. Instead, it is convenient to work with non-degenerate pairings on
\Sigma
that are adapted to its structure as a complex Clifford module. This leads to the notion of
admissible pairing
, introduced in
[
2
,
1
]
. In our case, we must distinguish between Hermitian and complex-bilinear admissible pairings.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. An
admissible Hermitian pairing
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a non-degenerate Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
on
\Sigma
satisfying:

\Scr H(\gamma(z)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr H(\eta_{1},\gamma(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})\eta_{2}),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. Similarly, an
admissible complex-bilinear pairing
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
on
\Sigma
satisfying:

\Scr B(\gamma(z)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\eta_{1},\gamma((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z))\eta_{2}),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

again for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. The sign factor
s
is called the
adjoint type
of the admissible pairing. We say that an admissible pairing is of
positive adjoint type
if
s=+1
and of
negative adjoint type
if
s=-1
.

Proof of the existence of admissible Hermitian and complex-bilinear pairings can be found in
[
2
,
1
,
26
,
41
]
, and is given explicitly in
[
23
, Prop. 3.9]
and
[
23
, Prop. 3.18]
, respectively. We will always assume that, given an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
, the square maps
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
are constructed using an admissible pairing. We will refer to such triples
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr H)
and
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr B)
as
Hermitian
and
complex-bilinear paired Clifford modules
. We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}/\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\gamma}}

Consequently, we define the
complex-bilinear
and
Hermitian square spinor maps
respectively as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

We define the
squares
of an irreducible complex spinor in even dimensions in terms of these square spinor maps.

Definition 2.2
.

The
complex-bilinear square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in even dimensions is the complex exterior form
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The
Hermitian square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in even dimensions is the complex exterior form
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\eta)\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
for
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

An element in the image of
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}
determines a complex spinor uniquely modulo a multiplicative unitary complex number. On the other hand, an element in the image of
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}
determines a complex spinor uniquely modulo a sign. Hence, the complex-bilinear square of a complex spinor contains
more
information than its Hermitian square and is equivalent to the spinor itself modulo a sign. This fact is key to developing the theory of
complex spinorial forms
and its applications to the study of spinors parallel under a general connection on the spinor bundle.

We introduce the following terminology, which we borrow from
[
41
]
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. The
dequantization

\mathfrak{q}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an endomorphism
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is
\mathfrak{q}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(Q)
.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
23
, Lemma 3.8]
)
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Then, the spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
satisfies
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\diamond\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\diamond\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)=0
.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
\alpha^{t}:=(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)
be the
\Scr B
-transpose of
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
23
, Lemma 3.21]
. If
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
, then the following equations are equivalent:

\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0,\qquad\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{q})=0.

Similarly, let
\alpha^{\dagger}:=(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\alpha})
be the
\Scr H
-transpose of
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
23
, Lemma 3.12]
. If
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, then the following equations are equivalent:

\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0,\qquad\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})=0.

For further reference, we introduce the standard spin-c group as follows:

\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q):=\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)\mathrm{U}(1):=(\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1))/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(d)

and we denote its identity component by
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
. Hence, we view elements in the group
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)
as equivalence classes
[u,z]
with
u\in\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)
and
z\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. There is a natural homomorphism
\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
defined by:

\mathrm{Ad}^{c}_{[u,z]}:=(\mathrm{Ad}_{u},z^{2})\,,\qquad[u,z]\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q),

where
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}(p,q)
is the standard double cover of
\mathrm{SO}(p,q)
defined via the adjoint representation of the spin group. Then, the map
\mathrm{Ad}^{c}
is a double cover of
\mathrm{SO}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
.

We now give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to a Hermitian paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr H)
. Recall that we denote by
\alpha^{(0)}\in\mathbb{C}
the degree zero component of the exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and that:

\pi(\alpha)=(-1)^{k}\alpha,\qquad\tau(\alpha)=(-1)^{\binom{k}{2}}\alpha

for every
k
-form
\alpha\in\wedge^{k}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
23
, Thm. 3.13 and Cor. 3.23]
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following equations:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}

for an exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following equations hold:

\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

If in addition the spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
is chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then we have to add the condition:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
(V^{*},h^{*})
and define
\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i_{1}})\circ\cdots\circ\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i_{k}})
. Then we can give an explicit form of the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of an explicit orthonormal basis of
(V^{*},h^{*})
, see
[
23
, Eq. 10]
:

\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Scr H(\eta,\eta)+\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr H((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}.

Moreover, in
[
23
, Section 3.3]
it is shown that the quadratic maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
are equivariant with respect to the natural action of
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
on
\Sigma
and
\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
defined via
\gamma
and the adjoint representation
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
, respectively.

We now give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated with a complex-bilinear paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr B)
.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
23
, Thm. 3.22 and Cor. 3.24]
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following equations:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha

for an exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following equations hold:

\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

If in addition the spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
is chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then we have to add the condition:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

As in the Hermitian case, for the complex-bilinear square
\alpha_{\eta}
of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, we have the following form in terms of an orthonormal basis (see
[
23
, Eq. 13]
):

\alpha_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Scr B(\eta,\eta)+\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr B((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}.

Let
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
be the standard double cover of
\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
and define the following map:

\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1),\qquad[u,z]\mapsto\mathrm{Ad}^{c}_{[u,z]}:=(\mathrm{Ad}_{u},z^{2}).

Then, by
[
23
, Section 3.5]
, the quadratic map
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is equivariant with respect to the natural action of
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
on
\Sigma
and
\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
defined via
\gamma
and the double cover representation
\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
, respectively.

2.2.
Spinorial forms in odd dimensions

Let
V
be an oriented real vector space of odd dimension
d
equipped with a non-degenerate metric of signature
(p,q)
. Let
(V^{*},h^{*})
be the quadratic vector space dual to
(V,h)
and denote by
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
the Clifford algebra of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. Consider the complexification
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}):=\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. Denote by
\nu
the pseudo-Riemannian volume form of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
and define the
complex volume form
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
by:

\nu_{\mathbb{C}}:=i^{q+\frac{d-1}{2}}\nu\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}).

It can be easily seen that the complex volume form satisfies
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}=1
and lies in the center of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Therefore,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
is non-simple and splits as a direct sum of unital and associative algebras:

(2)

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{+}(V^{*},h^{*})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{-}(V^{*},h^{*}),

where:

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\mid\nu_{\mathbb{C}}z=\ell z\}=\frac{1}{2}(1+\ell\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}),\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

The algebra
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})
is simple and isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2^{\frac{d-1}{2}},\mathbb{C})
. Therefore,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
admits two irreducible left Clifford modules of complex dimension
2^{\smash{\frac{d-1}{2}}}
:

\gamma_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

that correspond to the projection of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
to the factor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})
composed with an isomorphism of the later to the algebra
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
of endomorphisms of a complex vector space
\Sigma
of dimension
2^{\smash{\frac{d-1}{2}}}
. These two Clifford modules are distinguished by the value they take at the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
, namely:

\gamma_{\ell}(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})=\ell\,\mathrm{Id}\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma).

We transport the splitting (
2
) of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
to the Kähler-Atiyah algebra
(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
through the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism
\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
. This gives:

(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)=(\wedge_{+}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\oplus(\wedge_{-}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond),

where:

\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\diamond\alpha=\ell\alpha\}=\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid i^{q+\frac{d-1}{2}}*\tau(\alpha)=\ell\alpha\},\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Here we have used the identity:

(3)

\alpha\diamond\nu_{\mathbb{C}}=\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\diamond\alpha=i^{q+\frac{d-1}{2}}*\tau(\alpha)

given in Proposition
A.2
. By composing the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism with the irreducible complex representation
\gamma_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
we obtain a surjective morphism of unital and associative complex algebras that we denote by:

\Psi_{\ell}:=\gamma_{\ell}\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma).

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the natural projection given explicitly by:

(4)

\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\alpha):=\frac{1}{2}(1+\ell\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\diamond\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha+i^{q+\frac{d-1}{2}}\ell*\tau(\alpha)),

where we have used the identity (
3
). Consider the canonical linear inclusion
\iota_{\ell}\colon\wedge_{\ell}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\hookrightarrow\wedge V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, which is a right inverse to
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
. In particular:

\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}\colon(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras. Following
[
31
,
33
,
32
]
, we define:

\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\wedge^{k}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Note that
\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\wedge^{<}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\oplus*\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, restricting
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
to
\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain an isomorphism of vector spaces that we use to transport the algebra product in
(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
to
\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For every pair
\alpha,\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
we define the
truncated geometric product
by:

\alpha\vee\beta:=2\mathcal{P}_{<}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\alpha\diamond\beta)),

where
\mathcal{P}_{<}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the natural projection. By construction,
(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
and the
truncated Kähler-Atiyah algebra

(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)
are naturally isomorphic as unital and associative complex algebras. For further reference we introduce the following linear map:

(5)

\Psi^{<}_{\ell}:=\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|_{\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}}\colon(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),

which, by the previous discussion, is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras. Altogether, we obtain the following commutative diagram of unital and associative algebras:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{<}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|_{\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathcal{P}_{<}}

Recall that, similarly to the even-dimensional case, a Hermitian pairing
\Scr H\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
is called admissible if it satisfies:

\Scr H(\gamma_{\ell}(z)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr H(\eta_{1},\gamma_{\ell}(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})\eta_{2}),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2},

whereas a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
is called admissible if it satisfies:

\Scr B(\gamma_{\ell}(z)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\eta_{1},\gamma_{\ell}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z))\eta_{2}),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. Proof of the existence of admissible Hermitian and complex-bilinear pairings can be found in
[
2
,
1
,
26
,
41
]
, and is given explicitly in
[
23
, Prop. 4.3]
and
[
23
, Props. 4.8 and 4.11]
, respectively. We will always assume that, given an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
, the square maps
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
are constructed using an admissible pairing.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
and a Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
. Using the isomorphism
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\colon(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
given in (
5
), we define the
complex-bilinear
and
Hermitian square spinor maps
respectively as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\ell}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}\circ\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

As in the even-dimensional case, we have the following characterization of
constrained
spinors.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
23
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Then, the spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
satisfies
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\vee\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\vee\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}(\eta)=0
, where
\mathfrak{q}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}(Q)
is the
dequantization
of
Q
.

We now give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to a Hermitian paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr H)
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
23
, Thm. 4.4]
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\ell}^{\kappa}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following equations:

\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}

for an exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following equations hold:

\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

As it happened in the even-dimensional case, we can explicitly expand the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of an orthonormal basis
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
:

\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\Scr H(\eta,\eta)+\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr H((\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}},

where
\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}})\circ\cdots\circ\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(e^{i_{k}})
. Furthermore, the Hermitian spinor square maps in odd dimensions are also equivariant with respect to the natural action of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
.

We now give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to a complex-bilinear paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr B)
.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
23
, Thm. 4.12]
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following equations:

\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha

for an exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following equations hold:

\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Analogously to the Hermitian case, the complex-bilinear square
\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
can be expanded as follows:

\alpha_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\Scr B(\eta,\eta)+\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr B((\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}

in terms of any orthonormal basis
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. Furthermore, the complex-bilinear spinor square map in odd dimensions is also equivariant with respect to the natural action of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
.

3.
Constrained parallel complex spinors

In this section, we recall the spinorial background that we will use throughout the article to then introduce the notion of
constrained parallel spinor
, which is our main object of study.

3.1.
Bundles of irreducible complex Clifford modules

Let
(M,g)
denote a connected and
oriented
pseudo-Riemannian manifold of signature
(p,q)
and dimension
d=p+q
. The bundle of Clifford algebras of
(M,g)
will be denoted by
\mathrm{Cl}(M,g)
, whereas its complexification will be denoted by
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)
. Let
\pi
and
\tau
be the canonical automorphism and anti-automorphism of the Clifford bundle, given by fiberwise extension of the corresponding objects defined fiberwise.

Definition 3.1
.

A
bundle of complex Clifford modules
, or a
complex spinor bundle
for short, on
(M,g)
is a pair
(S,\gamma)
, where
S
is a complex vector bundle on
M
and
\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)\to\mathrm{End}(S)
is a morphism of bundles of unital and associative complex algebras, where
\mathrm{End}(S)
denotes the bundle of endomorphisms of
S
.

Given a complex spinor bundle
(S,\gamma)
, we obtain a natural morphism of unital and associative
real
algebras:

\gamma\colon\mathrm{Cl}(M,g)\to\mathrm{End}(S)

obtained by restricting
\gamma
to
\mathrm{Cl}(M,g)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)=\mathrm{Cl}(M,g)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. Since
M
is connected, any bundle of complex Clifford modules
(S,\gamma)
is modeled on a given Clifford module called its
type
, which is well-defined modulo
unbased
isomorphisms of Clifford modules, see
[
34
,
35
]
for more details. If this type is irreducible, then we say that
(S,\gamma)
is a bundle of irreducible complex spinors or an irreducible complex spinor bundle for short. In this case, the rank of a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules is
{\rm rk}_{\mathbb{C}}S=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma=2^{\smash{[\frac{d}{2}]}}
, where
[\frac{d}{2}]
denotes the integer part of
\frac{d}{2}
and
d
is the dimension of
M
. In this situation, it is shown in
[
34
,
35
,
36
]
that an orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
admits a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules if and only if it admits a spin-c structure
Q\to(M,g)
, in which case
S
may be obtained from
Q
via the associated vector bundle construction through an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)
. In the following, given an irreducible complex spinor bundle
(S,\gamma)
, we will always assume that
S
is given as an associated vector bundle to a given spin structure
Q
, that is, we fix a presentation of the form:

S=Q\times_{\varrho}\Sigma,

where
\varrho\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{GL}(\Sigma)
is an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)
on the complex vector space
\Sigma
. In particular, we have a canonically defined principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle:

P=Q\times_{\xi}\mathrm{U}(1)

associated to
Q
via the
square
morphism
\xi\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{U}(1)
defined by
\xi([u,z])=z^{2}
. Equivalently, we can consider
P
as a Hermitian complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to M
. We will respectively refer to
P
and
\mathcal{L}
as the
characteristic

\mathrm{U}(1)
and complex line bundles associated to
S
.

To proceed further, we need to distinguish between even and odd dimensions.

3.1.1.
The even-dimensional case

We denote by
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
the complex exterior bundle:

\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{d}\wedge^{k}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M

equipped with the pointwise extension of the geometric product
\diamond
, which depends on the metric
g
. This bundle of unital and associative algebras is called the
(complexified) Kähler-Atiyah bundle
of
(M,g)
(see
[
31
,
32
]
). The Chevalley-Riesz map extends to a unital isomorphism of bundles of algebras:

\Psi\colon(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g),

which allows us to view the Kähler-Atiyah bundle as a model for the Clifford bundle. We again denote by
\pi
and
\tau
the (anti-)automorphisms of the Kähler-Atiyah bundle obtained by transporting the corresponding objects from the Clifford bundle through
\Psi
.

The following is a well-known property of the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
, which also follows from the definition of the geometric product
\diamond
(see
[
31
,
32
]
).

Proposition 3.2
.

The canonical extension to
\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
of
(M,g)
, which we again denote by
\nabla^{g}
, acts by derivations of the geometric product, that is:

\nabla^{g}(\alpha\diamond\beta)=(\nabla^{g}\alpha)\diamond\beta+\alpha\diamond(\nabla^{g}\beta)

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M):=\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
. It is well-known that:

\nabla^{g}_{X}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)\wedge\beta+\alpha\wedge(\nabla^{g}_{X}\beta),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}(\iota_{Y}\alpha)=\iota_{\nabla^{g}_{X}Y}\alpha+\iota_{Y}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha).

Using the above formulas and the definition of the geometric product, see (
54
), it follows that
\nabla^{g}(\theta\diamond\alpha)=(\nabla^{g}\theta)\diamond\alpha+\theta\diamond(\nabla^{g}\alpha)
for all
\theta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. The general statement follows by linear and associative extension.
∎

The map
\Psi_{\gamma}
defined in (
1
) extends to a unital isomorphism of bundles of algebras which we denote by the same symbol:

\Psi_{\gamma}:=\gamma\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)\to(\mathrm{End}(S),\circ).

This map allows us to identify bundles
(S,\gamma)
of modules over
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)
with bundles of modules
(S,\Psi_{\gamma})
over the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
. For ease of notation, we denote by
dot
the Clifford multiplication of
(S,\Psi_{\gamma})
, whose action on global sections is:

\alpha\cdot\eta:=\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)\eta

for all
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
and let
W
be any vector bundle on
M
. The
symbol
of a section
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
is the section
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
defined through:

\mathfrak{q}:=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{W})^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}),

where
\mathrm{Id}_{W}
is the identity endomorphism of
W
.

Remark 3.4
.

In particular, the symbol of an endomorphism
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
is an exterior form
\mathfrak{q}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
, while the symbol of an
\mathrm{End}(S)
-valued one-form
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
is an element
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
, that is, a one-form taking values on the complex exterior algebra of
M
.

3.1.2.
The odd-dimensional case

Here we consider the
(complexified) truncated Kähler-Atiyah bundle

(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\vee)
of
(M,g)
, where:

\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\wedge^{k}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M

and for
\alpha,\beta\in\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
the
truncated geometric product
is given by:

\alpha\vee\beta:=\mathcal{P}_{<}(\alpha\diamond\beta+i^{q+\frac{d-1}{2}}\ell*\tau(\alpha\diamond\beta)),

where
\mathcal{P}_{<}\colon\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\to\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
is the natural projection and we have used (
4
). We have the following result analogous to Proposition
3.2
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The canonical extension to
\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
of
(M,g)
, which we again denote by
\nabla^{g}
, acts by derivations of the truncated geometric product, that is:

\nabla^{g}(\alpha\vee\beta)=(\nabla^{g}\alpha)\vee\beta+\alpha\vee(\nabla^{g}\beta)

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M):=\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
.

Since the dimension
d
of
M
is odd, a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules on
(M,g)
has two non-equivalent irreducible types distinguished by the value they take at the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}
. We denote by
(S,\gamma_{\ell})
the corresponding bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules, where
\gamma_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)\to\mathrm{End}(S)
satisfies
\gamma_{\ell}(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})=\ell\,\mathrm{Id}_{S}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

The map
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}
defined in (
5
) extends to a unital isomorphism of bundles of algebras which we denote by the same symbol:

\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\colon(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\vee)\to(\mathrm{End}(S),\circ).

This map allows us to identify bundles
(S,\gamma_{\ell})
of modules over
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)
with bundles of modules
(S,\Psi^{<}_{\ell})
over the truncated Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\vee)
. For ease of notation, we denote by
dot
the Clifford multiplication of
(S,\Psi^{<}_{\ell})
, whose action on global sections is:

\alpha\cdot\eta:=\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(\alpha)\eta

for all
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
.

3.2.
Hermitian spinor bundles

In order to define the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, we need to consider the appropriate notion of Hermitian pairing on the bundle
S
. In order to simultaneously deal with the even and odd cases, whenever necessary in the following
\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}
will denote
\Psi_{\gamma}
if
d
is even and
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}
if
d
is odd, and similarly for
\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
and
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. Then
(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
denotes the Kähler-Atiyah model if
d
is even, and the truncated Kähler-Atiyah model
(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\vee)
if
d
is odd.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. A fiberwise Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
on
S
is called
admissible
if
\Scr H_{p}\colon S_{p}\times S_{p}\to\mathbb{C}
is an admissible Hermitian pairing on the complex Clifford module
(S_{p},\gamma_{p})
for all
p\in M
. A
Hermitian spinor bundle
on
(M,g)
is a tuple
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
, where
(S,\gamma)
is a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
and
\Scr H
is an admissible Hermitian pairing on
S
.

Since
M
is connected, the adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
of the admissible Hermitian pairings
\Scr H_{p}
, which are non-degenerate by definition, is constant on
M
. Then
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is called the
adjoint type
of
\Scr H
or
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
. Since
M
is paracompact, the defining algebraic properties of an admissible Hermitian pairing can be formulated equivalently as follows using global sections when viewing
(S,\gamma)
as a bundle
(S,\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma})
of modules over the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
of
(M,g)
:

•

\Scr H(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\overline{\Scr H(\eta_{2},\eta_{1})}
for all
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
.

•

\Scr H(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr H(\eta_{1},\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\alpha}))\eta_{2})
for all
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
.

To guarantee the existence of an admissible Hermitian pairing on an irreducible complex spinor bundle, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(M,g)
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold. We say that
(M,g)
is:

•

Strongly orientable
if it admits a
strong orientation
, that is, if the orthonormal frame bundle of
(M,g)
reduces to the identity component
\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
of
\mathrm{SO}(p,q)
.

•

Strongly spin
if it admits a
strong spin structure
, that is, a spin structure that admits a reduction to the identity component
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
of the spin group
\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)
.

•

Strongly spin-c
if it admits a
strong spin-c structure
, that is, a spin-c structure that admits a reduction to the identity component
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
of the spin-c group
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)
.

Remark 3.8
.

When
pq=0
, the special orthogonal and spin groups are connected. In this case, orientation and strong orientation are equivalent, as are the properties of being spin and strongly spin. When
pq\neq 0
, the groups
\mathrm{SO}(p,q)
and
\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)
have two connected components. In this case,
(M,g)
is strongly orientable if and only if it is orientable and in addition the principal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-bundle associated to its bundle of oriented orthonormal frames
F(M,g)
through the group morphism
\mathrm{SO}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}(p,q)/\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
is trivial, while a spin structure
Q
reduces to a strong spin structure if and only if the principal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-bundle associated to
Q
through the group morphism
\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)\to\mathrm{Spin}(p,q)/\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
is trivial. When
(M,g)
is strongly spin, the short exact sequence:

1\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\to 1

induces a sequence in C̆ech cohomology which implies that the set of strong spin structures on
(M,g)
is a
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-torsor. A particularly simple case arises when
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
, e.g. when
M
is simply connected. In this situation,
M
is strongly orientable and any spin structure on
(M,g)
reduces to a strong spin structure
1
1

1

This is because the set of isomorphism classes of principal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-bundles over
M
is
[M,B\mathbb{Z}_{2}]=[M,K(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)]\cong H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
. Up to isomorphism, in this special case, there exists at most one spin structure, one strong spin structure, and one complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
.

Remark 3.9
.

When
pq=0
, the spin-c group is connected, so in this case, the properties of being spin-c and strongly spin-c are equivalent. When
pq\neq 0
, the group
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(p,q)
has two connected components. In this case,
(M,g)
is strongly spin-c if and only if it is spin-c and strongly orientable. When
(M,g)
is strongly spin-c, the short exact sequence:

1\to\mathrm{U}(1)\to\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\to 1

induces a sequence in C̆ech cohomology which implies that the set of strong spin-c structures on
(M,g)
is a
H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
-torsor. A particularly simple case arises when
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
and
H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})=0
. In this situation,
M
is strongly orientable and any spin-c structure on
(M,g)
reduces to a strong spin-c structure. Up to isomorphism, in this special case, there exists at most one spin-c structure, one strong spin-c structure, and one complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
.

An irreducible complex spinor bundle
(S,\gamma)
associated to a strong spin-c structure admits an admissible Hermitian pairing, since the latter is by construction preserved by the identity component
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
of the spin-c group. On the other hand, every spin-c structure on a strongly orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is strongly spin-c, namely, reduces to
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
. Hence, strong orientability of
(M,g)
suffices to guarantee that every irreducible complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
admits an admissible Hermitian pairing.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly oriented pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
(S,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex spinor bundle over
(M,g)
. Then,
(S,\gamma)
admits an admissible Hermitian pairing.

Since
S
is associated to a strong spin-c structure, the spinorial lift of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
of
(M,g)
to
S
and the choice of a connection
A\in\Omega^{1}(P,i\mathbb{R})
on
P
define a covariant derivative
\nabla^{g,A}
on
S
. The connection
\nabla^{g,A}
acts on
\Gamma(S)
by module derivations:

(6)

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\alpha\cdot\eta)=(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)\cdot\eta+\alpha\cdot(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
,
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
, and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
; and is compatible with
\Scr H
:

X(\Scr H(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))=\Scr H(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta_{1},\eta_{2})+\Scr H(\eta_{1},\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta_{2})

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
(see
[
18
, Section 3.1]
).

The connection
\nabla^{g,A}
induces a linear connection, which we denote by the same symbol for ease of notation, on the bundle of endomorphisms
\mathrm{End}(S)
. Given
X\in\Gamma(TM)
, by definition we have:

(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}T)\eta:=\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(T\eta)-T(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

for all
T\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
. Moreover, the connection
\nabla^{g,A}
acts on
\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
by derivations:

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(T_{1}\circ T_{2})=(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}T_{1})\circ T_{2}+T_{1}\circ(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}T_{2})

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and
T_{1},T_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be a Hermitian spinor bundle. Then the map
\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}
induces a unital isomorphism of complex algebras
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M),\diamond)\cong\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
which is compatible with
\nabla^{g}
and
\nabla^{g,A}
. In other words, the following equation holds:

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha))=\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

Proof.

Recall that
\alpha\cdot\eta:=\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\eta
. Using (
6
) we obtain:

\displaystyle\big(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha))\big)\eta

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\eta)-\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)\eta+\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)-\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)\eta

for all
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
.
∎

3.3.
Complex-bilinear paired spinor bundles

Whereas admissible Hermitian pairings on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{0})
are
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
-invariant, admissible complex-bilinear pairings
\Scr B
are not. Instead, as a consequence of
\Scr B
being
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
-invariant, it satisfies the following equation:

\Scr B(\gamma_{0}([u,z])\eta_{1},\gamma_{0}([u,z])\eta_{2})=\Scr B(z\gamma_{0}(u)\eta_{1},z\gamma_{0}(u)\eta_{2})=z^{2}\Scr B(\gamma_{0}(u)\eta_{1},\gamma_{0}(u)\eta_{2})=z^{2}\Scr B(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

for all
u\in\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
,
z\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. Therefore, a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
on
(S,\gamma)
takes values on the complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}
naturally associated to
S
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
with associated characteristic line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. An
admissible complex-bilinear pairing

\Scr B
on
(S,\gamma)
is a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
taking values on
\mathcal{L}
and such that
\Scr B_{m}\colon S_{m}\times S_{m}\to\mathcal{L}_{m}\cong\mathbb{C}
is an admissible complex-bilinear pairing on
(S_{m},\gamma_{m})
for all
m\in M
. A
complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle
on
(M,g)
is a tuple
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
, where
(S,\gamma)
is a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
and
\Scr B
is an admissible complex-bilinear pairing on
S
.

Since
M
is connected, the symmetry and adjoint type
\sigma,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
of the admissible complex-bilinear pairings
\Scr B_{m}
, which are non-degenerate by definition, are constant on
M
. Then
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are called the
symmetry type
and
adjoint type
of
\Scr B
or
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
, respectively. Since
M
is paracompact, the defining algebraic properties of an admissible complex-bilinear pairing can be formulated equivalently as follows using global sections when viewing
(S,\gamma)
as a bundle
(S,\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma})
of modules over the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
of
(M,g)
:

•

\Scr B(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\sigma\Scr B(\eta_{2},\eta_{1})
for all
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
.

•

\Scr B(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\eta_{1},\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha))\eta_{2})
for all
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
.

Similarly to Proposition
3.10
, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly oriented pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
(S,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex spinor bundle over
(M,g)
. Then,
(S,\gamma)
admits an admissible complex-bilinear pairing.

Since
S=Q\times_{\varrho}\Sigma
is associated to a spin-c structure
Q
, the combination of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
of
(M,g)
together with a choice of a connection
A\in\Omega^{1}(P,i\mathbb{R})
on the characteristic
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
P
defines a covariant derivative
\nabla^{g,A}
on
S
. The connection
\nabla^{g,A}
acts on
\Gamma(S)
by module derivations:

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\alpha\cdot\eta)=(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha)\cdot\eta+\alpha\cdot(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
,
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
, and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
. The connection
A\in\Omega^{1}(P,i\mathbb{R})
induces a Hermitian connection
\nabla^{A}
on
\mathcal{L}
. Then the compatibility with
\Scr B
reads as follows:

\nabla^{A}_{X}(\Scr B(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))=\Scr B(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta_{1},\eta_{2})+\Scr B(\eta_{1},\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta_{2})

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
. Therefore,
\Scr B\in\Gamma(S^{*}\otimes S^{*}\otimes\mathcal{L})
is parallel with respect to the tensor product connection
\nabla^{g,A}\otimes\nabla^{A}
.

To obtain a result analogous to Proposition
3.11
for complex-bilinear paired spinor bundles, we tensor
\mathrm{End}(S)
and
\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
with the complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}
and extend the corresponding products. That is, if
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathrm{End}(S)
and
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
, then:

(T_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1})\circ(T_{2}\otimes\lambda_{2}):=(T_{1}\circ T_{2})\otimes(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2})\in\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}.

Similarly, if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
and
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
, then:

(\alpha_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1})\diamond(\alpha_{2}\otimes\lambda_{2}):=(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2})\otimes(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2})\in\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}.

We define an action of
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior forms on spinors as follows. Let
\alpha_{0}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
,
\lambda\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
. Then:

(\alpha_{0}\otimes\lambda)\cdot\eta:=(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})(\alpha_{0}\otimes\lambda)\eta=(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{0})\eta)\otimes\lambda=(\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta)\otimes\lambda\in\Gamma(S\otimes\mathcal{L}),

where
\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L}\to\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L}
. The connection
\nabla^{g,A}\otimes\nabla^{A}
acts on
\Gamma(S\otimes\mathcal{L})
by:

(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X})((\alpha_{0}\otimes\lambda)\cdot\eta)=((\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha_{0})\cdot\eta)\otimes\lambda+(\alpha_{0}\cdot(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta))\otimes\lambda+(\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta)\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X}\lambda.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle. Then the map
\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}
induces an isomorphism of
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
-modules
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})\cong\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L})
which intertwines the extended products into
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2}
and is compatible with
\nabla^{g,A}
and
\nabla^{A}
. In other words, the following equation holds:

(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X})((\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})(\alpha))=(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\alpha)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{0}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\lambda\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
. Set
E:=\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(\alpha_{0})\otimes\lambda\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L})
and
\nabla:=\nabla^{g,A}\otimes\nabla^{A}
. Then:

\displaystyle(\nabla_{X}E)\eta

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}(E(\eta))-E(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}((\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta)\otimes\lambda)-(\alpha_{0}\cdot(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta))\otimes\lambda

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}(\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta))\otimes\lambda+(\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta)\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X}\lambda-(\alpha_{0}\cdot(\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta))\otimes\lambda

\displaystyle=((\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha_{0})\cdot\eta)\otimes\lambda+(\alpha_{0}\cdot\eta)\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X}\lambda

\displaystyle=(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})(\nabla^{g}_{X}\alpha_{0}\otimes\lambda)\eta+(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})(\alpha_{0}\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X}\lambda)\eta

\displaystyle=(\Psi^{\bullet}_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})((\nabla^{g}_{X}\otimes\nabla^{A}_{X})(\alpha_{0}\otimes\lambda))\eta

for all
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
.
∎

3.4.
Constrained parallel spinors

We proceed to introduce the main character of this article.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a complex spinor bundle on
(M,g)
equipped with a connection
\mathcal{D}
and let
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
with
W
a vector bundle on
M
. A section
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is a
constrained parallel spinor
with respect to
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})
if:

\mathcal{D}\eta=0,\qquad\mathcal{Q}(\eta)=0.

Suppose that
(S,\gamma)
is an irreducible complex spinor bundle. Since it is associated to a spin-c structure, we can write
\mathcal{D}=\nabla^{g,A}-\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
. In this case, the equation satisfied by a constrained parallel spinor can be written as:

\nabla^{g,A}\eta=\mathcal{A}(\eta),\qquad\mathcal{Q}(\eta)=0,

and their solutions are called constrained parallel spinors
relative
to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
. Using connectedness of
M
and the parallel transport of
\mathcal{D}
, the equation
\mathcal{D}\eta=0
implies that the space of constrained parallel spinors relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
is finite-dimensional and that a constrained parallel spinor which is not zero at some point of
M
is automatically nowhere vanishing on
M
.

4.
Parallel Hermitian spinorial forms

To study constrained parallel complex spinors, we will extend the algebraic theory developed in
[
23
]
, whose main results we have recalled in Section
2
, to bundles of irreducible complex Clifford modules equipped with an arbitrary connection
\mathcal{D}
. In this section, we focus on the Hermitian case, while we devote Section
5
to studying the complex-bilinear case. As in the algebraic case, we consider the even-dimensional and odd-dimensional cases separately.

4.1.
Hermitian spinorial forms in even dimensions

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be a Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. The admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
allows us to construct pointwise extensions to
M
of the Hermitian square maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and the Hermitian square spinor maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of Section
2
. We denote these by the same symbol:

\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon S\to\mathrm{End}(S),\qquad\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}\colon S\to\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M.

We have the following diagram of vector bundles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

which extends to maps of sections the algebraic maps, denoted with the same symbol, that we introduced in Section
2
. Note that although
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}
preserves fibers, it is not a morphism of vector bundles since it is fiberwise quadratic.

Definition 4.1
.

The
Hermitian spinor square maps
of the Hermitian spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
are the maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}\colon\Gamma(S)\to\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
,
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, induced by
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}
on sections. The
Hermitian square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is the complex exterior form
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
for
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Elements in the image of
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}
are generically called
Hermitian spinorial forms
.

By the results of Section
2
, we have that
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}
are real quadratic maps and satisfy:

\mathrm{supp}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta))=\mathrm{supp}(\eta)

for all
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
. Define:

\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma}):=\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)}\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma})\subset\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)

as the disjoint union of the images of the Hermitian square maps for all
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle. Then every nowhere vanishing form in
\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma})
determines a cohomology class:

\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})

encoding the obstruction to the existence of a globally defined irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, necessarily nowhere vanishing, such that
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. In particular, such
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
exists if and only if
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
.

Proof.

For every
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, the map
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}\colon S\setminus\{0\}\to\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\setminus\{0\}
induces a base-point preserving smooth map:

\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\gamma}_{\kappa}\colon\mathbb{P}(S)\to\mathbb{P}(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M),\qquad[\eta]\mapsto[\alpha_{\eta}]

between the complex projectivizations
\mathbb{P}(S)
and
\mathbb{P}(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
, of
S
and
\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
, respectively. This map is bijective onto its image. Since
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma})
, it follows that
[\alpha]\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma})
and therefore:

(\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma})^{-1}([\alpha])\colon M\to\mathbb{P}(S)

defines a smooth section of
\mathbb{P}(S)
, which is equivalent to a complex line subbundle
L_{\alpha}\subset S
whose first Chern class determines the cohomology class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha):=c_{1}(L_{\alpha})\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
that we associate to
\alpha
. A section
\eta
of
S
such that
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)=\alpha
would be a nowhere vanishing section of
L_{\alpha}
, which therefore exists if and only if
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
.
∎

We will refer to the cohomology class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
given by Proposition
4.2
as the
spinor class
of the nowhere vanishing exterior form
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma})
. Note that
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
depends only on
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
and
\alpha
, since all admissible Hermitian pairings on
(S,\gamma)
are related to each other by automorphisms of
S
, see
[
23
, Prop. 3.10]
. In particular,
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
is not a characteristic class of
S
since it depends on
\alpha
.

The category of bundles of irreducible complex spinors on
(M,g)
is well-known to be a torsor over the category of complex line bundles on
M
with the action given by the tensor product
[
17
,
34
]
. The action of such a complex line bundle
L
on a given
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
induces naturally an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H_{L}
on
(S_{L}:=S\otimes L,\gamma_{L})
by setting:

(7)

\Scr H_{L}(\eta_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1},\eta_{2}\otimes\lambda_{2}):=h_{L}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\Scr H(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

for every Hermitian inner product
h_{L}
on
L
. With this definition,
(S_{L},\gamma_{L},\Scr H_{L})
is a Hermitian spinor bundle. Using this torsor structure on the category of bundles of irreducible complex spinors, we can conveniently make the spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
vanish.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
(S_{o},\gamma_{o},\Scr H_{o})
be an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle. For every nowhere vanishing form
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\gamma_{o}})
there exists an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
on
(M,g)
, unique modulo isomorphism, such that
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
