.

Proof.

The irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle
(S_{o},\gamma_{o},\Scr H_{o})
yields
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma_{o}}(\alpha)\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
, generally non-vanishing. Consider a complex line bundle
L\to M
whose first Chern class is
c_{1}(L)=\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma_{o}}(\alpha)
. We compute now the spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
for the irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
, where
S=S_{o}\otimes L^{\ast}
,
L^{*}
is the dual line bundle of
L
,
\gamma
is the trivial
L^{*}
-extension of
\gamma_{o}
, and
\Scr H
is defined as in (
7
) for any choice of Hermitian inner product on
L^{*}
. First, we note that
\mathbb{P}(S)=\mathbb{P}(S_{o}\otimes L^{\ast})=\mathbb{P}(S_{o})
, and furthermore
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma_{o}}
induce the same smooth map at the projectivized level, that is:

\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}=\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma_{o}}\colon\mathbb{P}(S)=\mathbb{P}(S_{o})\to\mathbb{P}(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M).

Hence:

(\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma})^{-1}([\alpha])=(\mathbb{P}\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma_{o}})^{-1}([\alpha])\colon M\to\mathbb{P}(S)=\mathbb{P}(S_{o}).

Hence, if this section defines the line bundle
L^{\prime}\subset S_{o}
, necessarily isomorphic to
L
since
c_{1}(L^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma_{o}}(\alpha)
, then it defines the line bundle
L^{\prime}\otimes L^{\ast}\subset S_{o}\otimes L^{\ast}=S
. However, we have
L^{\prime}\otimes L^{\ast}\cong L\otimes L^{\ast}\cong\mathrm{End}(L)
, which is therefore trivial. Hence, Proposition
4.2
implies that there exists a nowhere vanishing section
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
such that
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)=\alpha
, which implies
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
.
∎

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be a Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. Recall that if
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
, then its symbol is defined as
\mathfrak{q}=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{W})^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
. Lemma
2.4
and Remark
2.5
imply:

Lemma 4.4
.

A spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)=0
if and only if one of the following equivalent equations holds:

\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0,\qquad\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})=0,

where
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be an
irreducible
Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
equipped with a connection
\mathcal{D}
. Set
\mathcal{A}:=\nabla^{g,A}-\mathcal{D}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and let:

\mathfrak{a}:=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T^{*}M})^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)

be the symbol of
\mathcal{A}
. We have:

Lemma 4.5
.

A nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\mathcal{D}\eta=0
only if:

\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),

where
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\eta
satisfies
\nabla^{g,A}\eta=\mathcal{A}(\eta)
. Fix
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
and set
E_{\eta}:=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}(\eta)\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
. Then:

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g,A}E_{\eta})\chi

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g,A}(E_{\eta}(\chi))-E_{\eta}(\nabla^{g,A}\chi)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g,A}(\kappa\Scr H(\chi,\eta)\eta)-\kappa\Scr H(\nabla^{g,A}\chi,\eta)\eta

\displaystyle=\kappa\Scr H(\chi,\nabla^{g,A}\eta)\eta+\kappa\Scr H(\chi,\eta)\nabla^{g,A}\eta

\displaystyle=\kappa\Scr H(\chi,\mathcal{A}(\eta))\eta+\kappa\Scr H(\chi,\eta)\mathcal{A}(\eta)

\displaystyle=E_{\eta}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(\chi))+\mathcal{A}(E_{\eta}(\chi)),

for all
\chi\in\Gamma(S)
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}
denotes the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
taken with respect to
\Scr H
. The previous equation implies:

\nabla^{g,A}E_{\eta}=\mathcal{A}\circ E_{\eta}+E_{\eta}\circ\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}.

Applying
\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}
to this equation, and using Proposition
3.11
and
\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{a}^{\dagger}=(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}})
, see Remark
2.5
, gives the result from the statement.
∎

We arrive at the following set of necessary conditions for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
to be the Hermitian square of a constrained parallel spinor relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
be an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
. Then there exists a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
only if:

•

There exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
with vanishing spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
.

•

The complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfies the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
or, equivalently, satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for every complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

If in addition the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then we have to add the equation:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

The complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
as above is determined by
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
through the relation:

\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)

for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Moreover,
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the algebraic equations of the theorem determines a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, unique up to a
\mathrm{U}(1)
-valued function, satisfying the relation
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
.

Proof.

Condition
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
is required by Proposition
4.2
. The algebraic conditions of the theorem follow from the pointwise extension of Theorem
2.6
together with Lemma
4.4
. The necessary differential condition follows from Lemma
4.5
. Note that
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
vanishes at a point
p\in M
if and only if its Hermitian square
\alpha
satisfies
\alpha_{p}=0
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Suppose that
(S,\gamma,\Scr H)
is an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle and
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form for which
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\neq 0
, but satisfies the remaining algebraic and differential conditions of Theorem
4.6
. Then Proposition
4.3
implies that there exists another irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle that admits a nowhere vanishing section whose Hermitian square is precisely
\alpha
.

In practical applications, such as in the study of supersymmetric configurations of supergravity theories, the specific form of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{Q}
as vector-valued endomorphisms of
S
is usually not fundamental. Instead, only their
symbols
are relevant. This is because we are interested in the consequences of such a parallel spinor existing with respect to
some
irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle, and for this, we only need to be concerned with studying constrained parallel spinors relative to pairs
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
that have a given fixed symbol
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
. In this regard, the following form of the previous theorem is the most convenient for our purposes.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
only if there exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

4.2.
Hermitian spinorial forms in odd dimensions

In this section, we present the characterization of Hermitian spinorial forms associated with irreducible Hermitian spinor bundles when
M
is odd-dimensional. The theory is completely analogous to the even-dimensional case considered in the previous subsection, and thus we will omit explicit proofs. The main difference in the odd-dimensional case is the use of the
truncated Kähler-Atiyah model
, as reviewed in Section
2
and explained in detail in
[
23
,
31
,
50
]
. Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr H)
be an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. The admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
allows us to construct pointwise extensions to
M
of the Hermitian square maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and the Hermitian square spinor maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of Section
2
. For simplicity in the exposition, we denote these by the same symbol, that is:

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}\colon S\to\mathrm{End}(S),\qquad\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}\colon S\to\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,

where
\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
is the natural pointwise extension of
\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 4.9
.

The
Hermitian spinor square maps
of the Hermitian spinor bundle
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr H)
are the maps
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}\colon\Gamma(S)\to\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
,
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, induced by
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}
on sections. The
Hermitian square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is the complex exterior form
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}(\eta)\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
for
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Elements in the image of
\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}
are generically called
Hermitian spinorial forms
.

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr H)
be an
irreducible
Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
, which is necessarily associated to a strong spin-c structure. Set
\mathcal{A}:=\nabla^{g,A}-\mathcal{D}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and let:

\mathfrak{a}:=(\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T^{*}M})^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)

be the symbol of
\mathcal{A}
. Recall that if
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
, then its symbol is defined as
\mathfrak{q}:=(\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{W})^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
. Then we have the following result, whose proof is completely analogous to that of Theorem
4.6
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr H)
be an irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle on
(M,g)
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
. Then there exists a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
only if:

•

There exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
with vanishing spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma_{\ell}}(\alpha)
.

•

The complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfies the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
or, equivalently, satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for every complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

The complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
as above is determined by
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
through the relation:

\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}(\eta)

for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Moreover,
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the algebraic equations of the theorem determines a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, unique up to a
\mathrm{U}(1)
-valued function, satisfying the relation
\alpha=\smash{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}(\eta)
.

If we do not worry about studying constrained parallel spinors of a fixed irreducible Hermitian spinor bundle, we obtain the odd-dimensional analog of Corollary
4.8
.

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
only if there exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

5.
Parallel complex-bilinear spinorial forms

In Section
4
, we have translated differential equations on an irreducible complex spinor into differential equations on its Hermitian square. However, these equations give only
necessary
conditions. This is because, as we explained in Section
2
, the Hermitian square of a spinor determines it only up to phase, while the complex-bilinear square determines it up to sign. In this section, we obtain
necessary and sufficient
conditions on a complex-bilinear spinorial form for it to be the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor parallel under a general connection on the spinor bundle. As in the Hermitian case, we will consider separately the cases where the dimension
d
of
M
is even or odd.

5.1.
Complex-bilinear spinorial forms in even dimensions

As in Section
4
, we consider the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
of
(M,g)
as a model for the Clifford algebra
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)
. We will also consider bundles of irreducible complex Clifford modules
(S,\gamma)
on
(M,g)
, which we identify with bundles of modules
(S,\Psi_{\gamma})
over the Kähler-Atiyah bundle
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)
, where:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\gamma}=\gamma\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)\to(\mathrm{End}(S),\circ)

is the composition of the Chevalley-Riesz bundle map
\Psi\colon(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,\diamond)\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)
and the unital morphism of bundles of algebras
\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)\to\mathrm{End}(S)
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. Recall that
\Scr B
is a complex-bilinear pairing taking values in the characteristic line bundle
\mathcal{L}
canonically associated to the spin-c structure
Q
in terms of which we have
S=Q\times_{\varrho}\Sigma
. The admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
allows us to construct global extensions to
M
of the complex-bilinear square map
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and the complex-bilinear square spinor map
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of Section
2
. However, these global maps are decorated with a complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}
, which is absent in the algebraic theory laid out in Section
2
but is crucial to develop the theory globally. In particular, we have:

\mathcal{E}\colon S\to\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}\colon S\to\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L}.

Furthermore, we have the following diagram of vector bundles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L},\diamond)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(M,g)\otimes\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}}

which extends to maps of sections that we denote by the same symbol.

Definition 5.1
.

The
complex-bilinear spinor square map
of the complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
is the map
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}\colon\Gamma(S)\to\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
induced by
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}
on sections. The
complex-bilinear square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is the
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. Elements in the image of
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}
are generically called
complex-bilinear spinorial forms
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. Recall that if
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
, then its symbol is defined as
\mathfrak{q}=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{W})^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
. Lemma
2.4
and Remark
2.5
imply the following.

Lemma 5.2
.

A spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)=0
if and only if one of the following equivalent equations holds:

\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0,\qquad\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{q})=0,

where
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. These are equations as sections of
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,W\otimes\mathcal{L})
.

Since
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
is an irreducible complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
, it is necessarily associated to a strong spin-c structure
[
17
,
34
]
, and thus we can write
\mathcal{A}:=\nabla^{g,A}-\mathcal{D}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
upon choosing a connection
A
on
\mathcal{L}
. Let:

\mathfrak{a}:=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T^{*}M})^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)

be the symbol of
\mathcal{A}
. We have:

Lemma 5.3
.

A nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\mathcal{D}\eta=0
if and only if:

(8)

\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a})\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L}),

where
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
and
\nabla^{g,A}=\nabla^{g}\otimes\nabla^{A}
.

Proof.

Assume that the spinor
\eta
satisfies
\nabla^{g,A}\eta=\mathcal{A}(\eta)
. Set
E_{\eta}:=\mathcal{E}(\eta)=\eta\otimes\Scr B(-,\eta)\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L})
and
\nabla:=\nabla^{g,A}\otimes\nabla^{A}
. Then:

\displaystyle(\nabla E_{\eta})\chi

\displaystyle=\nabla(E_{\eta}(\chi))-E_{\eta}(\nabla^{g,A}\chi)

\displaystyle=\nabla(\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta))-\eta\otimes\Scr B(\nabla^{g,A}\chi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g,A}\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)+\eta\otimes\nabla^{A}(\Scr B(\chi,\eta))-\eta\otimes\Scr B(\nabla^{g,A}\chi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g,A}\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)+\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\nabla^{g,A}\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\eta)\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)+\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\mathcal{A}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\eta)\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)+\eta\otimes\Scr B(\mathcal{A}^{t}(\chi),\eta)

for all
\chi\in\Gamma(S)
, where
\mathcal{A}^{t}
denotes the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
taken with respect to
\Scr B
. The equation above implies:

(9)

(\nabla^{g,A}\otimes\nabla^{A})E_{\eta}=(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})\circ E_{\eta}+E_{\eta}\circ\mathcal{A}^{t}.

Set
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)=(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\circ E_{\eta}
. Taking
(\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}
on Equation (
9
) and using Proposition
3.14
we obtain Equation (
8
).

Conversely, assume that
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfies Equation (
8
). Applying
\Psi_{\gamma}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{L}}
to it gives Equation (
9
), which reads:

\mathcal{D}_{X}\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)+\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\mathcal{D}_{X}\eta)=0

for all
\chi\in\Gamma(S)
and
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. Hence
\mathcal{D}_{X}\eta=\beta(X)\eta
for some
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. Plugging this into the above expression gives us:

\beta(X)\eta\otimes\Scr B(\chi,\eta)=0

for all
\chi\in\Gamma(S)
and
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. This implies
\beta=0
since
\Scr B
is non-degenerate and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is nowhere vanishing. Hence
\mathcal{D}\eta=0
.
∎

To globalize Theorem
2.7
, we need to acknowledge that the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. To deal with this fact, we extend the geometric product
\diamond
trivially along
\mathcal{L}
, so given two forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
we have:

\alpha\diamond\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2}).

Therefore, the algebraic equations of Theorem
2.7
now read as follows:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Note the following:

•

Since
\alpha^{(0)}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{L})=\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, the first equation is as sections of
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2})
.

•

Since
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2})=\Gamma(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2})
, the second equation is as sections of
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 3})
.

We proceed now to associate a cohomology class in
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
to every nowhere vanishing element
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. Given
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
together with a good open cover
\left\{U_{a}\right\}
for some index
a
, choose local sections
\eta_{a}\colon U_{a}\to S
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta_{a})=\alpha|_{U_{a}}
. This defines a family of functions:

s_{ab}\colon U_{a}\cap U_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}

defined by the condition
\eta_{a}=s_{ab}\eta_{b}
for all indices
a
and
b
such that
U_{a}\cap U_{b}\neq\emptyset
. This family of functions can be seen to satisfy, by verifying the consistency of its defining relation on triple overlaps, that
s_{ab}s_{bc}s_{ca}=1
. Hence, the collection of
s_{ab}
defines a C̆ech cocycle with values in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Furthermore, different choices of lifts define cohomologous cocycles, whence we obtain a well-defined class in
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)
and refer to as the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-
spinor class
of
\alpha
. Clearly, we have the following.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
and
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. There exists a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
such that
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
if and only if
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spinor class of a nowhere vanishing form
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{\gamma})
can be made to vanish by tuning the complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle without changing
\mathcal{L}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
(S_{o},\gamma_{o},\Scr B_{o})
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle and let
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{\gamma_{o}})
be nowhere vanishing. Then, there exists a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
and a nowhere vanishing section
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)=\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma_{o}}(\alpha)
be the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spinor class defined by
\alpha
, and fix a spin-c structure
Q_{o}
to which
(S_{o},\gamma_{o},\Scr B_{o})
is associated naturally. Denote by:

[x_{ab},z_{ab}]\colon U_{a}\cap U_{b}\to\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)

a C̆ech cocycle for
Q_{o}
. Then:

(10)

[x_{ab},s_{ab}z_{ab}]\colon U_{a}\cap U_{b}\to\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)

is again a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
-valued C̆ech cocycle, and hence defines a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
structure
Q
which has precisely the same characteristic bundle as
Q_{o}
. Via the associated bundle construction,
Q
defines a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
. If
\eta_{a}
is the family of local sections of
S_{o}
used to define the maps
s_{ab}\colon U_{a}\cap U_{b}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then it becomes a well-defined, nowhere vanishing section of
S
, since multiplying by
s_{ab}
is acting with the structure functions of
S
as determined in Equation (
10
).
∎

We arrive at the final characterization of a constrained parallel spinor relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
in terms of its associated complex-bilinear square.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be an irreducible complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle of symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
(M,g)
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
.

(b)

There exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
with vanishing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which satisfies the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
or, equivalently, satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for every
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
.

If in addition the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then we have to add the equation:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

The
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
as above is determined by
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
through the relation:

\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta).

Moreover,
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the algebraic equations of the theorem determines a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, unique up to sign, satisfying the relation
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
.

Proof.

Condition
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
is required by Lemma
5.4
. The algebraic conditions of the theorem follow from the pointwise extension of Theorem
2.7
together with Lemma
5.2
. The necessary and sufficient differential condition follows from Lemma
5.3
.
∎

As in the Hermitian case considered in Section
4
, we are interested in the necessary and sufficient conditions for a manifold
(M,g)
to admit a constrained parallel spinor, regardless of which spinor bundle it is a section of.

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

If the characteristic bundle
\mathcal{L}
is topologically trivial, then the spin-c structure reduces to a spin structure. Moreover, if the connection
A
on
\mathcal{L}
is trivial, then the connection
\nabla^{g,A}
on
S
corresponds to the spinorial Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
on
S
. In this case, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be an irreducible complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle of symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
.

(b)

There exists a nowhere vanishing complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
with vanishing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which satisfies the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
or, equivalently, satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\diamond\alpha=0

for every complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

If in addition the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then we have to add the equation:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

The complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
as above is determined by
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
through the relation:

\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta).

Moreover,
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying the algebraic equations of the theorem determines a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, unique up to sign, satisfying the relation
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
.

5.2.
Complex-bilinear spinorial forms in odd dimensions

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
. The admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
allows us to construct global extensions to
M
of the complex-bilinear square map
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and the complex-bilinear square spinor map
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of Section
2
. As in the even-dimensional case, these global maps are valued in the complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}
, that is:

\mathcal{E}\colon S\to\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes\mathcal{L},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\colon S\to\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L}.

Definition 5.9
.

The
complex-bilinear spinor square map
of the complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr B)
is the map
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\colon\Gamma(S)\to\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
induced by
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
on sections. The
complex-bilinear square
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is the
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta)\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. Elements in the image of
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
are generically called
complex-bilinear spinorial forms
.

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr B)
be an
irreducible
complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle on
(M,g)
, which, as explained earlier, we consider to be associated to a given spin-c structure
Q
. Hence, after choosing a connection
A
on the characteristic
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
P
of
Q
, we can set
\mathcal{A}:=\nabla^{g,A}-\mathcal{D}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and let:

\mathfrak{a}:=(\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T^{*}M})^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)

be the symbol of
\mathcal{A}
. Recall that if
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
, then its symbol is defined as
\mathfrak{q}:=(\Psi^{<}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{W})^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
. Then we have the following result, whose proof is completely analogous to that of Theorem
5.6
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell},\Scr B)
be an irreducible complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle of symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
(M,g)
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathrm{End}(S))
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S)\otimes W)
for a vector bundle
W
on
M
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
relative to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
.

(b)

There exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
with vanishing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spinor class
\mathfrak{s}_{\gamma}(\alpha)\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which satisfies the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
or, equivalently, satisfies the equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for every
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
.

The
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
as above is determined by
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
through the relation:

\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta).

Moreover,
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the algebraic equations of the theorem determines a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, unique up to sign, satisfying the relation
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta)
.

The analog of Corollary
5.7
is given as follows.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
(M,g)
be a strongly spin-c pseudo-Riemannian manifold and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes W)
be given. Then
(M,g)
admits a nowhere vanishing constrained parallel spinor relative to a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})
whose symbol is
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying the following algebraic and differential equations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\otimes\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}\vee\alpha+\alpha\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\vee\alpha=0

for a
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

6.
Spin-c Killing spinors in low dimensions

In this section, we apply the results obtained in the previous sections to study real and imaginary Killing spinors on spin-c Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
of dimension two, three, and four. These cases will be used to exemplify the applicability of the formalism of complex spinorial forms developed in this paper. We will recover some well-known results of
[
42
,
27
,
24
]
without requiring
(M,g)
to be simply connected or complete.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a spin-c Riemannian manifold. A nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfying:

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta=i\lambda X^{\flat}\cdot\eta

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and some non-zero complex number
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
is called a
Killing spinor
with
Killing number

\lambda
. It turns out that
\lambda
must be either real or imaginary. Hence, such
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is called a
real Killing spinor
if
\lambda=\lambda_{R}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and an
imaginary Killing spinor
if
\lambda=i\lambda_{I}\in i\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Note the factor
i
in the definition of a Killing spinor. In the mathematical literature, and in particular in the articles cited above, Clifford multiplication is defined using the convention opposite to ours. The inclusion of the factor
i
in our definition ensures that our notions and results of real and imaginary spin-c Killing spinors are consistent with those appearing in the literature.

For the following, recall that for one-forms
\theta,\vartheta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
we define:

\theta\wedge\vartheta:=\theta\otimes\vartheta-\vartheta\otimes\theta,\qquad\theta\odot\vartheta:=\theta\otimes\vartheta+\vartheta\otimes\theta

and:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Alt}(\nabla^{g}\theta)(X,Y)

\displaystyle:=(\nabla^{g}\theta)(X,Y)-(\nabla^{g}\theta)(Y,X),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}(\nabla^{g}\theta)(X,Y)

\displaystyle:=(\nabla^{g}\theta)(X,Y)+(\nabla^{g}\theta)(Y,X)

for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
. It is not difficult to show that:

\mathrm{Alt}(\nabla^{g}\theta)=\mathrm{d}\theta,\qquad\mathrm{Sym}(\nabla^{g}\theta)=\mathscr{L}_{\theta^{\sharp}}g.

6.1.
Spin-c Killing spinors in dimension two

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules on a two-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Assume that
(S,\gamma)
is equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of positive adjoint type. Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By the global version of Theorem
2.6
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(M,g)
be a two-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold. A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+if\nu,

where
r\in C^{\infty}(M)
is nowhere vanishing,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
satisfying
r^{2}=f^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
, where
\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. The linear equation
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
is imaginary. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
\theta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
, and
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
. Since
\omega
is a two-form, we can write
\omega=f\nu
for some
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, where
\nu
is the Riemannian volume form. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+if\nu
. We compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=(r+\theta+if\nu)\diamond(r+\theta+if\nu)=r^{2}+2r\theta+2irf\nu+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+f^{2}.

Then the equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
amounts to
r^{2}=f^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
. Since
\Scr H
is a positive-definite admissible Hermitian pairing and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is nowhere vanishing, we have that
r=\frac{1}{2}\Scr H(\eta,\eta)
is nowhere vanishing as well, thus it suffices to take
\beta=1
in the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
to conclude.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

If
\theta=0
, then
r^{2}=f^{2}
and
f=\mu r
for
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In this situation
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\mu r\nu
is the Hermitian square of a
chiral
spinor with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, see
[
23
, Cor. 5.1]
. Note that
\theta
need not be nowhere vanishing; hence there may exist points
p\in M
where
\theta_{p}=0
. At such points
\widehat{\alpha}_{p}=r_{p}+i\mu r_{p}\nu_{p}
, so the spinor
\eta
is pointwise chiral at
p
, even if
\eta
is not chiral on all of
M
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
(M,g)
be a two-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be a Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}
. Then:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}f\nu,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}*\theta,

where
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+if\nu
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Proof.

In the case of a Killing spinor, we have that
\mathcal{A}_{X}=i\lambda\Psi_{\gamma}(X^{\flat})\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
, so its symbol is just
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=i\lambda X^{\flat}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. We compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{X}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i\lambda X^{\flat}\diamond(r+\theta+if\nu)=i\lambda(rX^{\flat}+X^{\flat}\wedge\theta+\theta(X)+if*X^{\flat}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})

\displaystyle=(r+\theta+if\nu)\diamond(-i\bar{\lambda}X^{\flat})=-i\bar{\lambda}(rX^{\flat}+\theta\wedge X^{\flat}+\theta(X)-if*X^{\flat}).

By Corollary
4.8
, if the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta=\mathcal{A}_{X}(\eta)
, then
\nabla^{g}_{X}\widehat{\alpha}=\mathfrak{a}_{X}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}+\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})
. Separating by degrees this equation we obtain:

\nabla^{g}_{X}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta(X),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rX^{\flat}-2\lambda_{R}f*X^{\flat},\qquad(\nabla^{g}_{X}f)\nu=2\lambda_{R}X^{\flat}\wedge\theta

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. From the first equation, we obtain
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta
, and from the second equation, we obtain
\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}f\nu
. Taking the Hodge star operator of the third equation and using:

*(X^{\flat}\wedge\theta)=-*(\theta\wedge X^{\flat})=-\iota_{X}(*\theta)=-(*\theta)(X)

we obtain
\nabla^{g}_{X}f=-2\lambda_{R}(*\theta)(X)
, which in turn implies that
\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}*\theta
.
∎

Using
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta
and
\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}f\nu
we compute the Hessian of the function
r
:

\mathrm{Hess}(r):=\nabla^{g}\mathrm{d}r=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg+4\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}f\nu.

Since the Hessian of a function is a symmetric tensor (the Levi-Civita connection is torsion-free), the term
\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}f\nu\in\Omega^{2}(M)
must vanish. Suppose that
\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}\neq 0
. Then
f=0
and from
\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}*\theta
we obtain
\theta=0
, thus
r=0
, which is not possible. Therefore
\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}=0
, as expected.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
(M,g)
be a two-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be a real Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda_{R}
. Then
(M,g)
has constant positive Gaussian curvature
K^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.5
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=0,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{R}f\nu,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}*\theta.

Let
\omega:=f\nu
. Then
\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=2\lambda_{R}X^{\flat}\wedge\theta
and
\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta=-2\lambda_{R}\iota_{X}\omega
. A computation gives us:

(11)

R^{g}(X,Y)\theta=2\lambda_{R}(\iota_{X}(\nabla^{g}_{Y}\omega)-\iota_{Y}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega))=4\lambda_{R}^{2}(\theta(Y)X^{\flat}-\theta(X)Y^{\flat}).

The curvature of a two-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is completely determined by its Gaussian curvature
K^{g}\in C^{\infty}(M)
. For a one-form
\theta
, the curvature acts as
(R^{g}(X,Y)\theta)Z=-\theta(R^{g}(X,Y)Z)
. Then a computation gives:

R^{g}(X,Y)\theta=K^{g}(\theta(Y)X^{\flat}-\theta(X)Y^{\flat}).

Comparing this expression with (
11
) we obtain
(K^{g}-4\lambda_{R}^{2})(\theta(Y)X^{\flat}-\theta(X)Y^{\flat})=0
for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
.

Let
U:=\{p\in M\mid\theta_{p}\neq 0\}
. The subset
U\subset M
is open. Let
p\in U
, take
X_{p}=\theta^{\sharp}_{p}\in T_{p}M
and
Y_{p}\in T_{p}M
non-zero and orthogonal to
X_{p}
. Then
(\theta(Y)X^{\flat}-\theta(X)Y^{\flat})_{p}=-\langle\theta_{p},\theta_{p}\rangle Y_{p}\neq 0
and
K^{g}_{p}=4\lambda_{R}^{2}
. Since the point
p\in U
was arbitrary, we conclude that
K^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
on
U
.

Suppose that
U\subset M
is not dense, then its complement
U^{c}:=M\setminus U=\{p\in M\mid\theta_{p}=0\}
contains an open set
W
. On
W
,
\theta=0
implies that
\mathrm{d}f=0
, hence
f
is constant on
W
and
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=0
on
W
. The relation
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}fg
implies that
\lambda_{R}^{2}f=0
on
W
. Since
\lambda_{R}\neq 0
, then
f=0
on
W
. This implies that
r=0
on
W
since
r^{2}=f^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
, but this is not possible since
r
is nowhere vanishing. Therefore,
U^{c}
has empty interior, i.e.
U
is dense. Finally, since
K^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
on a dense open subset
U\subset M
, we conclude that
K^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
on
M
.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
(M,g)
be a two-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be an imaginary Killing spinor with Killing number
i\lambda_{I}
. Then
(M,g)
has constant negative Gaussian curvature
K^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.5
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=0.

These equations imply that
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
. Let
V_{r}:=\mathrm{grad}(r)=(\mathrm{d}r)^{\sharp}\in\Gamma(TM)
. Then:

g(\nabla^{g}_{X}V_{r},Y)=\mathrm{Hess}(r)(X,Y)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg(X,Y)=g(4\lambda_{I}^{2}rX,Y)

for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
, thus
\nabla^{g}_{X}V_{r}=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rX
. A computation gives us:

(12)

R^{g}(X,Y)V_{r}:=(\nabla^{g}_{X}\nabla^{g}_{Y}-\nabla^{g}_{Y}\nabla^{g}_{X}-\nabla^{g}_{[X,Y]})V_{r}=4\lambda_{I}^{2}(X(r)Y-Y(r)X).

Recall that the curvature of a two-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is completely determined by its Gaussian curvature
K^{g}\in C^{\infty}(M)
, which is given by:

R^{g}(X,Y)Z=K^{g}(g(Y,Z)X-g(X,Z)Y).

Setting
Z=V_{r}
we obtain
R^{g}(X,Y)V_{r}=K^{g}(Y(r)X-X(r)Y)
. Comparing this expression with (
12
) we obtain
(K^{g}+4\lambda_{I}^{2})(X(r)Y-Y(r)X)=0
for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
.

Let
U:=\{p\in M\mid(V_{r})_{p}\neq 0\}
. The subset
U\subset M
is open. Let
p\in U
, take
X_{p}=(V_{r})_{p}\in T_{p}M
and
Y_{p}\in T_{p}M
non-zero and orthogonal to
X_{p}
. Then
(X(r)Y-Y(r)X)_{p}=g_{p}((V_{r})_{p},(V_{r})_{p})Y_{p}\neq 0
and
K^{g}_{p}=-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
. Since the point
p\in U
was arbitrary, we conclude that
K^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
on
U
.

Suppose that
U\subset M
is not dense, then its complement
U^{c}:=M\setminus U=\{p\in M\mid(V_{r})_{p}=0\}
contains an open set
W
. On
W
,
V_{r}=0
implies that
\mathrm{d}r=0
, hence
r
is constant on
W
and
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=0
on
W
. The relation
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
implies that
\lambda_{I}^{2}r=0
on
W
. Since
r
is nowhere vanishing and
\lambda_{I}\neq 0
, this is not possible. Therefore,
U^{c}
has empty interior, i.e.
U
is dense. Finally, since
K^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
on a dense open subset
U\subset M
, we conclude that
K^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
on
M
.
∎

6.2.
Spin-c Killing spinors in dimension three

Let
(S,\gamma_{\ell})
be a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules on a three-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Assume that
(S,\gamma_{\ell})
is equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of positive adjoint type. The truncated Kähler-Atiyah bundle in this dimension and signature is defined on:

\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M=\mathbb{C}\oplus T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M,

where here
\mathbb{C}
denotes the trivial complex line bundle, endowed with the product:

\alpha\vee\beta=\mathcal{P}_{<}(\alpha\diamond\beta+i\ell*\tau(\alpha\diamond\beta)),\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\wedge^{<}T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M
. Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By the global version of Theorem
2.9
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
(M,g)
be a three-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold. A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta,

where
r\in C^{\infty}(M)
is nowhere vanishing and
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
satisfying
r^{2}=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in\Omega^{<}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. The linear equation
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
are real. Set
r:=\alpha^{(0)}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\theta:=\alpha^{(1)}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta
. Using that
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=r^{2}+2r\theta+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
, we obtain that the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
is equivalent to
r^{2}=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
. Since
\Scr H
is a positive-definite admissible Hermitian pairing and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is nowhere vanishing, we have that
r=\frac{1}{2}\Scr H(\eta,\eta)
is nowhere vanishing as well, thus it suffices to take
\beta=1
in the quadratic equation
\smash{\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}}
to conclude.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Since
\widehat{\alpha}
and
r
are nowhere vanishing, and the relation
r^{2}=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
holds, the one-form
\theta
is nowhere vanishing too.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
(M,g)
be a three-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be a Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}
. Then:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\ell\lambda_{R}*\theta,

where
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Proof.

In the case of a Killing spinor, we have that
\mathcal{A}_{X}=i\lambda\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(X^{\flat})\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
, so its symbol is just
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=i\lambda X^{\flat}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. We compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{X}\vee\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i\lambda X^{\flat}\vee(r+\theta)=i\lambda(rX^{\flat}+\theta(X)-i\ell*(X^{\flat}\wedge\theta)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\vee\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})

\displaystyle=(r+\theta)\vee(-i\bar{\lambda}X^{\flat})=-i\bar{\lambda}(rX^{\flat}+\theta(X)+i\ell*(X^{\flat}\wedge\theta)).

By Corollary
4.11
, if the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta=\mathcal{A}_{X}(\eta)
, then
\nabla^{g}_{X}\widehat{\alpha}=\mathfrak{a}_{X}\vee\widehat{\alpha}+\widehat{\alpha}\vee\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})
. Separating by degrees this equation we obtain:

\nabla^{g}_{X}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta(X),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rX^{\flat}+2\ell\lambda_{R}*(X^{\flat}\wedge\theta)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. These two equations are equivalent to those of the statement.
∎

Using
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta
and
\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\ell\lambda_{R}*\theta
we compute the Hessian of the function
r
:

\mathrm{Hess}(r)=\nabla^{g}\mathrm{d}r=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg+4\ell\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}*\theta.

Since
\mathrm{Hess}(r)
is a symmetric tensor and
\theta
is nowhere vanishing, we have that
\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}=0
, as expected.

Corollary 6.11
.

A three-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
admits a real spin-c Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda_{R}
if and only if it is an
\alpha
-Sasakian manifold with
\alpha=2\lambda_{R}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.10
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=0,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\ell\lambda_{R}*\theta.

The first equation implies that
r
is a non-zero constant function. The second equation implies that
\theta^{\sharp}
is a Killing vector field since
\mathscr{L}_{\theta^{\sharp}}g=\mathrm{Sym}(\nabla^{g}\theta)=0
. Set
\xi:=\frac{1}{r}\theta^{\sharp}
. Then
\xi
is a Killing vector field of unit length. Set
\varphi:=-\frac{1}{2\lambda_{R}}\nabla^{g}\xi
and
\zeta:=\xi^{\flat}
. Then:

\nabla^{g}_{X}\xi=\tfrac{1}{r}\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta^{\sharp}=\tfrac{1}{r}(\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta)^{\sharp}=\tfrac{1}{r}(-2\ell\lambda_{R}\iota_{X}(*\theta))^{\sharp}=-2\ell\lambda_{R}(\iota_{X}(*\zeta))^{\sharp}

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. Setting
J(X):=(\iota_{X}(*\zeta))^{\sharp}
, we get
\varphi(X)=-\frac{1}{2\lambda_{R}}\nabla^{g}_{X}\xi=\ell J(X)
, thus
\varphi^{2}=J^{2}
. Now let
\{e_{1},e_{2},\xi\}
be an orthonormal frame of
(M,g)
. Then
J(e_{1})=e_{2}
,
J(e_{2})=-e_{1}
, and
J(\xi)=0
. Hence:

\varphi^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}+\zeta\otimes\xi

since
\zeta(e_{1})=\zeta(e_{2})=0
and
\zeta(\xi)=1
. Finally, we compute:

\displaystyle((\nabla^{g}_{X}\varphi)Y)^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{X}\varphi(Y)^{\flat}-\varphi(\nabla^{g}_{X}Y)^{\flat}=\ell\nabla^{g}_{X}(\iota_{Y}(*\zeta))-\ell\iota_{\nabla^{g}_{X}Y}(*\zeta)=\ell\iota_{Y}(*\nabla^{g}_{X}\zeta)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{R}\iota_{Y}(*\iota_{X}(*\zeta))=-2\lambda_{R}\iota_{Y}(**(\zeta\wedge X^{\flat}))=2\lambda_{R}\iota_{Y}(X^{\flat}\wedge\zeta)

\displaystyle=2\lambda_{R}(g(X,Y)\zeta-\zeta(Y)X^{\flat}),

which gives
(\nabla^{g}_{X}\varphi)Y=2\lambda_{R}(g(X,Y)\xi-\zeta(Y)X)
. Therefore, we conclude that
(M,g)
is an
\alpha
-Sasakian manifold with
\alpha=2\lambda_{R}
.

To prove the converse, we use the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta
, which is computed in
[
23
, Cor. 5.5]
using an admissible complex-bilinear pairing of negative adjoint type. Its global version yields a nowhere vanishing isotropic
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex one-form
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
. By Corollary
5.11
, the equation
\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta=i\lambda X^{\flat}\cdot\eta
is equivalent to
\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\beta=\mathfrak{a}_{X}\vee\beta+\beta\vee(\pi\circ\tau)(\mathfrak{a}_{X})
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=i\lambda X^{\flat}
. In the case that
\eta
is a real Killing spinor, i.e.
\lambda=\lambda_{R}
, we obtain:

(13)

\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\beta=i\lambda_{R}(X^{\flat}\vee\beta-\beta\vee X^{\flat})=i\lambda_{R}(2i\ell*(\beta\wedge X^{\flat}))=-2\ell\lambda_{R}\iota_{X}(*\beta)

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. Hence, we have to prove that every
\alpha
-Sasakian three-manifold, which automatically admits a
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(3)
-structure, admits a nowhere vanishing isotropic
\mathcal{L}
-valued one-form
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
for which Equation (
13
) is satisfied.

Assume
(M,g)
is an
\alpha
-Sasakian manifold with
\alpha=2\lambda_{R}
. Let
(\xi,\zeta,\varphi,g)
be the
\alpha
-Sasakian structure, where
\zeta=\xi^{\flat}
and the fundamental endomorphism satisfies:

\nabla^{g}_{X}\xi=-\alpha\varphi(X)=-2\lambda_{R}\varphi(X).

We identify
\varphi=\ell J
, where
J
is the complex structure defined by the induced orientation in the contact distribution
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)\subset TM
. Thus, we have:

\nabla^{g}_{X}\xi=-2\ell\lambda_{R}J(X^{\perp_{\xi}}),

where
X^{\perp_{\xi}}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)
denotes the orthogonal projection of
X
to
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)
. The Levi-Civita connection does not, in general, preserve the contact distribution
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)\subset TM
. Instead, by projecting orthogonally, we obtain an induced connection that we denote by
\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}
and is defined as follows:

\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}_{X}w:=(\nabla_{X}^{g}w)^{\perp_{\xi}}\,,\quad X\in\Gamma(TM)\,,\quad w\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)).

Furthermore,
\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}
preserves the complex structure
J
on
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)
, and therefore it defines a unitary connection on the complex line bundle
(\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta),J)
equipped with the connection
\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}
. We identify:

\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)^{\ast},J^{\ast})

as complex line bundles, where
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)^{\ast}
is the vector bundle dual to
\mathrm{Ker}(\zeta)
equipped with the induced complex structure
J^{*}
. Since
\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}
preserves
J
, it induces a unitary connection on
\mathcal{L}
which is naturally associated to a connection
A
on a principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
P
inducing
\mathcal{L}
. The pair
(P,A)
is unique modulo isomorphism of principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundles with connection. Let
\{e_{1},e_{2},\xi\}
be a local orthonormal frame such that
J(e_{1})=e_{2}
and
J(e_{2})=-e_{1}
, with dual coframe
\{e^{1},e^{2},\zeta\}
. We define
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
as follows:

\beta=(e^{1}+ie^{2})\otimes(e^{1}-ie^{2}),

where
e^{1}-ie^{2}
is regarded as a local unitary frame of
\mathcal{L}
. Note that this gives a globally well-defined, nowhere vanishing, isotropic
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex one-form on
M
. We have:

\nabla^{g}_{X}(e^{1}+ie^{2})=-iA(X)(e^{1}+ie^{2})+2\ell\lambda_{R}(iX_{1}-X_{2})\zeta,

where
X_{i}=e^{i}(X)
,
i=1,2
. Define the connection
\nabla^{g,A}:=\nabla^{g}\otimes\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}
on the tensor product bundle
T^{\ast}M\otimes\mathcal{L}
. By the previous calculation, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\beta

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{X}(e^{1}+ie^{2})\otimes(e^{1}-ie^{2})+(e^{1}+ie^{2})\otimes\nabla^{\perp_{\xi}}_{X}(e^{1}-ie^{2})

\displaystyle=2\ell\lambda_{R}(iX_{1}-X_{2})\zeta\otimes(e^{1}-ie^{2})

\displaystyle=-2\ell\lambda_{R}(*\beta)(X^{\perp_{\xi}}),

where
X^{\perp_{\xi}}
is the projection of
X
to the orthogonal complement of
\xi
. Defining a new connection
\nabla^{g,\bar{A}}
as follows:

\nabla^{g,\bar{A}}_{X}\beta:=\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\beta-2i\ell\lambda_{R}\zeta(X)\beta

we immediately obtain:

\nabla^{g,\bar{A}}_{X}\beta=-2\ell\lambda_{R}\iota_{X}(*\beta)

and thus we obtain a solution to Equation (
13
). Hence, applying Theorem
5.10
, we conclude the existence of a real spin-c Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda_{R}
on
(M,g)
.
∎

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
(M,g)
be a three-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be an imaginary Killing spinor with Killing number
i\lambda_{I}
. Then the metric
g
is locally as follows:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+e^{-4\lambda_{I}t}g_{N},

where
g_{N}
is a Riemannian metric on the two-dimensional manifold
N
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.10
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg.

These equations imply that
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
. Hence, the metric
g
is locally a warped product:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+F(t)^{2}g_{N}

for a positive function
F(t)
, see e.g.
[
48
, Thm. 4.3.3]
. The coordinate
t
is defined by
t:=-(2\lambda_{I})^{-1}\log(r)
, which satisfies that
\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{r}\theta
is nowhere vanishing and
\partial_{t}=(\mathrm{d}t)^{\sharp}
is a unit vector field such that
\mathrm{d}t(\partial_{t})=1
. The Riemannian metric
g_{N}
is defined on a suitable domain
N\subset\{p\in M\mid t(p)=t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\}
. It is a standard exercise to show that the Hessian of a function
r(t)
in this warped product situation is given by:

(14)

\mathrm{Hess}(r)=r^{\prime\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+r^{\prime}(t)F^{\prime}(t)F(t)g_{N}.

In our case
r(t)=e^{-2\lambda_{I}t}
, so
r^{\prime}(t)=-2\lambda_{I}r(t)
and
r^{\prime\prime}(t)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t)
. Comparing with
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t)g
we obtain that
F^{\prime}(t)=-2\lambda_{I}F(t)
, thus
F(t)=e^{-2\lambda_{I}t}
, where we have absorbed the constant into
g_{N}
.
∎

6.3.
Spin-c Killing spinors in dimension four

Let
(S,\gamma)
be a bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules on a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Assume that
(S,\gamma)
is equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of positive adjoint type. Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By the global version of Theorem
2.6
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
(M,g)
be a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold. A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a nowhere vanishing irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu,

where
r\in C^{\infty}(M)
is nowhere vanishing,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
\theta,\vartheta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
, and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
satisfying:

\displaystyle\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle+f^{2}=3r^{2},\qquad*(\omega\wedge\vartheta)=r\theta,

\displaystyle*(\theta\wedge\vartheta)+f*\omega=-r\omega,\qquad\theta\wedge\omega=r*\vartheta,\qquad\omega\wedge\omega=-2rf\nu.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{4}\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}
, where
\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. The linear equation
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
, and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
are real; and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}
are imaginary. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
\theta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
,
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
, and
i*\vartheta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}
for
\vartheta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. The term
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
is a multiple of the volume form
\nu
, that is,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}=f\nu
for some
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu
. Note that
*\vartheta=\vartheta\diamond\nu
. A computation gives us:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(r+\theta+i\omega+i\vartheta\diamond\nu+f\nu)\diamond(r+\theta+i\omega+i\vartheta\diamond\nu+f\nu)

\displaystyle=r^{2}+2r\theta+2ir\omega+2ir\vartheta\diamond\nu+2rf\nu

\displaystyle\quad+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+i(\theta\diamond\omega+\omega\diamond\theta)+i(\theta\diamond\vartheta-\vartheta\diamond\theta)\diamond\nu

\displaystyle\quad-\omega\diamond\omega-(\omega\diamond\vartheta+\vartheta\diamond\omega)\diamond\nu+2if\omega\diamond\nu+\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle+f^{2}

\displaystyle=r^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle+f^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2r\theta+2*(\omega\wedge\vartheta)+2ir\omega-2i*(\theta\wedge\vartheta)-2if*\omega

\displaystyle\quad+2ir*\vartheta+2i\theta\wedge\omega+2rf\nu-\omega\wedge\omega,

where we have used that
\nu\diamond\alpha=\pi(\alpha)\diamond\nu
for all
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\nu\diamond\nu=1
. Therefore, the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
becomes the system of equations of the statement. Since
\Scr H
is a positive-definite admissible Hermitian pairing and
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is nowhere vanishing, we have that
r=\frac{1}{4}\Scr H(\eta,\eta)
is nowhere vanishing as well, thus it suffices to take
\beta=1
in the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
to conclude.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

Since
r
is nowhere vanishing, from the equations of Proposition
6.13
we conclude that
\omega
is nowhere vanishing too. Moreover, if
\theta=0
, then
\vartheta=0
and
f*\omega=-r\omega
, thus
f
is also nowhere vanishing. Hence, using that
*^{2}=\mathrm{Id}
on two-forms, we obtain that
f^{2}=r^{2}
, i.e.
f=-\mu r
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In this situation
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\omega-\mu r\nu
satisfying
*\omega=\mu\omega
and
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2r^{2}
is the Hermitian square of a
chiral
spinor with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, see
[
23
, Cor. 5.7]
. The same conclusion holds if
\vartheta=0
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu
be the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
. Then:

\langle\vartheta,\theta\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=r^{2}-f^{2},\qquad\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=r^{2}+f^{2}.

Proof.

From Proposition
6.13
we have
r*\theta=-\vartheta\wedge\omega
and
r*\vartheta=\theta\wedge\omega
. Hence
r\langle\theta,\vartheta\rangle\nu=\theta\wedge(r*\vartheta)=0
, which implies
\langle\vartheta,\theta\rangle=0
since
r
is nowhere vanishing. Similarly:

r\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle\nu=\vartheta\wedge(r*\vartheta)=\vartheta\wedge\theta\wedge\omega=r\theta\wedge*\theta=r\langle\theta,\theta\rangle\nu,

which implies
\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
. Recall that for
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
we have
(*\sigma_{1})\wedge(*\sigma_{2})=\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2}
. Using this,
r\omega=-f*\omega-*(\theta\wedge\vartheta)
, and
\omega\wedge\omega=-2rf\nu
we obtain:

\displaystyle r\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\nu

\displaystyle=r\omega\wedge*\omega=-f(*\omega)\wedge(*\omega)-*(\theta\wedge\vartheta)\wedge(*\omega)

\displaystyle=-f\omega\wedge\omega-\theta\wedge\vartheta\wedge\omega=2rf^{2}\nu+r\langle\theta,\theta\rangle\nu,

which implies
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2f^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
. Finally, plugging all these relations in the degree zero equation of Proposition
6.13
yields
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=r^{2}-f^{2}
, which in turn gives
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=r^{2}+f^{2}
.
∎

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
(M,g)
be a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be a Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}
. Then:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}\omega,\qquad\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{I}*\omega+2\lambda_{R}fg,

and:

\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=2\lambda_{R}X^{\flat}\wedge\theta-2\lambda_{I}*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat})

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
, where
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Proof.

In the case of a Killing spinor, we have that
\mathcal{A}_{X}=i\lambda\Psi_{\gamma}(X^{\flat})\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
, so its symbol is just
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=i\lambda X^{\flat}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. We compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{X}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i\lambda X^{\flat}\diamond(r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu)

\displaystyle=i\lambda(rX^{\flat}+X^{\flat}\wedge\theta+\theta(X)+iX^{\flat}\wedge\omega+i\iota_{X}\omega+i\vartheta(X)\nu+i*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat})+f*X^{\flat}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})

\displaystyle=(r+\theta+i\omega+i*\vartheta+f\nu)\diamond(-i\bar{\lambda}X^{\flat})

\displaystyle=-i\bar{\lambda}(rX^{\flat}+\theta\wedge X^{\flat}+\theta(X)+i\omega\wedge X^{\flat}-i\iota_{X}\omega+i*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat})-i\vartheta(X)\nu-f*X^{\flat}).

By Corollary
4.8
, if the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\nabla^{g,A}_{X}\eta=\mathcal{A}_{X}(\eta)
, then
\nabla^{g}_{X}\widehat{\alpha}=\mathfrak{a}_{X}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}+\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{X})
. Separating by degrees this equation we obtain:

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{X}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta(X),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rX^{\flat}-2\lambda_{R}\iota_{X}\omega,\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=2\lambda_{R}X^{\flat}\wedge\theta-2\lambda_{I}*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat}),

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{X}(*\vartheta)=-2\lambda_{I}X^{\flat}\wedge\omega+2\lambda_{R}f*X^{\flat},\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}(f\nu)=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta(X)\nu

for all
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. From the first equation we obtain
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta
, from the second equation we obtain
\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}\omega
. From the fourth one we get
\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{I}*\omega+2\lambda_{R}fg
since the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
commutes with the Hodge star operator and
*^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
on one-forms, and from the last one we get
\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta
since
\nabla^{g}\nu=0
.
∎

Using
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta
and
\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg-2\lambda_{R}\omega
we compute the Hessian of the function
r
:

\mathrm{Hess}(r)=\nabla^{g}\mathrm{d}r=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg+4\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}\omega.

Since
\mathrm{Hess}(r)
is a symmetric tensor and
\omega
is nowhere vanishing, we have that
\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I}=0
, as expected.

Corollary 6.17
.

Let
(M,g)
be a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be a real Killing spinor with Killing number
\lambda_{R}
. Then the metric
g
is locally as follows:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+\sin^{2}(2\lambda_{R}t)g_{N}

around any point where
\mathrm{d}f\neq 0
. Moreover, if
\mathrm{d}f_{p}=0
for
p\in M
, then
(M,g)
is locally isometric to the standard sphere of constant positive sectional curvature
\mathrm{sec}^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
on some neighborhood of
p
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.16
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=0,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{R}\omega,\qquad\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{R}fg,\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=2\lambda_{R}X^{\flat}\wedge\theta.

From
\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta
and
\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{R}fg
we get
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}fg
. Let
U:=\{p\in M\mid\mathrm{d}f_{p}\neq 0\}
. The function
f
has no critical points on
U
, hence
\vartheta
is nowhere vanishing on
U
. From
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}fg
we get that the metric
g
is locally a warped product:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+F(t)^{2}g_{N}

for a positive function
F(t)
. The coordinate
t
is defined by:

\mathrm{d}t:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{r^{2}-f^{2}}}\vartheta.

Integrating
\mathrm{d}f=-2\lambda_{R}\vartheta=-2\lambda_{R}\sqrt{r^{2}-f^{2}}\mathrm{d}t
we obtain
\arccos(\frac{f}{r})=2\lambda_{R}(t-t_{0})
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, after shifting
t
to absorb the factor
t_{0}
, we obtain
f(t)=r\cos(2\lambda_{R}t)
and
\smash{\sqrt{r^{2}-f(t)^{2}}}=r\sin(2\lambda_{R}t)
. Comparing
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}fg
with the expression (
14
), we obtain:

f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}f(t),\qquad f^{\prime}(t)F^{\prime}(t)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}f(t)F(t),

which is solved by
F(t)=\sin(2\lambda_{R}t)
, where we have absorbed the constant into
g_{N}
.

For every point in the complement
U^{c}=\{p\in M\mid\mathrm{d}f_{p}=0\}
we have
f_{p}\neq 0
. Indeed, if
\mathrm{d}f_{p}=0
, then
\vartheta_{p}=0
, which implies that
f^{2}_{p}=r^{2}_{p}
by Lemma
6.15
, thus
f_{p}
is non-zero since
r
is nowhere vanishing. Hence
g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+G(t)^{2}g_{S^{3}}
on some open neighborhood of
p\in M
. Comparing
\mathrm{Hess}(f)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}fg
with the expression (
14
), we obtain:

f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}f(t),\qquad f^{\prime}(t)G^{\prime}(t)=-4\lambda_{R}^{2}f(t)G(t),

with the initial conditions
f(0)=f_{0}>0
,
f^{\prime}(0)=0
,
G(0)=0
, and
G^{\prime}(0)=1
. The first equation above gives
f(t)=f_{0}\cos(2\lambda_{R}t)
and the second equation gives
G(t)=\frac{1}{2\lambda_{R}}\sin(2\lambda_{R}t)
. Then:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{4\lambda_{R}^{2}}\sin^{2}(2\lambda_{R}t)g_{S^{3}}.

This is precisely the standard metric of the four-dimensional sphere, which has constant positive sectional curvature
\mathrm{sec}^{g}\equiv 4\lambda_{R}^{2}
.
∎

Let
q_{\eta}:=r^{2}-\langle\theta,\theta\rangle
. By Lemma
6.15
we have
q_{\eta}=f^{2}
. If we assume that
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
is an imaginary Killing spinor, then
\mathrm{d}f=0
by Proposition
6.16
. This implies that
q_{\eta}
is constant. We distinguish two cases depending on whether
q_{\eta}
is zero or not.

Corollary 6.18
.

Let
(M,g)
be a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be an imaginary Killing spinor with Killing number
i\lambda_{I}
and
q_{\eta}=0
. Then the metric
g
is locally as follows:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+e^{-4\lambda_{I}t}g_{N},

where
g_{N}
is a Riemannian metric on the three-dimensional manifold
N
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.16
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=0,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg,\qquad\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{I}*\omega,\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=-2\lambda_{I}*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat}).

Note that in this case we have
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=r^{2}
by Lemma
6.15
, which implies that
\theta
is nowhere vanishing, as well as
\mathrm{d}r
. We have seen that
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
. Hence, the metric
g
is locally a warped product:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+F(t)^{2}g_{N}

for a positive function
F(t)
. The coordinate
t
is defined by
t:=-(2\lambda_{I})^{-1}\log(r)
, which satisfies that
\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{r}\theta
is nowhere vanishing and
\partial_{t}=(\mathrm{d}t)^{\sharp}
is a unit vector field such that
\mathrm{d}t(\partial_{t})=1
. Comparing
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t)g
with the expression (
14
), we obtain that
F(t)=e^{-2\lambda_{I}t}
, where we have absorbed the constant into
g_{N}
.
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

Let
(M,g)
be a four-dimensional spin-c Riemannian manifold and let
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
be an imaginary Killing spinor with Killing number
i\lambda_{I}
and
q_{\eta}>0
. Then the metric
g
is locally as follows:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+\sinh^{2}(2\lambda_{I}t)g_{N}

around any point where
\mathrm{d}r\neq 0
. Moreover, if
\mathrm{d}r_{p}=0
for
p\in M
, then
(M,g)
is locally isometric to the hyperbolic space of constant negative sectional curvature
\mathrm{sec}^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
on some neighborhood of
p
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.16
we have:

\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta,\qquad\mathrm{d}f=0,\qquad\nabla^{g}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}rg,\qquad\nabla^{g}\vartheta=2\lambda_{I}*\omega,\qquad\nabla^{g}_{X}\omega=-2\lambda_{I}*(\vartheta\wedge X^{\flat}).

Let
U:=\{p\in M\mid\mathrm{d}r_{p}\neq 0\}
. The function
r
has no critical points on
U
, hence
\theta
is nowhere vanishing on
U
. From
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
we get that the metric
g
is locally a warped product:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+F(t)^{2}g_{N}

for a positive function
F(t)
. The coordinate
t
is defined by:

\mathrm{d}t:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{r^{2}-f^{2}}}\theta.

Integrating
\mathrm{d}r=-2\lambda_{I}\theta=-2\lambda_{I}\sqrt{r^{2}-f^{2}}\mathrm{d}t
we obtain
\operatorname{arcosh}(\frac{r}{f})=-2\lambda_{I}(t-t_{0})
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, after shifting
t
to absorb the factor
t_{0}
, we obtain
r(t)=f\cosh(2\lambda_{I}t)
and
\smash{\sqrt{r(t)^{2}-f^{2}}}=-f\sinh(2\lambda_{I}t)
. Comparing
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
with the expression (
14
), we obtain:

r^{\prime\prime}(t)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t),\qquad r^{\prime}(t)F^{\prime}(t)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t)F(t),

which is solved by
F(t)=-\sinh(2\lambda_{I}t)
, where we have absorbed the constant into
g_{N}
.

For every point in the complement
U^{c}=\{p\in M\mid\mathrm{d}r_{p}=0\}
we have
\mathrm{d}r_{p}=0
and
4\lambda_{I}^{2}r_{p}\neq 0
, hence
g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+G(t)^{2}g_{S^{3}}
on some open neighborhood of
p\in M
, where
g_{S^{3}}
is the standard metric on the unit sphere
S^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, see e.g.
[
48
, Thm. 4.3.3]
. Comparing
\mathrm{Hess}(r)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}rg
with the expression (
14
), we obtain:
