r^{\prime\prime}(t)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t),\qquad r^{\prime}(t)G^{\prime}(t)=4\lambda_{I}^{2}r(t)G(t),

with the initial conditions
r(0)=r_{0}>0
,
r^{\prime}(0)=0
,
G(0)=0
, and
G^{\prime}(0)=1
. The first equation above gives
r(t)=r_{0}\cosh(2\lambda_{I}t)
and the second equation gives
G(t)=\frac{1}{2\lambda_{I}}\sinh(2\lambda_{I}t)
. Then:

g=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{4\lambda_{I}^{2}}\sinh^{2}(2\lambda_{I}t)g_{S^{3}}.

This is precisely the metric of the four-dimensional hyperbolic space, which has constant negative sectional curvature
\mathrm{sec}^{g}\equiv-4\lambda_{I}^{2}
.
∎

7.
Supersymmetric Brinkmann four-manifolds

In this section, we consider a natural spinorial differential system that arises as the supersymmetry conditions for the celebrated Freedman’s minimal gauged four-dimensional supergravity, or Freedman’s gauged supergravity for short
[
16
]
. Let
M
be an oriented four-manifold and let
P
be a principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle defined on
M
. A
supersymmetric configuration
of Freedman’s gauged supergravity on
(M,P)
is, by definition, a tuple
(g,A,\eta)
consisting of a Lorentzian metric
g
of
mostly plus
signature on
M
, a connection
A
on
P
, and an irreducible complex chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
satisfying the following equations:

(15)

\nabla^{g,A}\eta=0\,,\qquad(F^{\mu}_{A}-\lambda)\cdot\eta=0,

where:

•

S=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
is a bundle of irreducible complex spinors on
(M,g)
, which is associated to a given
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(3,1)
-structure
Q
, whose characteristic
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle is
P
.

•

\nabla^{g,A}
is the canonical lift of the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and
A
to
P
.

•

F_{A}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is the curvature of
A
, understood as a real two-form on
M
.

•

\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
F_{A}^{\mu}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the following complex combination:

F_{A}^{\mu}:=\frac{1}{2}(F_{A}+i\mu\ast_{g}F_{A}).

Note that we have
\ast_{g}F_{A}^{\mu}=-i\mu F_{A}^{\mu}
since
\ast_{g}^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
on two-forms. Therefore,
F_{A}^{+}
is complex anti-self-dual, whereas
F_{A}^{-}
is complex self-dual.

•

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
is a non-zero complex constant.

The first equation in (
15
) is typically called the
gravitino equation
, since it arises from imposing the vanishing of the infinitesimal supersymmetry transformation of the gravitino on a bosonic background, whereas the second equation in (
15
) is commonly called the
gaugino equation
, since it arises from imposing the vanishing of the infinitesimal supersymmetry transformation of the gaugino on a bosonic background. The gravitino equation implies, therefore, the existence of a spin-c parallel spinor on a Lorentzian four-manifold. The study of such parallel spinors in pseudo-Riemannian signature has been pioneered in
[
28
,
29
]
, where the holonomy groups of all simply connected irreducible non-locally symmetric pseudo-Riemannian spin-c manifolds that admit parallel spinors are described, obtaining a classification in the Lorentzian symmetric case. In contrast to
opere citato
, the
\mathrm{U}(1)
-connection occurring in the parallelicity condition of the gravitino equation has to satisfy its own coupled equation.

Let
{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)
denote the set of time-orientable, strongly spin-c, Lorentzian metrics on
M
, and let
C(P)
denote the affine space of connections on
P
. It is convenient to refer to supersymmetric solutions simply as pairs
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)\times C(P)
, meaning that
(g,A)
is supersymmetric for some choice of irreducible complex spinor bundle
S
and section
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
. A supersymmetric configuration
(g,A)
is a
supersymmetric solution
of Freedman’s gauged supergravity on
(M,P)
if
(g,A)
satisfies the following differential system:

(16)

\mathrm{Ric}^{g}-\frac{1}{2}s_{g}g=\Lambda g+\mathfrak{e}^{2}\,F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A}-\frac{\mathfrak{e}^{2}}{2}|F_{A}|^{2}_{g}g\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}\ast_{g}F_{A}=0

for a pair of non-zero real constants
\Lambda,\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
. These are precisely the equations of the Lorentzian-signature Einstein-Maxwell system with a possibly non-vanishing cosmological constant
\Lambda
and coupling constant
\mathfrak{e}
.

Remark 7.1
.

The proper supersymmetry conditions and equations of motion of Freedman’s gauged supergravity fix
\mathfrak{e}
and
\Lambda
in terms of
\lambda
. Here, we have opted for greater initial flexibility in order to encounter the relation between
\mathfrak{e}
and
\lambda
as a consistency condition derived from imposing that a given supersymmetric configuration is a supersymmetric solution. Note that, crucially, if
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
, then
F_{A}^{-\mu}\cdot\eta=0
identically on
M
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
be an irreducible complex chiral spinor with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on an orientable Lorentzian four-manifold
(M,g)
. Then:

•

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
with
\kappa=1
if and only if there exists an isotropic real one-form
\widehat{u}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that:

\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{u}+i\mu\ast\widehat{u}.

The one-form
\widehat{u}
is the so-called
Dirac current
of
\eta
.

•

A
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
is the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
if and only if it is a decomposable complex
\mu
-self-dual two-form valued in
\mathcal{L}
. That is, around every point in
M
there exists a local section
\mathfrak{l}
of
\mathcal{L}
such that:

\alpha=(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\otimes\mathfrak{l}\,,\qquad i\ast(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}

for a pair of local isotropic and orthogonal complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

Proof.

The Hermitian square of
\eta
is computed in
[
23
, Prop. 3.26]
using an admissible Hermitian pairing of positive adjoint type. We proceed to compute the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
pointwise, thus
S^{\mu}_{p}\cong\Sigma^{\mu}
and
(\wedge T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M\otimes\mathcal{L})_{p}\cong\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
for all
p\in M
. We equip
\Sigma
with a skew-symmetric admissible complex-bilinear pairing of positive adjoint type, see
[
23
, Prop. 3.18 and Table 3]
. Applying Theorem
2.7
to four Lorentzian dimensions, it follows that
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
if and only if:

(17)

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=4\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=4(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha,\quad\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=i\mu\alpha

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. The linear equations in (
17
) are solved by setting
\alpha=\omega
for a complex two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying:

i\ast\omega=\mu\omega.

The first equation in (
17
) becomes:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=\omega\diamond\omega=\omega\wedge\omega-\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0,

which in turn is equivalent to
\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
and
\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\theta_{2}\rangle=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle^{2}
for a pair of complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then the equation
i*\omega=\mu\omega
translates into
i*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
. Plugging
\theta_{1}^{\smash{\sharp}}
and
\theta_{2}^{\smash{\sharp}}
into this equation, we conclude that the one-forms
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are isotropic and mutually orthogonal. We complete
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
into a basis
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}\}
of
V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
are isotropic and orthogonal, and furthermore are
conjugate
to
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, that is:

\langle\theta_{1},\vartheta_{1}\rangle=\langle\theta_{2},\vartheta_{2}\rangle=1,\qquad\langle\theta_{1},\vartheta_{2}\rangle=\langle\theta_{2},\vartheta_{1}\rangle=0.

Setting
\beta=\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2}
, the second equation (
17
) becomes:

(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\diamond(\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2})\diamond(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=-4\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2},

where we have used that
((\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\diamond(\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2}))^{(0)}=-1
. Expanding the above equation yields an identity; hence, we conclude.
∎

The geometry of decomposable complex self-dual isotropic two-forms in four Lorentzian dimensions, as they occur in the previous lemma, has been extensively studied within the Newman-Penrose formalism. We use this characterization to refine the description of the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
.

Proposition 7.3
.

A
\mathcal{L}
-valued complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathcal{L})
is the complex-bilinear square of
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that locally around every point in
M
we have:

\alpha=u\wedge(l+i\mu n)\otimes\mathfrak{l},

where
\mathfrak{l}
is a local section of
\mathcal{L}
, and
l,n
are unit-norm orthogonal local one-forms orthogonal to
u
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
, there exists a local section
\mathfrak{l}
of
\mathcal{L}
and local complex one-forms, isotropic and orthogonal, such that
\alpha=(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\otimes\mathfrak{l}
and
i\ast(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
. Write:

\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=\omega_{R}+i\omega_{I}

for real two-forms
\omega_{R},\omega_{I}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
. The complex self-duality condition translates into:

\ast\omega_{R}=\mu\omega_{I}

and therefore
\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=\omega_{R}+i\mu\ast\omega_{R}
. From
\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=0
we obtain
\omega_{R}\wedge\ast\omega_{R}=0
, and from the complex self-duality condition we automatically have
(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\wedge(\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})=0
, which yields
\omega_{R}\wedge\omega_{R}=0
. These equations are solved by:

\displaystyle\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=u\wedge l+i\mu\ast(u\wedge l)

for local real one-forms
u,l
such that
u
is isotropic and
l
is of unit norm and orthogonal to
u
. We can introduce another, unit-norm, local one-form
n
that is orthogonal to both
u
and
l
and satisfies:

\ast(u\wedge l)=u\wedge n\,,\qquad\ast(u\wedge n)=-u\wedge l.

Hence:

\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=u\wedge(l+i\mu n)

and thus we can identify
\theta_{1}=u
and
\theta_{2}=l+i\mu n
.

Given a good open cover
\{U_{a}\}
of
M
labeled by an index
a
, we can perform the previous construction at every
U_{a}
, obtaining tuples
\{u_{a},l_{a},n_{a},\mathfrak{l}_{a}\}
such that:

u_{a}\wedge(l_{a}+i\mu n_{a})\otimes\mathfrak{l}_{a}=u_{b}\wedge(l_{b}+i\mu n_{b})\otimes\mathfrak{l}_{b}

on non-trivial overlaps
U_{ab}:=U_{a}\cap U_{b}\neq\emptyset
. Hence:

(18)

u_{a}=r_{ab}u_{b}\,,\qquad l_{a}+f^{l}_{ab}u_{a}+i\mu(n_{a}+f^{n}_{ab}u_{a})=e^{i\theta_{ab}}(l_{b}+i\mu n_{b})

for families of functions defined on overlaps:

r_{ab}\in C^{\infty}(U_{ab},\mathbb{R}_{>0}),\qquad f^{l}_{ab},f^{n}_{ab},\theta_{ab}\in C^{\infty}(U_{ab},\mathbb{R})

such that
\mathfrak{l}_{a}=r^{-1}_{ab}e^{i\theta_{ab}}\mathfrak{l}_{b}
. That is,
r^{-1}_{ab}e^{i\theta_{ab}}\colon U_{ab}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
are transition functions for
\mathcal{L}
, which therefore satisfy the corresponding cocycle condition. Since the sheaf of smooth positive functions on
M
is soft, its cohomology vanishes, implying that the family of isotropic real one-forms
u_{a}
assembles into a well-defined isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

The decomposition of the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
given in the previous proposition provides an alternative perspective on the notion of
isotropic parallelism
introduced in
[
51
]
for an irreducible real spinor on a four-dimensional Lorentzian manifold.

The Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of the same irreducible chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
are not independent. Instead, it follows from
[
23
, Prop. 3.30]
that
\widehat{u}=c\,u
for a non-zero real constant
c
which is unimportant for our purposes. Hence, in the following, we will only refer to
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
as the Dirac current of
\eta
. As a corollary to the previous proposition, we have the following characterization of
\mathcal{L}
.

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
(S,\gamma,\Scr B)
be a complex-bilinear paired spinor bundle. Then,
S
admits a nowhere vanishing section
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
with Dirac current
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
only if:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{u}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{L},

that is, only if the screen bundle determined by
u
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}
as rank-two real vector bundles.

Remark 7.6
.

We remind the reader that the
screen bundle
defined by a nowhere vanishing isotropic one form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
is the following abstract vector bundle:

\mathfrak{S}_{u}:=\frac{\mathrm{Ker}(u)}{\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle}.

Proof.

Follows from Equation (
18
) after taking the quotient by the span of
u
, which eliminates the terms proportional to
u
and therefore implies that the collection of local basis
(l_{a},n_{a})
of the screen bundle is related on overlaps by the transition functions of
\mathcal{L}
, once these are reduced to
\mathrm{U}(1)
.
∎

The Hermitian square of
\eta
suffices to obtain convenient necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a solution to the gaugino equation in (
15
).

Lemma 7.7
.

A pair
(M,P)
admits a solution
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)\times C(P)
to the gaugino equation for a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that:

(19)

F_{A}(u^{\sharp_{g}})+\lambda_{R}u=0\,,\qquad F_{A}\wedge u+\mu\,\lambda_{I}\ast_{g}u=0.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
, a pair
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)\times C(P)
satisfies the gaugino equation if and only if:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(F^{\mu}_{A}-\lambda)\diamond(u+i\mu\ast_{g}u)

\displaystyle=2(F^{\mu}_{A}\wedge u-F_{A}^{\mu}(u^{\sharp_{g}}))-(\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I})u-\mu(i\lambda_{R}-\lambda_{I})\ast_{g}u

\displaystyle=F_{A}\wedge u+i\mu(\ast_{g}F_{A})\wedge u-F_{A}(u^{\sharp_{g}})-i\mu\ast_{g}(F_{A}\wedge u)-(\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I})u-\mu(i\lambda_{R}-\lambda_{I})\ast_{g}u,

where we have written
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}
. Isolating by degree and real and imaginary part, this equation is equivalent to:

\displaystyle F_{A}\wedge u+\mu\,\lambda_{I}\ast_{g}u=0\,,\qquad(\ast_{g}F_{A})\wedge u-\lambda_{R}\ast_{g}u=0\,,

\displaystyle F_{A}(u^{\sharp_{g}})+\lambda_{R}u=0\,,\qquad\ast_{g}(F_{A}\wedge u)+\mu\,\lambda_{I}u=0\,.

The first and fourth equations are equivalent via applying the Hodge operator. Similarly, the second and third equations are equivalent via the same mechanism, upon using
\ast_{g}^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
on two-forms.
∎

Equations (
19
) are a natural generalization of the
Lorentzian instanton equations
obtained for an equation of the form
F_{A}\cdot\eta=0
and studied briefly in
[
51
]
for a real irreducible spinor. The Hermitian square of
\eta
can be further exploited to obtain a neat necessary condition for the first equation in (
15
).

Lemma 7.8
.

A pair
(M,P)
admits a solution
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)\times C(P)
to the gravitino equation for a nowhere vanishing spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
only if its Dirac current
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
satisfies:

(20)

\nabla^{g}u=0,

that is, only if its Dirac current is parallel with respect to the Levi-Civita connection on
(M,g)
. In particular,
(M,g,u)
is a Brinkmann four-manifold.

Proof.

Follows directly from Theorem
4.6
, after setting
\mathcal{A}=0
, which is the case for the first equation in (
15
).
∎

Equations (
19
) and (
20
) contain all the information that can be extracted from the supersymmetry transformations (
15
) through the Hermitian square of
\eta
. In other words, the Hermitian square of
\eta
satisfies the system of Theorem
4.6
for the supersymmetry transformations (
15
) if and only if it satisfies equations (
19
) and (
20
). This leads us to introduce the following weaker notion of supersymmetry.

Definition 7.9
.

A pair
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(M)\times C(P)
on
(M,P)
is a
quasi-supersymmetric
configuration of Freedman’s gauged supergravity if and only if there exists a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that
(g,A,u)
satisfies equations (
19
) and (
20
).

Remark 7.10
.

The notion of
quasi-supersymmetry
, or
quasi-supersymmetric solutions
, can be applied to every supergravity with complex infinitesimal supersymmetry generators, and hence defines a weaker notion of supersymmetry which we are very intrigued to explore in the future for other, more general, supergravities.

Studying the full supersymmetry system of Freedman’s gauged supergravity is a subtle problem that we will consider in a separate publication. For the remainder of this section, we consider the necessary and sufficient conditions for a quasi-supersymmetric configuration of Freedman’s gauged supergravity to be a solution of the theory, focusing on a class of solutions naturally adapted to the presence of a nowhere vanishing parallel isotropic vector field.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
X
be an oriented two-dimensional manifold equipped with a principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
\pi\colon P\to X
, and let
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X\to X
be the canonical projection, where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
are intervals with coordinates
\mathfrak{u}
and
\mathfrak{v}
, respectively. A tuple
(g,A,u)
, where
(g,A)\in{\rm Lor}_{o}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X)\times C(\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P)
and
u\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X)
is isotropic on
(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X,g)
, is
standard Brinkmann
if:

(21)

g=\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot(\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})+h_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad u=\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\,,\qquad\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}A=0,

where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(X)
is a family of functions on
X
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of one-forms on
X
, and
h_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of complete Riemannian metrics on
X
, all parametrized by
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Remark 7.12
.

It is well-known that a Brinkmann space-time
[
5
]
, that is, a Lorentzian four-manifold equipped with a nowhere vanishing parallel vector field, is locally isometric to an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
equipped with a metric of the form given in (
21
), hence the terminology introduced in the previous definition. Brinkmann space-times describe idealized gravitational waves and are therefore of enormous importance in general relativity and mathematical physics. In fact, each of the ingredients has a neat interpretation:

•

\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the tidal potential. That is, in the geodesic equations for a test particle, the negative spatial gradient of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
appears exactly as an external force pushing or pulling the particle across the transverse space
X
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}
encodes the gravito-magnetic twist of the space-time, which induces Coriolis-like forces on the transverse spatial slices. This represents the gravito-magnetic twist, transforming the spacetime into a
gyraton

[
19
]
. It signifies that the source of the geometry is a beam of radiation carrying intrinsic angular momentum. For a test particle moving on the spatial slice
X
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}
acts identically to a magnetic vector potential, generating a velocity-dependent Coriolis-like force that twists the particle’s trajectory.

•

(X,h_{\mathfrak{u}})
is the dynamical wave-front of the wave. The coordinate
\mathfrak{u}
acts as the retarded time of the wave, and the
\mathfrak{u}
-constant slices define a family of parallel null hypersurfaces. Each of these null hypersurfaces represents the spacetime history of a single phase of the wave. Taking a spatial cross-section of this propagating wave isolates the wavefront itself. As
\mathfrak{u}
advances,
h_{\mathfrak{u}}
allows this screen to expand or shear. This dynamic geometry acts as a time-dependent mass matrix for particles, leading to phenomena such as kinetic damping as the space stretches beneath them.

Let
(g,A,u)
be a standard Brinkmann triple on
(\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times X,\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P)
. Then, we have the canonical identification:

\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P=\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times P\to\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times X.

Using this identification, we write:

(22)

A=(\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ\mathfrak{p})\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+(\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ\mathfrak{p})\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+a_{\mathfrak{u}}

for uniquely determined families of functions
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(X)
and a family of connections
a_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{1}(P)
, all parametrized by
\mathfrak{u}
. Note that condition
\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}A=0
is equivalent to none of the terms in the above expansion depending on
\mathfrak{v}
. Hence, we will equivalently refer to standard Brinkmann triples
(g,A,u)
on
(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X,\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P)
as tuples
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
on
(X,P)
, where
h_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of complete Riemannian metrics on
X
,
a_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of connections on
P
, and
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(X)
are families of functions on
X
.

Here, and for the remainder of this section,
\mathrm{d}\colon\Omega(X)\to\Omega(X)
will denote the exterior derivative on
X
.

Proposition 7.13
.

A standard Brinkmann configuration
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
on
(X,P)
is quasi-supersymmetric if and only if it satisfies the following differential system:

\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\lambda_{R}\mathfrak{u}+\lambda_{R}^{o}\,,\qquad\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I},

where
\lambda_{R}^{o}\in\mathbb{R}
is a real constant.

Proof.

Let
(g,A,u)
be such a standard Brinkmann configuration on
(\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times X,\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P)
, with associated tuple
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
. Taking the exterior derivative of Equation (
22
) as a one-form on the
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}P=\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times P
, we obtain:

F_{A}=-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}+\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}a_{\mathfrak{u}}+F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}.

We quickly compute:

\displaystyle F_{A}(u^{\sharp_{g}})

\displaystyle=F_{A}(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})=-\mathrm{d}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u},

\displaystyle F_{A}\wedge u

\displaystyle=F_{A}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}=-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}.

Plugging these expressions into both equations in (
19
), we obtain:

\mathrm{d}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\lambda_{R}\,,\qquad F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}},

where
\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}
is the Riemannian volume form of
(X,h_{\mathfrak{u}})
, and we have used
\ast_{g}u=\ast_{g}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}=-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}
. Hence,
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\lambda_{R}\,\mathfrak{u}+\lambda_{R}^{o}
for
\lambda_{R}^{o}\in\mathbb{R}
and the only non-trivial remaining condition is
F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}
, or equivalently:

\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}.

In particular,
a_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of Yang-Mills connections on
P
over
(X,h_{\mathfrak{u}})
.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
be a quasi-supersymmetric configuration on
(X,P)
. Then:

(23)

\displaystyle\ast_{g}F_{A}=\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}a_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\lambda_{R}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})-\lambda_{R}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}+\mu\lambda_{I}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge(\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}),

where
A
is given as in Equation (
22
).

Proof.

Set:

u:=\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\,,\qquad v:=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},

in terms of which we have:

F_{A}=u\wedge(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}a_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\lambda_{R}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})+\lambda_{R}u\wedge v+F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}.

The result follows from applying the following general identities to the previous formula:

\ast_{g}(u\wedge v)=-\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\,,\qquad\ast_{g}(u\wedge\alpha)=u\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\alpha,

where
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(X)
and we have used that, by Proposition
7.13
,
F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}
, which implies that
\ast_{g}F_{a_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}\ast_{g}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mu\lambda_{I}u\wedge v
.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

A quasi-supersymmetric configuration
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
on
(X,P)
satisfies the Maxwell equation in (
16
) if and only if the following equation holds:

\mathrm{d}\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}a_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\lambda_{R}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})+\lambda_{R}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}+\mu\lambda_{I}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}=0.

Proof.

We apply the exterior derivative to Equation (
23
), obtaining:

0=\mathrm{d}\ast_{g}F_{A}=-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}a_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}-\lambda_{R}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})-\lambda_{R}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\nu_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}-\mu\lambda_{I}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},

from which the result follows.
∎

We now introduce a natural class of standard Brinkmann configurations, which will be our main focus for the remainder of this section.

Definition 7.16
.

A standard Brinkmann configuration
(h_{\mathfrak{u}},a_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}})
on
(X,P)
is
stationary
if it is independent of
\mathfrak{u}
, in which case we will drop the subscript and write
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
.

We proceed now to elucidate the necessary and sufficient conditions for a stationary quasi-supersymmetric configuration
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
on
(X,P)
to be an honest solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity. The general,
non-stationary
, case leads to an interesting evolution problem in the null coordinate
\mathfrak{u}
, and will be considered elsewhere.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
be a stationary configuration on
(X,P)
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}=\mathrm{Ric}^{h}+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{h\ast}F_{\mathcal{A}})\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\frac{1}{2}\left(|F_{\mathcal{A}}|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\,,

\displaystyle s_{g}=s_{h}\,,

where
g
is given as in Equation (
21
) in terms of the given tuple
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
, and for convenience we have set
F_{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

For further reference, recall that the components of the metric inverse to (
21
) are given by:

\displaystyle g^{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}=0\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{v}}=1\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{u}i}=0\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{v}}=|\mathcal{A}|^{2}_{h}-\mathcal{H}\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{v}i}=-\mathcal{A}^{i}\,,\qquad g^{ij}=h^{ij}.

To obtain the Ricci curvature of
g
, we first compute its non-zero Christoffel symbols in a coordinate basis
(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},\partial_{i})
for
i=1,2
:

\displaystyle 2\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{A}^{\sharp_{h}})\,,\qquad 2\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}i}=\partial_{i}\mathcal{H}+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{A}^{\sharp_{h}},\partial_{i})\,,\qquad 2\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{ij}=(\delta^{h}\mathcal{A})(\partial_{i},\partial_{j})\,,

\displaystyle 2\Gamma_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}^{i}=-h^{ik}\partial_{k}\mathcal{H}\,,\qquad 2\Gamma^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}j}=h^{ik}\left(\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}\right)\,,\qquad\Gamma^{i}_{jk}=(\Gamma^{h})^{i}_{jk}\,,

where
\delta^{h}\colon\Omega^{1}(X)\to\Gamma(TX\odot TX)
denotes the symmetrization of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{h}
on
(X,h)
, and
\Gamma^{h}
denotes the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla^{h}
. We proceed to compute the Riemann curvature tensor
{\mathcal{R}}^{g}
in the coordinate basis
(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},\partial_{i})
, which we label by Greek indices. We have:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}=\nabla^{g}_{\mu}\nabla^{g}_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}-\nabla^{g}_{\nu}\nabla^{g}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}.

Since
\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}
is parallel, we immediately obtain
{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}=0
. Furthermore, because all Christoffel symbols with a lower
\mathfrak{v}
index identically vanish and the metric is independent of
\mathfrak{v}
, it follows that
{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mathfrak{v}\mu}\partial_{\rho}=0
. For the remaining elements in the coordinate basis, we obtain:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{ij}\partial_{k}={\mathcal{R}}^{h}_{ij}\partial_{k}+(\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{jk}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{ik}+\Gamma^{m}_{jk}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{im}-\Gamma^{m}_{ik}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{jm})\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}i}\partial_{j}=(\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}m}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}j}-\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{im})\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}+(\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}k}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}-\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\Gamma^{m}_{ik})\partial_{m}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{ij}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}=(\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{j\mathfrak{u}}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{i\mathfrak{u}}+\Gamma^{m}_{j\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{im}-\Gamma^{m}_{i\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{jm})\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}+(\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{j\mathfrak{u}}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{m}_{i\mathfrak{u}}+\Gamma^{k}_{j\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{m}_{ik}-\Gamma^{k}_{i\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{m}_{jk})\partial_{m}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}i}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}=(\Gamma^{m}_{i\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}m}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}-\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{im})\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}+(\Gamma^{k}_{i\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}k}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}-\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}\Gamma^{m}_{ik})\partial_{m}\,,

where
{\mathcal{R}}^{h}_{ij}\partial_{k}
is the Riemann curvature tensor of the transverse Riemannian metric
h
. From these explicit equations, we obtain the following non-zero components of the Ricci tensor of
g
. First, we consider
\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle=g({\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}i}\partial_{j},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})h^{ij}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}m}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}j}-\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{im})h^{ij}+(\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}k}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}-\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\Gamma^{m}_{ik})\mathcal{A}_{m}h^{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}h^{ij}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\partial_{k}\mathcal{H}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}j}h^{ij}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\mathcal{A}_{m}h^{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(|F_{\mathcal{A}}|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\right),

where
\nabla^{h\ast}\alpha=-\mathrm{Tr}_{h}(\nabla^{h}\alpha)
for any one-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(X)
. For the non-zero
mixed
components, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{k})

\displaystyle=g({\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}i}\partial_{j},\partial_{k})h^{ij}

\displaystyle=h_{mk}\left(\Gamma^{l}_{ij}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}l}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}-\Gamma^{l}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\Gamma^{m}_{il}\right)h^{ij}

\displaystyle=h_{mk}\left(\Gamma^{l}_{ij}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}l}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{m}_{\mathfrak{u}j}\right)h^{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{h\ast}F_{\mathcal{A}})_{k}.

For the
purely spatial
components, we have:

\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{i},\partial_{m})=g({\mathcal{R}}^{g}_{ij}\partial_{k},\partial_{m})h^{jk}=h({\mathcal{R}}^{h}_{ij}\partial_{k},\partial_{m})h^{jk}=\mathrm{Ric}^{h}(\partial_{i},\partial_{m})

and thus we conclude.
∎

Proposition 7.18
.

A quasi-supersymmetric stationary configuration
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
on
(X,P)
satisfies Freedman’s gauged supergravity equations (
16
) on
\mathcal{I}_{u}\times\mathcal{I}_{v}\times X
if and only if:

\displaystyle|F_{\mathcal{A}}|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}F_{\mathcal{A}}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\mu\lambda_{I}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=\mu\lambda_{I}F_{\mathcal{A}}\,,

\displaystyle s_{h}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}\,,\qquad 2\Lambda+\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}=0\,,

where
g
is given as in Equation (
21
) in terms of the given tuple
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
and
F_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.13
, a stationary configuration
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
on the pair
(X,P)
is quasi-supersymmetric if and only if
\phi
is constant, whence
\lambda_{R}=0
, and
a
satisfies
\ast_{h}F_{a}=\mu\lambda_{I}
. The corresponding connection
A
on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times X
is given by Equation (
22
), and consequently reads:

F_{A}=-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi+F_{a}.

Recall that
(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(w_{1},w_{2}):=\langle F_{A}(w_{1}),F_{A}(w_{2})\rangle_{g}
. We compute:

\displaystyle(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})=|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})=0\,,\qquad(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})=0\,,

\displaystyle(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},w)=-F_{a}(w,(\mathrm{d}\psi)^{\sharp_{h}})\,,\qquad(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},w)=0\,,

\displaystyle(F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A})(w_{1},w_{2})=\langle F_{a}(w_{1}),F_{a}(w_{2})\rangle_{h}\,,

where
w,w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(TX)
. Hence:

F_{A}\circ_{g}F_{A}=|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot F_{a}((\mathrm{d}\psi)^{\sharp_{h}})+F_{a}\circ_{h}F_{a}.

Using that
s_{g}=s_{h}
and
|F_{A}|_{g}^{2}=|F_{a}|_{h}^{2}
, together with the previous formulae and Lemma
7.17
, we substitute in the Einstein equation of (
16
), obtaining:

\displaystyle|F_{\mathcal{A}}|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}s_{h}=2\Lambda\mathcal{H}+\mathfrak{e}^{2}(2|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}-\mathcal{H}|F_{a}|^{2}_{h})\,,

\displaystyle 2\Lambda+s_{h}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}|F_{a}|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}F_{\mathcal{A}}-s_{h}\mathcal{A}=2\Lambda\mathcal{A}+2\mathfrak{e}^{2}F_{a}((\mathrm{d}\psi)^{\sharp_{h}})-\mathfrak{e}^{2}|F_{a}|^{2}_{h}\mathcal{A}\,,

\displaystyle 0=2\Lambda h+2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\,F_{a}\circ_{h}F_{a}-\mathfrak{e}^{2}|F_{a}|^{2}_{g}h\,.

Substituting
F_{a}=\mu\lambda_{I}\nu_{h}
and manipulating, the previous set of equations reduces to:

\displaystyle|F_{\mathcal{A}}|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}F_{\mathcal{A}}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\mu\lambda_{I}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi\,,

\displaystyle 2\Lambda+s_{h}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}\,,\qquad 2\Lambda+\mathfrak{e}^{2}\,\lambda_{I}^{2}=0\,.

Combining these equations with Proposition
7.15
, we obtain the conditions in the statement.
∎

Theorem 7.19
.

A standard stationary configuration
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
on the pair
(X,P)
is a quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity equations (
16
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times X
if and only if all the following conditions hold:

•

X=S^{2}
, that is,
X
is diffeomorphic to the two-dimensional sphere.

•

h
is the round metric of scalar curvature
s_{h}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}
, that is, of radius
R=|\mathfrak{e}\lambda_{I}|^{-1}
.

•

The Chern number
c(P)
satisfies
\lambda_{I}\mathfrak{e}^{2}c(P)=2\mu
, and
a
is a Yang-Mills connection on
P
.

•

\psi\in C^{\infty}(S^{2})
is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian on
(S^{2},h)
with the lowest non-zero eigenvalue.

•

\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(S^{2})
is given by
\mathcal{A}=\mu\lambda^{-1}_{I}(\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{f})
for a function
\mathfrak{f}\in C^{\infty}(S^{2})
.

•

\mathcal{H}\in C^{\infty}(S^{2})
is given by
\lambda_{I}^{2}\mathcal{H}=-(R^{-2}\psi^{2}+\mathfrak{c})
for a real constant
\mathfrak{c}
.

•

The cosmological constant
\Lambda
is determined by
2\Lambda=-\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}
and
\phi
is constant.

In particular, the aforementioned Poisson equation for
\mathcal{H}
is guaranteed to have a solution.

Proof.

Let
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
be a stationary configuration of Freedman’s gauged supergravity on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times X
. By Proposition
7.13
, the quasi-supersymmetry conditions reduce to
\lambda_{R}=0
, hence
\phi
is constant, and
a
being a Yang-Mills connection on
P
with normalization:

F_{a}=\mu\lambda_{I}\nu_{h}.

Equivalently, the Chern number
c(P)
of
P
satisfies:

c(P)=\frac{\mu\chi(X)}{\lambda_{I}\mathfrak{e}^{2}},

where
\chi(X)
is the Euler characteristic of
X
. These conditions, together with Proposition
7.18
, give the necessary and sufficient conditions for the tuple
(h,a,\mathcal{A},\psi,\phi,\mathcal{H})
being a stationary quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity equations (
16
). By condition
s_{h}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}
in Proposition
7.18
it immediately follows that
(X,h)
is the round sphere of radius
R:=|\mathfrak{e}\lambda_{I}|^{-1}
since
h
is complete by definition. Hence
\chi(X)=2
. On the other hand, the equation
\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=\mu\lambda_{I}F_{\mathcal{A}}
in Proposition
7.18
can be explicitly integrated by setting:

\mathcal{A}=\mu\lambda^{-1}_{I}(\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{f})

for a smooth function
\mathfrak{f}\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Substituting in the remaining conditions contained in Proposition
7.18
, and setting
2\Lambda:=-\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}
, we obtain:

\lambda_{I}^{-2}|\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda^{2}_{I}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi

as the remaining system that needs to be solved. We observe that the second equation above can be rewritten as follows:

\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=(\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}+\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}^{h\ast})\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=\Delta_{h}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda^{2}_{I}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi=\frac{2}{R^{2}}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi,

where
\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}
is the formal adjoint of the exterior derivative,
R
is the radius of
(S^{2},h)
, and
\Delta_{h}
is the positive Laplace operator on
(S^{2},h)
. Hence, we conclude that
\psi
is, modulo a constant, a linear combination of the three linearly independent eigenfunctions of the Laplacian with the lowest eigenvalue. Using standard notation for the spherical harmonics
Y^{m}_{l}
on
(S^{2},h)
, we have:

\psi=\sum_{m=-1}^{1}Y_{1}^{m}\,c_{m}

for constants
c_{1},c_{0},c_{-1}
. It remains to solve:

\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}-\lambda_{I}^{-2}|\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}.

Integrating, we obtain:

\int_{S^{2}}|\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}\nu_{h}=\int_{S^{2}}\langle\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\psi,\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\psi\rangle_{h}=\int_{S^{2}}\langle\mathrm{d}\psi,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\psi\rangle_{h}=\int_{S^{2}}\langle\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi,\Delta_{h}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi\rangle_{h}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lambda_{I}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|_{h}^{2}.

Hence:

\int_{S^{2}}\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\nu_{h}=\int_{S^{2}}(2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}-\lambda_{I}^{-2}|\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h})\nu_{h}=\int_{S^{2}}(2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}-2\mathfrak{e}^{2}|\mathrm{d}\psi|_{h}^{2})\nu_{h}=0

and thus, crucially, the standard theory of elliptic operators implies the existence of a solution
\mathcal{H}
that is unique up to an additive constant. Furthermore, we have:

\lambda_{I}^{-2}|\mathrm{d}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}=\lambda_{I}^{-2}(\Delta_{h}\psi)^{2}=\frac{4}{R^{4}\lambda^{2}_{I}}\psi^{2}.

Hence, the Poisson equation for
\psi
reduces to:

\lambda^{2}_{I}\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=\frac{2}{R^{2}}|\mathrm{d}\psi|^{2}_{h}-\frac{4}{R^{4}}\psi^{2}.

Now, we observe that the following choice:

\lambda_{I}^{2}\mathcal{H}=-(R^{-2}\psi^{2}+\mathfrak{c}),

where
\mathfrak{c}
is a real constant, is a solution of the Poisson equation for
\mathcal{H}
. Since solutions to such an equation are unique up to an additive constant, this is the general solution.
∎

After a change of coordinates in the
(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})
-plane to absorb the factor
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{f}
and the constants
\lambda_{I}
and
\mu
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 7.20
.

Every standard stationary quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity is isometric to a Lorentzian four-manifold
(M,g)
of the form:

(24)

M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{2}\,,\qquad g=-(R^{-2}\psi^{2}+\mathfrak{c})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot(\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi)+h\,,

where
(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})
are the Cartesian coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
h
is the round metric on
S^{2}
of radius
R
,
\mathfrak{c}
is a real constant, and
\psi
is a first eigenfunction of the Laplacian on
(S^{2},h)
, that is,
\Delta_{h}\psi=2R^{-2}\psi
.

In local standard angular coordinates
(\theta,\phi)
on the sphere, the function
\psi
can be written as follows:

\psi(\theta,\phi)=c_{1}\sin(\theta)\cos(\phi)+c_{2}\sin(\theta)\sin(\phi)+c_{3}\cos(\theta)

for constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
. Plugging this expression in Equation (
24
), we obtain its complete expression in local coordinates. We also find that standard stationary quasi-supersymmetric solutions of Freedman’s gauged supergravity are very well-behaved causally.

Corollary 7.21
.

Every standard stationary quasi-supersymmetric solution of Freedman’s gauged supergravity is geodesically complete and globally hyperbolic.

Proof.

Geodesic completeness follows from
[
7
,
49
]
, after observing that
\psi
and its gradient are both bounded on
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{2}
, and hence have sublinear behavior, satisfying thus the conditions for completeness obtained in
opere citato
. To establish the global hyperbolicity of the space-time (
24
), we apply the general theory developed in
[
15
]
together with an isocausal metric comparison argument to bound the
gyratonic
twist term. Let
\mathcal{A}=\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}\psi
denote the twist one-form on
S^{2}
. For any constant
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define a twist-free comparison metric
g_{co}
:

g_{co}:=(\mathcal{H}-\frac{1}{\delta}|\mathcal{A}|^{2}_{h})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+(1-\delta)h.

Evaluating the difference
g-g_{co}
on an arbitrary tangent vector
w=w^{\mathfrak{u}}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}+w^{\mathfrak{v}}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}+w^{\perp}
, we obtain:

(g-g_{co})(w,w)=\frac{1}{\delta}|\mathcal{A}|^{2}_{h}(w^{\mathfrak{u}})^{2}+2w^{\mathfrak{u}}\mathcal{A}(w^{\perp})+\delta|w^{\perp}|^{2}_{h}=\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}w^{\mathfrak{u}}\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{\delta}w^{\perp}\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0.

Thus,
g\geq g_{co}
everywhere on
M
. The comparison metric
g_{co}
represents a standard generalized plane-fronted wave om
\mathbb{R}\times S^{2}
as defined in
[
15
]
. Because the transverse manifold
(S^{2},h)
is compact, the effective profile function
\mathcal{H}_{co}=\mathcal{H}-\frac{1}{\delta}|\mathcal{A}|^{2}_{h}
is globally bounded, satisfying the conditions of
[
15
]
and ensuring
g_{co}
is globally hyperbolic on
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{2}
. The algebraic bound
g\geq g_{co}
implies that for any
g
-causal vector
w\in TM
, we necessarily have
g_{co}(w,w)\leq g(w,w)\leq 0
. The light cones of
g
are therefore contained within the light cones of
g_{co}
. Since
g_{co}
admits a global Cauchy hypersurface, this spatial slice is intersected exactly once by any inextendible causal curve of
g_{co}
, and by causal containment, exactly once by any inextendible causal curve of
g
. Thus,
(M,g)
inherits strict global hyperbolicity.
∎

Remark 7.22
.

Imposing strict supersymmetry requires the existence of an isomorphism between
P
and a certain power of the unit bundle in
TS^{2}
. The quasi-supersymmetric solutions of the theory will, in general, not satisfy this condition and therefore will be strictly non-supersymmetric.

The class of solutions obtained in Theorem
7.19
gives a vast generalization of the solution found by Meessen and Ort
\́mathbf{i}
n in
[
40
]
, which inspired this section.

8.
Skew-torsion parallel spinors on Lorentzian six-manifolds

In this section, we characterize Lorentzian six-dimensional manifolds equipped with an irreducible complex and chiral spinor parallel under a metric connection with totally skew-symmetric torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
, with the goal of proving a structural result regarding their foliated structure. We will refer to such spinors as
skew-torsion parallel spinors
, and we will denote a metric connection on
(M,g)
with totally skew-symmetric torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
by
\nabla^{g,H}
. Hence, a skew-torsion parallel spinor on
(M,g)
is an irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
satisfying:

\nabla^{g,H}\eta=0,

where we are denoting by the same symbol the lift of
\nabla^{g,H}
to
S^{\mu}
. Since every metric connection
\nabla^{g,H}
with totally skew-symmetric torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
can be expanded as:

\nabla^{g,H}_{w_{1}}w_{2}=\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}w_{2}+\frac{1}{2}H(w_{1},w_{2})^{\sharp_{g}}\,,\qquad w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(TM),

it follows that equation
\nabla^{g,H}\eta=0
is equivalent to
\nabla^{g}_{w}\eta=\mathcal{A}_{w}(\eta)
with:

\mathcal{A}_{w}=-\frac{1}{4}\Psi_{\gamma}(H(w))\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))\,,\qquad w\in\Gamma(TM),

whose symbol is given by
4\mathfrak{a}_{w}=-H(w)\in\Omega^{2}(M)
. This identification will be important later in this section to apply Theorems
4.6
and
5.6
.

In Section
9
we will apply the results of this section to the study of the quasi-supersymmetric and supersymmetric solutions of six-dimensional minimal supergravity.

8.1.
Algebraic squares in six dimensions

In this subsection, we compute the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of an irreducible complex chiral spinor in six Lorentzian dimensions. The algebraic equations characterizing the Hermitian and complex-bilinear square of such spinor were obtained in
[
23
, Prop. 7.4]
and
[
23
, Cor. 7.8]
, respectively. We state the global characterization in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
be an irreducible complex chiral spinor with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on an oriented Lorentzian six-manifold
(M,g)
. Then:

•

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu*_{g}u

for a unique nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
and a two-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
satisfying:

*_{g}(u\wedge\omega)=\mu u\wedge\omega,\qquad\omega(u^{\sharp_{g}})=0,\qquad\langle\omega,\omega\rangle_{g}=2,\qquad 2\mu*_{g}u=u\wedge\omega\wedge\omega.

•

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
if and only if it is a decomposable
\mu
-self-dual three-form. That is, around every point in
M
we have:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3},\qquad*_{g}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=\mu\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}

in terms of three isotropic and orthogonal complex local one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
.

The two-form
\omega
in the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu*_{g}u
is determined up to transformations of the form
\omega\mapsto\omega+u\wedge\theta
with
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
orthogonal to
u
. Given an isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
we choose a
conjugate
one-form
v\in\Omega^{1}(M)
, which by definition is isotropic and satisfies
\langle u,v\rangle_{g}=1
. Note that the existence of
v
is guaranteed since
(M,g)
is assumed to be oriented and time-oriented. For each choice of one-form
v
conjugate to
u
, we have a canonical codimension-two distribution in
TM
defined by:

D_{uv}:=\mathrm{Ker}(u)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(v)=(\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle)^{\perp_{g}}\subset TM.

We will occasionally refer to
D_{uv}
as the
transverse
distribution. In terms of
D_{uv}\subset TM
, the tangent bundle splits orthogonally as follows:

TM=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle\oplus D_{uv}

in terms of which the metric
g
splits accordingly:

g=u\odot v+h_{uv}\,,\qquad h_{uv}=g|_{D_{uv}}.

Note that
h_{uv}
is a positive-definite metric on
D_{uv}
. Given
u
and a conjugate one-form
v
, we may choose the unique representative for
\omega
satisfying
\omega(v^{\sharp_{g}})=0
, which we denote by
\omega_{uv}
. With this choice, we have the following identities:

(25)

\begin{gathered}*_{g}u=\frac{\mu}{2}u\wedge\omega_{uv}\wedge\omega_{uv},\qquad*_{g}v=-\frac{\mu}{2}v\wedge\omega_{uv}\wedge\omega_{uv},\qquad*_{g}(u\wedge v)=\frac{\mu}{2}\omega_{uv}\wedge\omega_{uv},\\
*_{g}\omega_{uv}=-\mu u\wedge v\wedge\omega_{uv},\qquad*_{g}(u\wedge\omega_{uv})=\mu u\wedge\omega_{uv},\qquad*_{g}(v\wedge\omega_{uv})=-\mu v\wedge\omega_{uv}.\end{gathered}

Since
\omega_{uv}(u^{\sharp_{g}})=\omega_{uv}(v^{\sharp_{g}})=0
, it follows that
\omega_{uv}
descends to
D_{uv}
, and hence we can consider it as a section of the form
\omega_{uv}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}D^{\ast}_{uv})
, where
D_{uv}^{\ast}
denotes the vector bundle dual to
D_{uv}
. One-forms conjugate to a given isotropic one-form
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
are not unique. Indeed, if
v,\tilde{v}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
are both conjugate to
u
, then there exists a unique
\mathfrak{w}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
orthogonal to
u
and
v
such that:

\tilde{v}=v-\frac{|\mathfrak{w}|^{2}_{g}}{2}u+\mathfrak{w}\,.

The transverse distribution changes accordingly via the following isomorphism of vector bundles:

D_{uv}\to D_{u\tilde{v}}\,,\qquad w\mapsto w-\mathfrak{w}(w)u.

In particular, we have the following isomorphisms of vector bundles:

\mathrm{Ker}(u)=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle\oplus D_{uv}=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle\oplus D_{u\tilde{v}}.

The two-form
\omega_{uv}
also changes accordingly:

\omega_{u\tilde{v}}=\omega_{uv}-u\wedge\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}}),

where we have used that
\omega_{u\tilde{v}}(\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}})=0
. We induce a natural orientation on
D_{uv}
by defining a volume form
\nu_{uv}
on
D_{uv}
via the relation
\nu_{g}=u\wedge v\wedge\nu_{uv}
, where
\nu_{g}
is the Lorentzian volume form on
(M,g)
. In turn,
\nu_{uv}
defines a Hodge star operator
*_{uv}
on
D_{uv}
. In particular, it follows that:

\ast_{uv}\omega_{uv}=-\mu\omega_{uv}.

Since
\omega_{uv}
is a non-degenerate two-form on
D_{uv}
, which is also equipped with a Euclidean metric
h_{uv}
, there exists a unique almost complex structure
J_{uv}\colon D_{uv}\to D_{uv}
on
D_{uv}
such that:

h_{uv}(w_{1},w_{2})=\omega_{uv}(w_{1},J_{uv}w_{2}).

Furthermore, by the duality relation satisfied by
\omega_{uv}
, it follows that
(\omega_{uv},J_{uv})
defines an almost Hermitian structure on
D_{uv}
, compatible with the given orientation if
\mu=-1
, and opposite to the given orientation if
\mu=1
. Equivalently, we can consider the pair
(\omega_{uv},J_{uv})
as defining a
\mathrm{U}(2)
-structure on
D_{uv}
. In particular, the six-dimensional Lorentzian metric
g
can be written as follows:

g=u\odot v+\omega_{uv}\circ J_{uv},

and therefore the metric
g
is completely determined by
u,v,\omega_{uv}
and
J_{uv}
. As expected, changing the conjugate one-form
v
changes
J_{uv}
accordingly.

Lemma 8.2
.

The following relation holds:

J_{u\tilde{v}}=J_{uv}-u\otimes J_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})\otimes u^{\sharp_{g}}

for
\mathfrak{w}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
orthogonal to
u
and
v
.

Proof.

To prove that the given endomorphism
J_{u\tilde{v}}
is the correct almost complex structure on the new transverse distribution
D_{u\tilde{v}}
, we must verify that it annihilates the null vectors
u^{\sharp_{g}}
and
\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}}
, preserves the transverse space
D_{u\tilde{v}}=\mathrm{Ker}(u)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\tilde{v})
, and satisfies the metric compatibility condition
g=u\odot\tilde{v}+\omega_{u\tilde{v}}(-,J_{u\tilde{v}}-)
. By evaluating the proposed formula for
J_{u\tilde{v}}
on
u^{\sharp_{g}}
, we immediately get:

J_{u\tilde{v}}(u^{\sharp_{g}})=J_{uv}(u^{\sharp_{g}})-u(u^{\sharp_{g}})J_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},u^{\sharp_{g}})u^{\sharp_{g}}=0.

Next, we evaluate
J_{u\tilde{v}}
on the new conjugate one-form
\tilde{v}=v-\frac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{w}|_{g}^{2}u+\mathfrak{w}
:

J_{u\tilde{v}}(\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}})=J_{uv}(\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}})-u(\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}})J_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},\tilde{v}^{\sharp_{g}})u^{\sharp_{g}}=J_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})-J_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})=0.

For any vector field
w\in D_{u\tilde{v}}
, we have
u(w)=0
and
\tilde{v}(w)=0
. Applying
J_{u\tilde{v}}
yields:

J_{u\tilde{v}}(w)=J_{uv}(w)+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},w)u^{\sharp_{g}}.

We must check that
J_{u\tilde{v}}(w)\in D_{u\tilde{v}}
for every
w\in D_{u\tilde{v}}
. We have:

J_{uv}(w)=J_{uv}(w-\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}}(w)u^{\sharp_{g}})\in D_{uv}\subset\mathrm{Ker}(u).

We compute:

\displaystyle\tilde{v}(J_{u\tilde{v}}(w))

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}(J_{uv}(w))+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},w)\tilde{v}(u^{\sharp_{g}})

\displaystyle=h_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},J_{uv}(w-\mathfrak{w}(w)u^{\sharp_{g}}))+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},w)

\displaystyle=-\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},w)+\omega_{uv}(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}},w)

\displaystyle=0.

Hence
J_{u\tilde{v}}(w)\in\mathrm{Ker}(u)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\tilde{v})=D_{u\tilde{v}}
.
∎

Given two tuples
(u,v,\omega_{uv},J_{uv})
and
(u,\tilde{v},\omega_{u\tilde{v}},J_{u\tilde{v}})
associated to the same chiral complex spinor
\eta
, one can check that:

g=u\odot v+\omega_{uv}\circ J_{uv}=u\odot\tilde{v}+\omega_{u\tilde{v}}\circ J_{u\tilde{v}}

as required by the internal consistency of the construction. The previous discussion shows that every pair
(g,\eta)
consisting of a Lorentzian metric
g
on
M
and an irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
defines a tuple
(u,v,\omega_{uv},J_{uv})
as defined above, which is unique modulo the choice of conjugate one-form
v
. Hence, the pair
(g,\eta)
defines a natural equivalence class
[u,v,\omega,J]
of tuples
(u,v,\omega,J)
consisting of an isotropic vector field
u
, a conjugate one-form
v
, and a
\mathrm{U}(2)
-structure
(\omega,J)
on
D_{uv}
compatible with the induced orientation if
\mu=-1
and opposite otherwise. Two such tuples
(u,v,\omega,J)
and
(\tilde{u},\tilde{v},\tilde{\omega},\tilde{J})
are considered as equivalent if and only if:

(26)

(\tilde{u},\tilde{v},\tilde{\omega},\tilde{J})=(u,v-\frac{|\mathfrak{w}|_{g}^{2}}{2}u+\mathfrak{w},\omega-u\wedge\omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}}),J-u\otimes J(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})+\omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})\otimes u^{\sharp_{g}})

for a unique section
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
of the transverse distribution
D_{uv}\subset TM
. We will refer to such a tuple
[u,v,\omega,J]
as an
isotropic almost Hermitian class
. They represent a natural generalization of the notion of isotropic parallelism, introduced in
[
51
]
, from four to six dimensions. Note that there is a one-to-one correspondence between isotropic almost Hermitian classes and pairs
(g,\widehat{\alpha})
, where
g
is a Lorentzian metric on
M
and
\widehat{\alpha}
is the Hermitian square of an irreducible complex and chiral spinor. Hence, the Hermitian square of such a spinor can be equivalently studied in terms of the notion of isotropic almost Hermitian class.

8.2.
The Hermitian square of a skew-torsion parallel spinor

In this subsection, we study the differential system satisfied, via Theorem
4.6
, by the Hermitian square of every skew-torsion parallel spinor. This will provide a set of necessary conditions for a Lorentzian six-manifold
(M,g)
to admit a skew-torsion parallel spinor that we will interpret in terms of an associated foliation of
M
.

Lemma 8.3
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the Hermitian square of a skew-torsion parallel spinor with torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
if and only if it satisfies the following differential system:

(27)

\nabla^{g}_{w}\widehat{\alpha}=\mathfrak{a}_{w}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}+\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{w})\,,\qquad 4\mathfrak{a}_{w}=-H(w)\in\Omega^{2}(M)\,,\qquad w\in\Gamma(TM)\,,

where
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast_{g}u
is the Hermitian square of a chiral complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
4.8
after noticing that the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}
is computed with respect to a Hermitian pairing of positive adjoint type.
∎

The goal now is to expand Equation (
27
) into its components, which can be studied and understood geometrically.

Lemma 8.4
.

The Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast_{g}u
of an irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
satisfies Equation (
27
) if and only if:

\nabla^{g}_{w}u=\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}}),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega=\frac{1}{2}H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega+\kappa_{w}\wedge u

for a certain
\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(M,T^{*}M)
such that
\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}=0
for all
w\in\Gamma(TM)
.

Proof.

We expand Equation (
27
). We have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{w}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}_{w}\diamond(u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu u\diamond\nu_{g})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge u-\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})+\mu*_{g}(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge u)+\mu*_{g}(\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}}))

\displaystyle\quad+i(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge u\wedge\omega-\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\omega)-\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{2}(u\wedge\omega)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}_{w})

\displaystyle=(u+iu\wedge\omega+\mu\nu_{g}\diamond u)\diamond(-\mathfrak{a}_{w})

\displaystyle=-u\wedge\mathfrak{a}_{w}-\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})-\mu*_{g}(u\wedge\mathfrak{a}_{w})+\mu*_{g}(\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}}))

\displaystyle\quad-i(u\wedge\omega\wedge\mathfrak{a}_{w}+(u\wedge\omega)\triangle_{1}\mathfrak{a}_{w}-(u\wedge\omega)\triangle_{2}\mathfrak{a}_{w}),

where we have used that
*_{g}u=u\diamond\nu_{g}=-\nu_{g}\diamond u
. Separating by degrees this equation we obtain:

\nabla^{g}_{w}u=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}}),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{w}(u\wedge\omega)=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\omega)

for all
w\in\Gamma(TM)
. Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{6}\}
be a frame of
M
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\omega)

\displaystyle=g^{ij}\iota_{e_{i}}\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge\iota_{e_{j}}(u\wedge\omega)

\displaystyle=g^{ij}\iota_{e_{i}}\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge(\iota_{e_{j}}u\wedge\omega-u\wedge\iota_{e_{j}}\omega)

\displaystyle=(g^{ij}\iota_{e_{i}}\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge\iota_{e_{j}}u)\wedge\omega+u\wedge(g^{ij}\iota_{e_{i}}\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge\iota_{e_{j}}\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\omega+u\wedge(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega),

where we have used
\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}u=\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})
. Thus
\nabla^{g}_{w}(u\wedge\omega)=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\omega-2u\wedge(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega)
, which implies:

u\wedge\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega=-2u\wedge(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega)

since
\nabla^{g}_{w}u=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})
. Hence:

\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega+\kappa_{w}\wedge u

for some
\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(M,T^{*}M)
. Substituting
\mathfrak{a}_{w}=-\frac{1}{4}H(w)
yields the equations from the statement. To get the condition
\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}=0
for all
w\in\Gamma(TM)
, we differentiate
\omega(u^{\sharp_{g}})=0
. Then we get:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega)(u^{\sharp_{g}})+\omega((\nabla^{g}_{w}u)^{\sharp_{g}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iota_{u^{\sharp_{g}}}(H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega)+\iota_{u^{\sharp_{g}}}(\kappa_{w}\wedge u)+\frac{1}{2}\omega\triangle_{1}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\omega+\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}u+\frac{1}{2}\omega\triangle_{1}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}})

\displaystyle=\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}u,

which implies that
\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}=0
since
u
is nowhere vanishing.
∎

As we explained in the previous subsection, pairs
(g,\widehat{\alpha})
are in one-to-one correspondence with isotropic almost Hermitian classes
[u,v,\omega,J]
.

Proposition 8.5
.

A Lorentzian six-dimensional manifold
(M,g)
admits a skew-torsion parallel spinor with torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
only if it admits an isotropic almost Hermitian class
[u,v,\omega,J]
satisfying:

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{w}u=\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}}),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{w}v=\frac{1}{2}H(w,v^{\sharp_{g}})-\kappa_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega=\frac{1}{2}H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega+\kappa_{w}\wedge u,\qquad\nabla^{g}_{w}J=\frac{1}{2}(H(w)\circ J-J\circ H(w))+\kappa_{w}\otimes u^{\sharp_{g}}-u\otimes\kappa_{w}^{\sharp_{g}}

for all
w\in\Gamma(TM)
and some
\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(M,T^{*}M)
such that
\langle\kappa_{w},u\rangle_{g}=0
.

Proof.

We have computed
\nabla^{g}_{w}u
and
\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega
in Lemma
8.4
. It remains to compute
\nabla^{g}_{w}v
and
\nabla^{g}_{w}J
. First note that from
\langle v,v\rangle_{g}=0
we obtain
\langle\nabla_{w}^{g}v,v\rangle_{g}=0
, that is,
\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(\nabla^{g}_{w}v)=0
. Set
2T_{w}:=H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
to simplify the notation. We will use equations (
25
) repeatedly. Using the relation
2u\wedge v=-\mu\ast_{g}(\omega\wedge\omega)
we compute on one hand:

\nabla^{g}_{w}(u\wedge v)=\nabla^{g}_{w}u\wedge v+u\wedge\nabla^{g}_{w}v=\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge v+u\wedge\nabla^{g}_{w}v.

Taking the interior product with
v^{\sharp_{g}}
yields:

\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(\nabla^{g}_{w}(u\wedge v))=\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}},v^{\sharp_{g}})v+\nabla^{g}_{w}v.

On the other hand:

\nabla^{g}_{w}(-\frac{\mu}{2}*_{g}(\omega\wedge\omega))=-\mu*(\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega\wedge\omega)=-\mu*_{g}(T_{w}\wedge\omega+\kappa_{w}\wedge u\wedge\omega).

Taking the interior product with
v^{\sharp_{g}}
yields:

\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(\nabla^{g}_{w}(-\frac{\mu}{2}*_{g}(\omega\wedge\omega)))=-\mu*_{g}(T_{w}\wedge\omega\wedge v+\kappa_{w}\wedge u\wedge\omega\wedge v).

Using
*_{g}(v\wedge\omega)=-\mu v\wedge\omega
and Proposition
A.1
we obtain:

-\mu*_{g}(T_{w}\wedge\omega\wedge v)=-\mu*_{g}(v\wedge\omega\wedge T_{w})=-\mu T_{w}\triangle_{2}(*_{g}(v\wedge\omega))=T_{w}\triangle_{2}(v\wedge\omega).

A computation using the definition of
\triangle_{2}
and the fact that
\omega(v^{\sharp_{g}})=0
gives us:

T_{w}\triangle_{2}(v\wedge\omega)=\langle T_{w},\omega\rangle v+T_{w}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\omega.

Note also that
T_{w}(v^{\sharp_{g}})=\frac{1}{2}\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega)=-\frac{1}{2}H(w,v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\omega
. Now we need to compute two identities:

•

Let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Then
\langle\theta_{1}\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta_{2}\rangle=-\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle
. Indeed:

\langle\theta_{1}\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta_{2}\rangle=\langle\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp_{g}}),\theta_{2}\rangle=\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp_{g}},\theta_{2}^{\sharp_{g}})=-\omega(\theta_{2}^{\sharp_{g}},\theta_{1}^{\sharp_{g}})=-\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle.

•

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
. Then
\langle\iota_{w_{1}}\omega,\iota_{w_{2}}\omega\rangle=g(w_{1},w_{2})
. Indeed, using that the exterior product is adjoint to the interior product,
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2
, and
*_{uv}\omega=-\mu\omega
on
D_{uv}
we have:

\displaystyle\langle\iota_{w_{1}}\omega,\iota_{w_{2}}\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega\wedge w_{1}^{\flat},\omega\wedge w_{2}^{\flat}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega,\iota_{w_{1}}\omega\wedge w_{2}^{\flat}\rangle+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle g(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=-\langle\iota_{w_{1}}\omega,\iota_{w_{2}}\omega\rangle+2g(w_{1},w_{2}).

A one-form
\beta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
can be decomposed as
\beta=\beta(v^{\sharp_{g}})u+\beta(u^{\sharp_{g}})v+\beta^{\perp}
, where
\beta^{\perp}(u^{\sharp_{g}})=\beta^{\perp}(v^{\sharp_{g}})=0
, i.e.
\beta^{\perp}\in\Gamma(D_{uv}^{*})
. Thus
\beta\triangle_{1}\omega=\beta^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega
. Using the two identities above we compute:

\displaystyle\langle(\beta\triangle_{1}\omega)\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\beta^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega)\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\beta^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\beta^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega,\theta^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\beta^{\perp},\theta^{\perp}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\beta^{\perp},\theta\rangle

for all
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Hence:

(\beta\triangle_{1}\omega)\triangle_{1}\omega=-\beta^{\perp}=-\beta+\beta(v^{\sharp_{g}})u+\beta(u^{\sharp_{g}})v.

In our situation we have:

-\frac{1}{2}(H(w,v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\omega)\triangle_{1}\omega=\frac{1}{2}H(w,v^{\sharp_{g}})-\frac{1}{2}H(w,v^{\sharp_{g}},u^{\sharp_{g}})v.

One can show that
\langle\sigma_{1}\triangle_{1}\omega,\sigma_{2}\rangle=-\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle
for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
. Using this and
\omega\triangle_{1}\omega=0
we get:

\langle T_{w},\omega\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle H(w)\triangle_{1}\omega,\omega\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle H(w),\omega\triangle_{1}\omega\rangle=0.

Finally, using
*_{g}(u\wedge v\wedge\omega)=\mu\omega
and Proposition
A.1
we obtain:

-\mu*_{g}(\kappa_{w}\wedge u\wedge v\wedge\omega)=-\mu*_{g}(u\wedge v\wedge\omega\wedge\kappa_{w})=-\mu\kappa_{w}\triangle_{1}(*_{g}(u\wedge v\wedge\omega))=-\kappa_{w}\triangle_{1}\omega.

Putting everything together, we obtain the expression for
\nabla^{g}_{w}v
from the statement. Finally, the expression for
\nabla^{g}_{w}J
is obtained from that of
\nabla^{g}_{w}\omega
and the relation
\omega=g(J-,-)
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

We note that if a tuple
(u,v,\omega,J)
satisfies the equations of Proposition
8.5
for a certain
\kappa\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M\otimes T^{\ast}M
), then another tuple
(u,\tilde{v},\tilde{\omega},\tilde{J})
as in Equation (
26
) satisfies the same equations for:

\tilde{\kappa}_{w}=\kappa_{w}+\nabla^{g}_{w}(\omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}}))-\frac{1}{2}H(w)\triangle_{1}(\omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})).

Let
\eta
be an irreducible complex and chiral spinor on
(M,g)
, with Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu*_{g}u
. Since
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
is an isotropic one-form, it canonically determines a
screen bundle
over
(M,g)
, that is, a vector bundle
\mathfrak{S}_{u}\to(M,g)
defined by:

\mathfrak{S}_{u}:=\frac{\mathrm{Ker}(u)}{\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp_{g}}\rangle}.

In our case,
\mathfrak{S}_{u}
is of real rank
4
. Since
u
is canonically determined by
\eta
, it follows that
\mathfrak{S}_{u}
is also canonically determined by
\eta
. Note that every choice of one-form
v
conjugate to
u
determines a distribution
D_{uv}\subset TM
that is naturally isomorphic, as a vector bundle, to
\mathfrak{S}_{u}
.

Definition 8.7
.

The screen bundle
\mathfrak{S}_{u}
is
\emph{integrable}
if there exists a one-form
v
conjugate to
u
such that
D_{uv}\subset TM
is Frobenius integrable.

Let us study under which conditions the distribution
D_{uv}=\mathrm{Ker}(u)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(v)\subset TM
is integrable. For that, we use the Frobenius criterion, which says that
D_{uv}
is integrable if and only if:

u\wedge v\wedge\mathrm{d}u=0,\qquad u\wedge v\wedge\mathrm{d}v=0.

To evaluate this criteria, it is convenient to decompose
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
with respect to:

T^{*}M=\langle\mathbb{R}u\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v\rangle\oplus D^{*}_{uv}

as follows:

(28)

H=u\wedge v\wedge\beta+u\wedge\chi_{u}+v\wedge\chi_{v}+H^{\perp}

for uniquely determined sections:

\beta\in\Gamma(D_{uv}^{*}),\qquad\chi_{u},\chi_{v}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}D_{uv}^{*}),\qquad H^{\perp}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{3}D_{uv}^{*}).

Remark 8.8
.

Note that in the decomposition of
H
given in Equation (
28
), it follows that
H
is
\mu
-self-dual if and only if:

*_{uv}\chi_{u}=-\mu\chi_{u},\qquad*_{uv}\chi_{v}=\mu\chi_{v},\qquad*_{uv}H^{\perp}=\mu\beta.

Lemma 8.9
.

The distribution
D_{uv}\subset TM
is integrable if and only if:

\chi_{v}=0,\qquad\chi_{u}(w_{1},w_{2})=-\omega(\kappa_{w_{1}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{2})+\omega(\kappa_{w_{2}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1})

for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.5
, the skew-symmetrization of
\nabla^{g}u
is:

\mathrm{d}u=-H(u^{\sharp_{g}})=u\wedge\beta-\chi_{v}.

Hence
u\wedge v\wedge\mathrm{d}u=0
if and only if
\chi_{v}=0
. We now compute
\mathrm{d}v
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=-H(v^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1},w_{2})-(\kappa_{w_{1}}\triangle_{1}\omega)(w_{2})+(\kappa_{w_{2}}\triangle_{1}\omega)(w_{1})

\displaystyle=-H(v^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1},w_{2})-\omega(\kappa_{w_{1}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{2})+\omega(\kappa_{w_{2}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1})

\displaystyle=-v(w_{1})\beta(w_{2})+v(w_{2})\beta(w_{1})-\chi_{u}(w_{1},w_{2})-\omega(\kappa_{w_{1}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{2})+\omega(\kappa_{w_{2}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1}),

where we have used that
H(v^{\sharp_{g}})=v\wedge\beta+\chi_{u}
. Since
u\wedge v\wedge\mathrm{d}v=0
if and only if
\mathrm{d}v(w_{1},w_{2})=0
for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
, we conclude that:

\chi_{u}(w_{1},w_{2})=-\omega(\kappa_{w_{1}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{2})+\omega(\kappa_{w_{2}}^{\sharp_{g}},w_{1})

for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
.
∎

Using the aforementioned notion of integrable screen bundle together with the previous lemmata, we extract the following structural result.

Theorem 8.10
.

An oriented Lorentzian six-manifold admits a skew-torsion parallel spinor with an integrable screen bundle only if it admits a codimension-two foliation by locally conformally Kähler complex surfaces.

Proof.

Let
\eta
be such a parallel spinor, with associated isotropic almost Hermitian class
[u,v,\omega,J]
. We first show that
\mathrm{d}\omega=\beta\wedge\omega
. Let
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
. Then:

\displaystyle(H(w_{1})\triangle_{1}\omega)(w_{2},w_{3})

\displaystyle=\iota_{w_{3}}\iota_{w_{2}}(H(w_{1})\triangle_{1}\omega)

\displaystyle=\iota_{w_{3}}(-H(w_{1},w_{2})\triangle_{1}\omega+H(w_{1})\triangle_{1}\omega(w_{2}))

\displaystyle=H(w_{1},w_{2})\triangle_{1}\omega(w_{3})-H(w_{1},w_{3})\triangle_{1}\omega(w_{2})

\displaystyle=H(w_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3}),

where we have used
\omega(w)^{\sharp_{g}}=Jw
. Hence:

(\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}\omega)(w_{2},w_{3})=\frac{1}{2}(H(w_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3})).

We now compute:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\omega(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}\omega)(w_{2},w_{3})+(\nabla^{g}_{w_{2}}\omega)(w_{3},w_{1})+(\nabla^{g}_{w_{3}}\omega)(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=H(Jw_{1},w_{2},w_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3})+H(w_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3}).

Using that
*_{uv}\omega=-\mu\omega
,
*_{uv}\beta=-\mu H^{\perp}
, and
\nu_{uv}\diamond\nu_{uv}=1
on
D_{uv}
, we compute:

H^{\perp}\diamond\omega=(-\mu*_{uv}\beta)\diamond(-\mu*_{uv}\omega)=(\beta\diamond\nu_{uv})\diamond(-\nu_{uv}\diamond\omega)=-\beta\diamond\omega.

In particular,
H^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega=-\beta\wedge\omega
. Then:

-(\beta\wedge\omega)(w_{1})=\iota_{w_{1}}(H^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega)=-H^{\perp}(w_{1})\triangle_{1}\omega-H^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega(w_{1})

and:

\displaystyle(H^{\perp}(w_{1})\triangle_{1}\omega)(w_{2},w_{3})

\displaystyle=H(w_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3}),

\displaystyle(H^{\perp}\triangle_{1}\omega(w_{1}))(w_{2},w_{3})

\displaystyle=H(Jw_{1},w_{2},w_{3})

for all
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
. Hence, we conclude that
\mathrm{d}\omega=\beta\wedge\omega
on
D_{uv}
.

Now we show that
\beta
is closed. From
\mathrm{d}\omega=\beta\wedge\omega
we obtain
\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge\omega=0
. Moreover, from the proof of Lemma
8.9
we get
\mathrm{d}u=-H(u^{\sharp_{g}})=u\wedge\beta
, which in turn implies
\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge u=0
. Write:

\mathrm{d}\beta=\gamma_{0}u\wedge v+u\wedge\gamma_{u}+v\wedge\gamma_{v}+\gamma^{\perp},

where
\gamma_{0}
is a function,
\gamma_{u},\gamma_{v}\in\Gamma(D_{uv}^{*})
and
\gamma^{\perp}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}D_{uv}^{*})
. The equation
\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge u=0
implies that
\gamma_{v}=0
and
\gamma^{\perp}=0
. The equation
\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge\omega=0
implies that
\gamma_{0}=0
and
\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega=0
. Since
\omega
is non-degenerate on
D_{uv}
, there exists
w\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
such that
\gamma_{u}=\iota_{w}\omega
. Hence:

0=\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega=\iota_{w}\omega\wedge\omega=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota_{w}(\omega\wedge\omega)=-\mu\iota_{w}\nu_{uv}=-\mu*_{uv}w^{\flat}

if and only if
w=0
, i.e.
\gamma_{u}=0
. Therefore
\mathrm{d}\beta=0
.

Finally, we show that
J
is integrable. From
\omega=g(J-,-)
and the equation for
(\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}\omega)(w_{2},w_{3})
we get:

g((\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}J)w_{2},w_{3})=\frac{1}{2}(H(w_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3}))

for all
w_{1},w_{2},w_{2}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
. Using the standard formula for the Nijenhuis tensor
N_{J}
in terms of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
, namely:

N_{J}(w_{1},w_{2})=(\nabla^{g}_{w_{1}}J)Jw_{2}-(\nabla^{g}_{w_{2}}J)Jw_{1}+(\nabla^{g}_{Jw_{1}}J)w_{2}-(\nabla^{g}_{Jw_{2}}J)w_{1},

we obtain:

g(N_{J}(w_{1},w_{2}),w_{3})=H(Jw_{1},Jw_{2},w_{3})+H(Jw_{1},w_{2},Jw_{3})+H(w_{1},Jw_{2},Jw_{3})-H(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}).

Now note that:

\omega(\beta^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\omega=\tfrac{1}{2}\iota_{\beta^{\sharp_{g}}}(\omega\wedge\omega)=-\mu*_{uv}\beta=H^{\perp}.

Using
\omega(\beta^{\sharp_{g}},w)=g(J\beta^{\sharp_{g}},w)=-g(\beta^{\sharp_{g}},Jw)=-\beta(Jw)
we compute:

H(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=H^{\perp}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=-\beta(Jw_{1})\omega(w_{2},w_{3})-\beta(Jw_{2})\omega(w_{3},w_{1})-\beta(Jw_{3})\omega(w_{1},w_{2})

for all
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\in\Gamma(D_{uv})
. Plugging this into
g(N_{J}(w_{1},w_{2}),w_{3})
we obtain that
N_{J}=0
on
D_{uv}
. Hence, the almost complex structure
J
on
D_{uv}
is integrable.
∎

8.3.
The complex-bilinear square of a skew-torsion parallel spinor

In this subsection, we compute, via Theorem
5.6
with
4\mathfrak{a}_{w}=-H(w)\in\Omega^{2}(M)
, the necessary and sufficient conditions for a Lorentzian six-dimensional manifold
(M,g)
to admit a skew-torsion parallel spinor with totally skew-symmetric torsion
H\in\Omega^{3}(M)
. Since we have already obtained the conditions that arise from the Hermitian square via Theorem
4.6
, for internal consistency across sections, it is convenient to first obtain the necessary and sufficient conditions for a Hermitian and complex-bilinear square to be the square of the
same
spinor
\eta
.

Lemma 8.11
.

The Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and complex-bilinear
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
squares are the squares of the same irreducible complex chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

u=\frac{\mu}{4}*_{g}(\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha}),\qquad\omega=-\frac{i}{4}\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\overline{\alpha}(v^{\sharp_{g}}),

where
v
is a one-form conjugate to
u
.

Proof.

In order to compute the Hermitian and the complex-bilinear squares of
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
, we needed to equip the bundle of irreducible complex Clifford modules
(S,\gamma)
with a Hermitian pairing
\Scr H
of positive adjoint type, and a skew-symmetric complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
, also of positive adjoint type. These two pairings are related through a quaternionic structure
K\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(S))
, namely
\Scr B(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr H(\eta_{1},K\eta_{2})
for all
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Gamma(S)
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta},\alpha_{K\eta}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
denote the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
, respectively. From Lemma
8.1
and
[
23
, Prop. 3.29]
we have that
\alpha_{K\eta}=\overline{\alpha}_{\eta}
. By
[
23
, Prop. 3.30]
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the same spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{\eta}=8(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha_{\eta},\qquad\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=-8(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond\tau(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Taking
\beta=v
a conjugate one-form of
u
is sufficient since
(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond v)^{(0)}=\langle u,v\rangle_{g}=1
. We compute
\alpha_{\eta}\diamond v\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}
. First of all note that
\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=0
. Indeed:

\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=(\mu*_{g}\alpha_{\eta})\diamond(\mu*_{g}\alpha_{K\eta})=(-\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\nu_{g})\diamond(\nu_{g}\diamond\alpha_{K\eta})=-\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\alpha_{K\eta},
