where we have used
*_{g}\alpha_{\eta}=\mu\alpha_{\eta}
,
*_{g}\alpha_{K\eta}=\mu\alpha_{K\eta}
,
\nu_{g}\diamond\nu_{g}=1
, and Proposition
A.2
. Then:

\alpha_{\eta}\diamond v\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=(2\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})-v\diamond\alpha_{\eta})\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=2(\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\alpha_{K\eta}-\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\alpha_{K\eta}-\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{2}\alpha_{K\eta}).

From
\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=-8(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\diamond\tau(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}
we obtain:

u=\frac{1}{4}\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{2}\alpha_{K\eta},\qquad iu\wedge\omega=\frac{1}{4}\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\alpha_{K\eta},\qquad\mu*_{g}u=\frac{1}{4}\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\alpha_{K\eta}.

The first and last equations are equivalent. Taking the interior product with
v^{\sharp_{g}}
on the second equation gives
i\omega=\frac{1}{4}\alpha_{\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})\triangle_{1}\alpha_{K\eta}(v^{\sharp_{g}})
. Therefore, we obtain the equations from the statement.
∎

As explained in detail in
[
23
]
, the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta
contains
less
information than its complex-bilinear square, which is equivalent to
\eta
modulo a sign. Hence, it is natural to wonder about which extra piece of information we need to add to the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}
of
\eta
to uniquely determine its complex-bilinear square. This is determined in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
be, respectively, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of the same irreducible complex chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then:

\alpha=u\wedge\Omega,\qquad\Omega:=\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(M).

Two complex two-forms
\Omega
and
\tilde{\Omega}
are related if and only if:

\tilde{\Omega}=\Omega-u\wedge\Omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})

for
\mathfrak{w}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
orthogonal to
u
and
v
.

Proof.

Locally, the complex-bilinear square can be written as
\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}
for three isotropic and orthogonal complex local one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. Set
t_{i}:=\langle v,\theta_{i}\rangle_{g}
for
i=1,2,3
. Then:

\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})=\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=t_{1}\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}-t_{2}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{3}+t_{3}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}.

Taking the wedge product with
\overline{\alpha}=\overline{\theta}_{1}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{2}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{3}
yields:

\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha}=t_{1}\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{1}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{2}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{3}-t_{2}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{1}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{2}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{3}+t_{3}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{1}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{2}\wedge\overline{\theta}_{3}.

Hence
*_{g}(\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha})\in\mathrm{span}\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\}
, which implies that
*_{g}(\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha})\wedge\alpha=0
. By Lemma
8.11
we have:

u\wedge\alpha=\frac{\mu}{4}*_{g}(\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha})\wedge\alpha=0,

thus
0=\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(u\wedge\alpha)=\alpha-u\wedge\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})
, which yields the claimed result. Note also that:

\alpha(u^{\sharp_{g}})=\iota_{u^{\sharp_{g}}}\alpha=\mu\iota_{u^{\sharp_{g}}}(*_{g}\alpha)=\mu*_{g}(\alpha\wedge u)=0

since
*_{g}\alpha=\mu\alpha
. Finally, a direct computation shows that if
\tilde{v}=v-\frac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{w}|_{g}^{2}u+\mathfrak{w}
for a unique
\mathfrak{w}\in\Omega^{1}(M)
orthogonal to
u
and
v
, then
\tilde{\Omega}=\Omega-u\wedge\Omega(\mathfrak{w}^{\sharp_{g}})
.
∎

By Lemma
8.12
, we see that the pair
(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha)
canonically determines a class of complex two-forms, and that every one-form
v
conjugate to
u
determines a complex two-form
\Omega
on
D_{uv}
, which in turn defines a complex structure
J_{\Omega}
on
D_{uv}
since
\Omega
is decomposable and satisfies
\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}=2\omega\wedge\omega\neq 0
. Indeed, from Lemma
8.11
and equations (
25
) we obtain:

\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}=\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha}(v^{\sharp_{g}})=\iota_{v^{\sharp_{g}}}(\alpha(v^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\overline{\alpha})=4\mu\iota_{\mathbf{v}^{\sharp_{g}}}(*_{g}u)=4\mu*_{g}(u\wedge v)=2\omega\wedge\omega.

Hence,
\Omega
is a complex two-form of type
(2,0)
with respect to the complex structure
J_{\Omega}
. Using
J_{\Omega}
and the metric
g
we define
\omega_{\Omega}:=g(J_{\Omega}-,-)
. Therefore, the tuple
(\omega_{\Omega},J_{\Omega},\Omega)
defines an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structure on
D_{uv}
.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
(M,g)
be an oriented Lorentzian six-manifold. An irreducible complex chiral spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
satisfies
\nabla^{g,H}\eta=0
if and only if:

\nabla^{g}_{w}u=\frac{1}{2}H(w,u^{\sharp_{g}}),\qquad\nabla^{g}_{w}\Omega=\frac{1}{2}H(w)\triangle_{1}\Omega+\Theta_{w}\wedge u

for all
w\in\Gamma(TM)
and some
\Theta\in\Omega^{1}(M,T_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}M)
.

Proof.

The complex-bilinear square
\alpha
is computed with respect to a complex-bilinear pairing of positive adjoint type. Hence we compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{w}\diamond\alpha

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}_{w}\wedge\alpha-\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\alpha-\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{2}\alpha,

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\tau(\mathfrak{a}_{w})

\displaystyle=-\alpha\wedge\mathfrak{a}_{w}-\alpha\triangle_{1}\mathfrak{a}_{w}+\alpha\triangle_{2}\mathfrak{a}_{w}.

By Corollary
5.8
, the spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
satisfies
\nabla^{g}_{w}\eta=\mathcal{A}_{w}(\eta)
if and only if
\nabla^{g}_{w}\alpha=\mathfrak{a}_{w}\diamond\alpha+\alpha\diamond\tau(\mathfrak{a}_{w})
. This equation gives us:

\nabla^{g}_{w}\alpha=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\alpha.

We now write
\alpha=u\wedge\Omega
as in Lemma
8.12
. A computation gives us:

\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\Omega)=\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})\wedge\Omega+u\wedge(\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\Omega).

Using that
\nabla^{g}_{w}u=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}(u^{\sharp_{g}})
we obtain:

\nabla^{g}_{w}\Omega=-2\mathfrak{a}_{w}\triangle_{1}\Omega+\Theta_{w}\wedge u

for some
\Theta\in\Omega^{1}(M,T_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}M)
. Substituting
\mathfrak{a}_{w}=-\frac{1}{4}H(w)
yields the equations from the statement.
∎

The previous proposition, which guarantees the existence of the complex two-form
\Omega
of type
(2,0)
, can be used to refine Theorem
8.10
in terms of a foliation by locally conformally Kähler surfaces with topologically trivial canonical bundle. This and other associated results on skew-torsion parallel spinors on Lorentzian six-manifolds will be presented in a separate publication.

9.
Supersymmetric self-dual bundle gerbes

In this section, we characterize the quasi-supersymmetric and supersymmetric solutions of minimal supergravity in six dimensions, whose bosonic sector naturally couples a Lorentzian metric on a six-dimensional oriented manifold
M
to a self-dual curving on a bundle gerbe with connective structure defined on
M
.

9.1.
Minimal six-dimensional supergravity

In the following, the triple
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
will denote a bundle gerbe
[
46
]
, with principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle
\mathcal{P}\to Y\times_{M}Y
over the fibered product of a submersion
Y\to M
, equipped with a fixed connective structure
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P})
and defined on the given six-dimensional manifold
M
. Given a curving
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
we denote its curvature by
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M)
. A triple
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
determines a unique minimal six-dimensional bosonic supergravity on
M
, as prescribed in the following definition. For simplicity in the exposition, we will omit the term
bosonic
in the following.

Definition 9.1
.

The
six-dimensional minimal supergravity system
determined by
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
on
M
is the following differential system:

(29)

\mathrm{Ric}^{g}=\frac{1}{2}H_{b}\circ_{g}H_{b}\,,\qquad\ast_{g}H_{b}=\mu H_{b}

for pairs
(g,b)
consisting of a Lorentzian metric
g
on
M
and a curving
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
, where
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M)
is the curvature of
b
, and
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

By the previous definition, we can consider the six-dimensional minimal supergravity system as a natural
gauge-theoretic
system that couples Lorentzian metrics to curvings on a gerbe.

Remark 9.2
.

Solutions
(g,b)
to equations (
29
) will be called
minimal supergravity solutions
. The first equation in (
29
) is the so-called
Einstein equation
, whereas the second equation in (
29
) is called the self-duality condition for the curving
b
. Curvings on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
are usually called
b-fields
, a terminology that we will use occasionally.

Given
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
, the
configuration space
of the NS-NS system on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
is the set of pairs
(g,b)
consisting of a Lorentzian metric
g
on
M
, and a curving
b
on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
. Given an element
(g,b)
we denote by
\nabla^{g,b}
the unique metric connection on
(M,g)
with totally skew-symmetric torsion given by
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M)
, the curvature of
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
. For ease of notation, we denote by the same symbol
\nabla^{g,b}
its lift to any irreducible complex spinor bundle defined on
(M,g)
.

Definition 9.3
.

A
supersymmetric configuration
on
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},Y)
is a pair
(g,b)
consisting of a Lorentzian metric
g
on
M
, and a curving
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
satisfying the following differential system:

(30)

\nabla^{g,b}\eta=0

for a non-vanishing section
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
of an irreducible complex chiral spinor bundle
S^{\mu}
, of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, defined on
(M,g)
and associated to a strong spin structure.

Therefore, the supersymmetry parameter of a supersymmetric configuration is a particular instance of an irreducible spinor parallel under a metric connection with totally skew-symmetric, closed, torsion.

In this section, we consider the quasi-supersymmetric and supersymmetric solutions of six-dimensional minimal supergravity whose underlying Lorentzian metric belongs to the following class of standard Kundt six-manifolds, which is especially well-adapted to the existence of an isotropic Killing vector field.

Definition 9.4
.

A six-dimensional oriented Lorentzian manifold
(M,g)
is
standard Kundt
, or
Kundt
for short, if it has the following isometry type:

(31)

M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N\,,\qquad g=\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot(e^{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})+h_{\mathfrak{u}},

where,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
is a direct product with Cartesian coordinates
(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})
,
N
is an oriented four-manifold,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(N)
are families of smooth functions on
N
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{1}(N)
is a family of one-forms on
N
, and
h_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of complete Riemannian metrics on
N
.

Similarly to the standard Brinkmann case considered in Section
7
, we say that a standard Kundt manifold is
stationary
if all elements in the tuple
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}},h_{\mathfrak{u}})
are independent of
\mathfrak{u}
, in which case we will drop the subscript
\mathfrak{u}
. On the other hand, we refer to a standard Kundt manifold as
non-twisting
if
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
.

Studying the quasi-supersymmetric or supersymmetric systems of minimal six-dimensional for such a class of metrics yields an evolution differential system defined on the transverse wave front of the underlying Kundt six-manifold. We begin with the corresponding reduction of the self-dual bundle gerbe that defines the given six-dimensional minimal supergravity.

9.2.
Self-dual bundle gerbes on standard Kundt six-manifolds

In the following, we fix a Kundt six-manifold of the form (
31
). Let
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
be a bundle gerbe on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
. Since the goal is to reduce the minimal supergravity system on
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
, we will assume that the
topological data
contained in
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
, namely
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y})
, is given by the pull-back of a bundle gerbe
(\mathcal{P},Y)
defined over the four-dimensional manifold
N
, while we will allow the connective structure
\bar{A}
to be genuinely
\mathfrak{u}
-
dependent
, that is, not necessarily given by the pull-back of a fixed connective structure on
(\mathcal{P},Y)
. We will refer to such bundle gerbes as
reducible
bundle gerbes on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
. This setup captures the dynamics of the
\mathfrak{u}
-evolution problem in its full generality. Since the submersion
\bar{Y}\to M
of a reducible bundle gerbe on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
is, by assumption, a pull-back submersion, it follows that it is isomorphic to:

\bar{Y}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y\to\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N,

where the projection on the interval factors is the identity map and
Y\to N
is the submersion underlying the bundle gerbe
(\mathcal{P},Y)
. Similarly:

\bar{Y}\times_{M}\bar{Y}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y\times_{N}Y\to\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N,

where the projection restricts to the identity map on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. Since
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
is the pull-back of
\mathcal{P}\to Y\times_{N}Y
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y\times_{N}Y
, we have:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y\times_{N}Y

again restricting to the identity on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. The principal action of the group
\mathrm{U}(1)
on
\bar{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times\mathcal{P}
is the one induced by the principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
-action carried by
\mathcal{P}
and the trivial action on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. Let
\bar{A}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times\mathcal{P},\mathbb{R})
be a connective structure on
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
. Then we can write:

\bar{A}=\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+A_{\mathfrak{u}},

where
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
are families of functions on
\mathcal{P}
and
A_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of connections on
\mathcal{P}
. Recall that
\mathfrak{u}
denotes the Cartesian coordinate on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
. Since
\bar{A}
is a connection on
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
, it is in particular invariant under the
\mathrm{U}(1)
-action of the principal bundle
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
, and since this action is ineffective on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
we conclude that
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
define in fact families of invariant functions on
\mathcal{P}
, whence they descend to families of invariant functions on
Y\times_{N}Y
that we denote by the same symbol for ease of notation. Since
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
is a bundle gerbe, it comes equipped with an isomorphism:

(\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{12}\bar{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{23}\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{12}\bar{A}\otimes\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{23}\bar{A})\xrightarrow{\cong}(\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{13}\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{\pi}^{\ast}_{13}\bar{A}),

where
\bar{\pi}\colon\bar{Y}\to M
and the notation
\bar{\pi}_{ij}\colon\bar{Y}\times_{M}\bar{Y}\times_{M}\bar{Y}\to\bar{Y}\times_{M}\bar{Y}
forgets the entry labeled neither by
i
nor
j
. This implies
\delta\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\delta\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
, where
\delta
is the simplicial differential of the C̆ech nerve of
\pi\colon Y\to N
. Since the C̆ech complex for smooth functions is exact, it follows that both
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
descend through
\delta
to a pair of families of functions on
Y
that we denote again by
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
, respectively. Hence, we obtain the following correspondence.

Proposition 9.5
.

There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between reducible bundle gerbes
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
and tuples
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
, consisting of a bundle gerbe
(\mathcal{P},Y)
on
N
, a pair of families of functions
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
Y
, and a family of connective structures
A_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
(\mathcal{P},Y)
.

We will refer to
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
as the
reduction
of
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
. A direct computation gives the following result.

Lemma 9.6
.

The curvature
F_{\bar{A}}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y\times_{N}Y)
of
\bar{A}=\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+A_{\mathfrak{u}}
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle F_{\bar{A}}=\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}+F_{A_{\mathfrak{u}}},

where
\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\colon\Omega(Y\times_{N}Y)\to\Omega(Y\times_{N}Y)
is the exterior derivative on
\Omega(Y\times_{N}Y)
and
F_{A_{\mathfrak{u}}}
is the family of curvatures of
A_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Remark 9.7
.

Since the set of connections on
\mathcal{P}
is an affine space over the one-forms on
Y\times_{N}Y
, the one-form
\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}
, which is a priori a one-form on
\mathcal{P}
, is well-defined as a one-form on the base space
Y\times_{N}Y
.

A curving on
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
is by definition a two-form
\bar{b}\in\Omega^{2}(\bar{Y})
satisfying:

\bar{\delta}\bar{b}=F_{\bar{A}}.

Since
\bar{Y}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times Y
, we can canonically write every curving
\bar{b}
on
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
as follows:

(32)

\bar{b}=\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+b_{\mathfrak{u}}

for uniquely determined families of functions
\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, one forms
\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}
, and two-forms
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
Y
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
be a reducible bundle gerbe with connective structure on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
and let
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
be its reduction on
N
. A two-form
\bar{b}\in\Omega^{2}(\bar{Y})
is a curving on
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
if and only if, in the decomposition given in (
32
),
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of curvings on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}})
and the triplet
(\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfies:

(33)

\delta b_{\mathfrak{u}}=F_{A_{\mathfrak{u}}}\,,\qquad\delta\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad\delta\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}=-\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad\delta\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}

for every
(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}
, where
\delta
is the simplicial differential of the C̆ech nerve of
\pi\colon Y\to N
.

Proof.

Plugging Equation (
32
) into
\bar{\delta}\bar{b}
we have:

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}\bar{b}

\displaystyle=\bar{\delta}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+b_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle=\delta\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\delta\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\delta\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+\delta b_{\mathfrak{u}}.

Using the expression for
F_{\bar{A}}
obtained in Lemma
9.6
and isolating by tensor type in
\bar{\delta}\bar{b}=F_{\bar{A}}
we obtain (
33
) and thus we conclude.
∎

Corollary 9.9
.

There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between curvings
\bar{b}
on a reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
and tuples
(b_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}})
consisting of a family of curvings
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}})
, families of one-forms
\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
Y
, and a family of functions
\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
Y
satisfying Equation (
33
).

Remark 9.10
.

Note that the second, third, and fourth equations in (
33
) can be equivalently written as follows:

\delta(\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}_{Y}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad\delta(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}_{Y}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})=0\,,\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}+\pi^{\ast}f_{\mathfrak{u}}

for a unique family of functions
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
N
. Here we use the same symbols
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
for the families of functions of
Y
whose simplicial differentials are the families of functions in Lemma
9.8
. In particular, from the second equation above, it follows that there exists a unique family of one-forms
\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
N
such that:

\pi^{\ast}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}_{Y}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}.

We will respectively refer to
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}
as the
derived families of functions and one-forms
of the curving
\bar{b}
on the reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
.

Let
\bar{b}=\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}}+b_{\mathfrak{u}}
be a curving on the reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
, defined over the six-dimensional manifold
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
. By Remark
9.10
, it follows that
\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is completely determined by
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
. Hence, we will refer to the reduction of a curving
\bar{b}\in\Omega^{2}(\bar{Y})
as a tuple of the form
(f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
. We have:

\mathrm{d}_{\bar{Y}}\bar{b}=\mathrm{d}_{Y}b_{\mathfrak{u}}+\pi^{\ast}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y}\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}})-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\pi^{\ast}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}},

where
\mathrm{d}_{\bar{Y}}
is the exterior derivative on
\bar{Y}
and
\mathrm{d}_{Y}
is the exterior derivative on
Y
.

Lemma 9.11
.

The family of two-forms
\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y}\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
satisfies
\delta(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y}\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}})=0
for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, where
\delta
denotes the simplicial differential of the C̆ech nerve of
\pi\colon Y\to N
.

Proof.

We compute:

\delta(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y}\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\delta b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\delta\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}F_{A_{\mathfrak{u}}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}\psi_{\mathfrak{u}})=0\,,

where we have used Lemma
9.8
and the fact that
F_{A_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mathrm{d}_{Y^{[2]}}A_{\mathfrak{u}}
.
∎

Since the C̆ech complex for smooth forms is exact, it follows from the previous lemma that there exists a unique family of two-forms
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{2}(N)
on
N
such that:

(34)

\pi^{\ast}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}b_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}_{Y}\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}}\,.

This leads us to introducing the following definition.

Definition 9.12
.

The family of two-forms
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the
derived family of two-forms
of the curving
\bar{b}
on the reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
.

With the previous definition we have:

\mathrm{d}_{\bar{Y}}\bar{b}=\mathrm{d}_{Y}b_{\mathfrak{u}}+\pi^{\ast}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\pi^{\ast}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\pi^{\ast}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\,.

Since
\mathrm{d}_{\bar{Y}}\bar{b}=\bar{\pi}^{\ast}H_{\bar{b}}
for a uniquely determined three-form
H_{\bar{b}}\in\Omega^{3}(M)
, and
\mathrm{d}_{Y}b_{\mathfrak{u}}=\pi^{\ast}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}
, it follows that:

(35)

H_{\bar{b}}=H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}+(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}},

where
H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\in\Omega^{3}(N)
is the curvature of the family of curvings
b_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
. We conclude that every curving
\bar{b}
on a reducible bundle gerbe is equivalent to a tuple
(b_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}})
as introduced in Lemma
9.8
, and this tuple defines in turn families of
derived
one-forms and two-forms
\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
N
as described above. The fact that
H_{\bar{b}}\in\Omega^{3}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N)
is closed translates into:

(36)

\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}=\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}=0\,.

Since
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a family of curvings on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}})
, the second condition holds automatically.

Remark 9.13
.

Using the decomposition given in (
35
), we have:

|H_{\bar{b}}|_{g}^{2}=|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}^{2}-e^{-2\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}-2e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\langle\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}},

where
|-|_{g}^{2}
denotes the pseudo-norm defined by
g
and
|-|_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}^{2}
denotes the norm defined by
h_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Let
\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(N)
be a
k
-form on
N
. A direct computation gives the following relations:

\displaystyle\ast_{g}(u\wedge\alpha)=(-1)^{k+1}u\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\alpha\,,\qquad\ast_{g}(v\wedge\alpha)=(-1)^{k}v\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\alpha\,,

\displaystyle\ast_{g}(u\wedge v\wedge\alpha)=-\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\alpha\,,\qquad\ast_{g}\alpha=u\wedge v\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\alpha\,,

where
u
and
v
are one-forms on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
in terms of which the six-dimensional Lorentzian metric
g
is given by:

g=u\odot v+h.

For our standard Kundt metric we can take:

u:=\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\,,\qquad v:=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}u+e^{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}.

With these definitions, we have:

\displaystyle H_{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge u\wedge v

\displaystyle\quad+u\wedge\Big(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}-v)\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}.

Hence:

\displaystyle\ast_{g}H_{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=u\wedge v\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\Big(H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})-e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}v\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}

\displaystyle\quad-u\wedge\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\Big(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\Big),

from which we immediately obtain the following result.

Lemma 9.14
.

A curving
\bar{b}
on a reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
is
\mu
-self-dual, that is:

\ast_{g}H_{\bar{b}}=\mu H_{\bar{b}}\,,\qquad\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,,

if and only if:

\displaystyle\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(e^{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}+\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}})=\mu(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\,,\qquad\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mu\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}=-\mu\ast_{h_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}+e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{u}})\,,

where
f_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(N)
,
\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{1}(N)
and
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\Omega^{2}(N)
are the derived forms of the reduction of
\bar{b}
.

9.3.
Minimal six-dimensional supergravity on a wave-front

In this subsection, we reduce the differential system (
29
) of minimal six-dimensional supergravity to the wave front of a stationary
non-twisting
standard Kundt six-manifold. Recall that a stationary standard Kundt six-manifold is non-twisting if, using the notation of Definition
9.4
, it has
\mathcal{A}=0
. The general twisting case is remarkably richer and is therefore reserved for a series of separate future publications. We begin by computing the six-dimensional Ricci and scalar curvatures of the Lorentzian metric:

(37)

g=\mathcal{H}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+h

as a differential system on the underlying wave-front
(N,h)
. We first compute the non-zero Christoffel symbols of
g
:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{v}i}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{v}}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{\mathcal{F}}\partial^{k}\mathcal{F}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathfrak{u}i}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{i}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F})\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{k}\mathcal{H}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathfrak{u}i}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=(\Gamma^{h})^{k}_{ij}\,.

Here we have used the fact that the inverse metric of
g
is given by:

\displaystyle g^{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{u}}=g^{\mathfrak{u}i}=0\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{v}}=g^{\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{u}}=e^{-\mathcal{F}}\,,\qquad g^{ij}=h^{ij}\,,

\displaystyle g^{\mathfrak{v}i}=g^{i\mathfrak{v}}=0\,,\qquad g^{\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{v}}=-e^{-2\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}\,.

In particular, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{v}}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}=\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{\mathcal{F}}\partial^{k}\mathcal{F}\partial_{k}\,,\quad\nabla^{g}_{i}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}=\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}}\partial_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{i}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F})\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}\,,

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{u}}\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{k}\mathcal{H}\partial_{k}\,,\quad\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{v}}\partial_{i}=\nabla^{g}_{i}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathcal{F}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}\,,\quad\nabla^{g}_{i}\partial_{j}=\nabla^{g}_{j}\partial_{i}=\nabla^{h}_{i}\partial_{j}\,.

From these expressions, a direct computation gives the following non-zero components of the six-dimensional Ricci tensor:

(38)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{h}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{g}|_{TN\times TN}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ric}^{h}-\nabla^{h}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\,,

where
\nabla^{h\ast}
is the formal adjoint of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{h}
and
\mathrm{Ric}^{h}
denotes the Ricci tensor of
h
. Taking the trace of the Ricci tensor
\mathrm{Ric}^{g}
with
g
, we obtain the scalar curvature of
(M,g)
:

\displaystyle s_{g}=s_{h}+2\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{3}{2}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}\,,

where
s_{h}
is the scalar curvature of the wave-front manifold
(N,h)
.

Proposition 9.15
.

Let
\bar{b}
be a curving on a reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
, and let
g
be a non-twisting, stationary, standard Kundt six-dimensional metric. Then,
(g,\bar{b})
is a solution of six-dimensional minimal supergravity if and only if the following differential system:

(39)

\displaystyle\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{h}-\mathcal{H}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}=|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}-\mathcal{H}|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}\,,\quad\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}-|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

(40)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{h}-\nabla^{h}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=\frac{1}{2}(2H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}-h|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h})\,,

(41)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\alpha_{0}=0\,,\quad\ast_{h}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=-\mu\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\quad\mathrm{d}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}})=0\,,\quad\langle\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}},H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w)\rangle_{h}=0

for all
w\in\Gamma(TN)
, is satisfied by the components of
g
and
\bar{b}
in the splitting (
31
) and (
35
).

Proof.

A pair
(g,\bar{b})
is a solution of six-dimensional minimal supergravity on
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
if and only if:

\mathrm{Ric}^{g}=\frac{1}{2}H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}}\,,\qquad\ast_{g}H_{\bar{b}}=\mu H_{\bar{b}}\,.

Since
g
is by assumption a standard non-twisting Kundt six-dimensional metric on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
as given in (
37
), its Ricci tensor is given in Equation (
38
). We proceed then to decompose the symmetric tensor
H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M\odot T^{\ast}M)
in the splitting given by
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
. First, we compute:

\displaystyle H_{\bar{b}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle=e^{-\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}u-v)+\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,

\displaystyle H_{\bar{b}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\wedge u-\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,

\displaystyle H_{\bar{b}}(w)

\displaystyle=H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w)+e^{-\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}(w))u\wedge v

\displaystyle\quad-u\wedge(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}(w))+e^{-\mathcal{F}}v\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)

for every
w\in\Gamma(TN)
. Using these expressions, a calculation gives:

(42)

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle=|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}-\mathcal{H}e^{-2\mathcal{F}}|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}|_{h}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})

\displaystyle=-e^{-\mathcal{F}}|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}-\langle\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}},\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle_{h}\,,

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}},w)

\displaystyle=\langle\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}},H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w)\rangle_{h}-e^{-\mathcal{F}}\langle\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}},\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)+\mathcal{H}e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)\rangle_{h}\,,

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}},w)

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}},H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w)\rangle_{h}-e^{-\mathcal{F}}\langle\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)\rangle_{h}\,,

\displaystyle(H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}})|_{TN\times TN}

\displaystyle=H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}-e^{-2\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\circ_{h}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})

\displaystyle-e^{-\mathcal{F}}(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ_{h}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ_{h}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}})-\mathcal{H}e^{-2\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ_{h}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}\,.

Combining these equations with the previous decomposition of the Ricci tensor, it follows that the equation
\mathrm{Ric}^{g}=\frac{1}{2}H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}}
is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{h}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}=\frac{1}{2}(|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}-\mathcal{H}e^{-2\mathcal{F}}|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}|_{h}^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{\mathcal{F}}\left(\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\mathcal{F}}|\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{h}-\nabla^{h}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=\frac{1}{2}(H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}-e^{-2\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}})\circ_{h}(\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}))\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}=0\,,\qquad\langle\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}},H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w)\rangle_{h}=e^{-\mathcal{F}}\langle\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}},\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)\rangle_{h}\,,\qquad w\in\Gamma(TN)\,.

On the other hand, by Lemma
9.14
it follows that if
(g,\bar{b})
is a solution to the Einstein equation
\mathrm{Ric}^{g}=\frac{1}{2}H_{\bar{b}}\circ_{g}H_{\bar{b}}
, then
H_{\bar{b}}
is
\mu
-self-dual if and only if:

\ast_{h}\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=-\mu\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\,,\qquad\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mu e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\,.

The second equation is immediately solved by:

\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}:=\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mu e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b_{\tau}}-\mathrm{d}f_{\tau})\mathrm{d}\tau+\alpha_{0}

for a
\mathfrak{u}
-independent one-form
\alpha_{0}\in\Omega^{1}(N)
. Such family of one-forms
\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}
is closed if and only if:

\mathrm{d}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b_{\tau}})=0\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}\alpha_{0}=0\,,

in which case it automatically satisfies
\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
. Combining the previous two sets of equations, we conclude that
(g,\bar{b})
is a solution to minimal six-dimensional supergravity if and only if the equations in the statement of the proposition hold. For this, we have used the following identity:

\displaystyle e^{-\mathcal{F}}\langle\partial_{\mathfrak{u}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{u}}+\mathrm{d}f_{\mathfrak{u}},\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)\rangle_{h}=\mu\langle\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}},\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}(w)\rangle_{h}=-\langle\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}},\ast_{h}(\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\wedge w^{\flat_{h}})\rangle_{h}=-\langle H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}(w),\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle_{h}

for all
w\in\Gamma(TN)
.
∎

Proposition 9.16
.

Let
\bar{b}
be a curving on a reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{A},\bar{Y})
on
M=\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\times\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{v}}\times N
with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
, and let
g
be a non-twisting, stationary, standard Kundt six-dimensional metric. Then,
(g,\bar{b})
is a solution of six-dimensional minimal supergravity only if:

s_{h}=2|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{H}e^{-\mathcal{F}}))=|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

where
s_{h}
is the scalar curvature of
h
.

Proof.

Taking the trace of Equation (
40
), we obtain:

s_{h}+\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}=|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

which, combined with the second equation in (
39
), gives
s_{h}=2|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}
. On the other hand, combining both equations in (
39
) to eliminate
|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}
and
|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}
, we obtain:

0=\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{h}-|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}=\nabla^{h\ast}(\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F})-|\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

which gives the second equation in the statement since
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{H}e^{-\mathcal{F}})
.
∎

Remark 9.17
.

For simplicity in the exposition, we will refer to tuples
(h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfying the system of Proposition
9.15
as solutions of minimal six-dimensional supergravity on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
.

Lemma 9.18
.

Let
(h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
be a solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
, defined over a compact four-manifold
N
without boundary. Then its derived family of two-forms is zero, that is
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
, and consequently
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
is flat and
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{H}
are constant.

Proof.

Integrating the second equation in Proposition
9.16
and using the divergence theorem we get
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
. On the other hand, the second equation in (
39
) can be written as
\nabla^{h*}\mathrm{d}e^{\mathcal{F}}=-e^{\mathcal{F}}|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}
. Integrating again we obtain
H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}=0
, so
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
is flat. Moreover, since
N
is compact, the only harmonic functions are constant, thus
e^{\mathcal{F}}
is constant, which implies that
\mathcal{F}
is also constant. Finally, we obtain that
\mathcal{H}
is constant from the first equation in (
39
).
∎

Hence, if
N
is compact without boundary then the reduced differential system (
39
), (
40
), and (
41
) reduces to the Ricci-flatness condition on
(N,h)
.

Example 9.19
.

As an example of solution to Proposition
9.15
, let us consider
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
,
\mathcal{H}=e^{\mathcal{F}}
and a globally conformally flat ansatz for the transverse four-manifold
N=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{0\}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times S^{3}
with metric:

h=e^{U}(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}h_{S^{3}}),

where
U=U(r)
is a smooth function depending only on the radial coordinate
r
, and
h_{S^{3}}
is the standard round metric on the unit three-sphere
S^{3}
. By spherical symmetry, we assume the three-form gerbe flux is proportional to the Riemannian volume form
\nu_{S^{3}}
on the standard sphere:

H_{b}=\mathfrak{e}\nu_{S^{3}},

where
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant magnetic charge. The Hodge dual of the flux with respect to
h
is given by:

\ast_{h}H_{b}=\mathfrak{e}e^{-U}r^{-3}\mathrm{d}r.

Substituting this expression into the Maxwell equation in Proposition
9.15
(third equation in (
41
)) yields:

\mathrm{d}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b})=\mathrm{d}(\mathfrak{e}e^{\mathcal{F}-U}r^{-3}\mathrm{d}r)=0,

which is automatically satisfied for any choice of radial functions
\mathcal{F}(r)
and
U(r)
. To continue further, we impose the BPS-like constraint linking the longitudinal wave profile to the transverse conformal factor:

\mathcal{F}(r)=-U(r).

Under this assumption, the squared norm of the flux becomes
|H_{b}|^{2}_{h}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{-3U}r^{-6}
. Substituting
\mathcal{F}=-U
into the non-linear dilaton and Einstein equations in Proposition
9.15
(second equation in (
39
) and equation (
40
), respectively) causes the non-linear terms to cancel, and the coupled system reduces entirely to the Laplace equation on the flat Euclidean background
\mathbb{R}^{4}
together with a constraint on the integration constants:

\partial_{r}^{2}e^{U}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}e^{U}=0,\qquad\mathfrak{e}=2\mathfrak{m}.

The general spherically symmetric solution to this equation is the harmonic function:

e^{U(r)}=1+\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{r^{2}},

for a positive constant
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. The Lorentzian metric on
M
is given by
g=e^{\mathcal{F}}(\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v})+h
. However, by the change of coordinates
\mathfrak{v}\mapsto\mathfrak{v}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{u}
, we can get rid of the term
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}
. Thus, we obtain the solution:

g=\Big(1+\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{r^{2}}\Big)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+\Big(1+\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{r^{2}}\Big)(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}h_{S^{3}}),\qquad H_{b}=2\mathfrak{m}\nu_{S^{3}},

which represents a
black brane
in six Lorentzian dimensions and recovers a well-known supersymmetric solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity
[
14
]
.

For the remainder of the article, we consider the case
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
, in which case the system in Proposition
9.15
reduces to the following differential system on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
:

(43)

\displaystyle\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{h}-\mathcal{H}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}+\mathcal{H}|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}=0\,,\quad\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{h}-|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h}\,,

(44)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{h}-\nabla^{h}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=\frac{1}{2}(2H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}-h|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{h})\,,

(45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\alpha_{0}=0\,,\ \quad\mathrm{d}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}})=0\,.

To proceed further, it is convenient to perform the following conformal transformation of the metric, as implemented in the following lemma.

Lemma 9.20
.

A tuple
(h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfies the system in Proposition
9.15
if and only if
(\mathfrak{h}=e^{\mathcal{F}}h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfies:

(46)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+2\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}-\mathcal{H}|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}+\mathcal{H}e^{2\mathcal{F}}|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}=0\,,\quad\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}=0\,,

(47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}(H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}-|H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h})\,,\quad\mathrm{d}\alpha_{0}=0\,,\quad\mathrm{d}(e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}})=0\,.

Proof.

Setting
\mathfrak{h}=e^{\mathcal{F}}h
, we compute:

\nabla^{h}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F})-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}\,,

from which we obtain:

\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}=e^{\mathcal{F}}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}+\langle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}})\,,\qquad\nabla^{h\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}=e^{\mathcal{F}}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}})\,.

Plugging these expressions into the equations in (
43
), we obtain both equations in (
46
). On the other hand, a computation gives the following formulas:

\mathrm{Ric}^{h}=\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{h}}+\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}})\mathfrak{h}\,,\qquad\ast_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}=e^{\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\,.

Plugging these expressions into the equations in (
44
) and (
45
), we obtain both equations in (
47
) after noticing that
H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{h}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}=e^{2\mathcal{F}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}\circ_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b_{\mathfrak{u}}}
.
∎

We now proceed to decouple the system comprising equations (
43
), (
44
), and (
45
), thereby providing a clear procedure for constructing standard Kundt solutions in minimal six-dimensional supergravity with a vanishing derived family of two-forms.

Theorem 9.21
.

Every
\mathfrak{u}
-independent and non-twisting standard Kundt solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity with vanishing derived family of two-forms
\Theta_{\mathfrak{u}}=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times N
determines a solution
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F})
of the system:

(48)

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}(H_{b}\circ_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}-|H_{b}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h})\,,\\
\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}|H_{b}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}=0\,,\\
\mathrm{d}(e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b})=0\end{gathered}

on
(\mathcal{P},Y,A)
together with a harmonic function
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
. Conversely, every tuple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
consisting of a solution
(h,b,\mathcal{F})
to the previous system together with a harmonic function
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
, defines a natural solution to minimal six-dimensional supergravity on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times N
with vanishing derived family of two-forms, Lorentzian metric:

g=\bar{\mathcal{H}}e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}+e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{h}\,,

and curving curvature
H_{\bar{b}}=H_{b}+\mu e^{2\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}
.

Remark 9.22
.

The differential system (
48
) has a strong resemblance to the generalized Ricci soliton system
[
21
]
, albeit being different. It would be interesting to explore whether some of the techniques used to study the generalized Ricci soliton system, see for instance
[
54
,
52
,
53
]
, are also applicable to (
48
).

Proof.

Let
(g,\bar{b})
be a
\mathfrak{u}
-independent non-twisting standard Kundt solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity on the reducible bundle gerbe
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times N
, with reduction
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}},\psi_{\mathfrak{u}},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}})
. Then, by Proposition
9.15
together with Remark
9.17
,
(g,\bar{b})
defines a tuple
(h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfying the differential system (
43
), (
44
), and (
45
). In turn, by Lemma
9.20
, the associated tuple
(\mathfrak{h}=e^{\mathcal{F}}h,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F},f_{\mathfrak{u}},\varrho_{\mathfrak{u}},\vartheta_{\mathfrak{u}},b_{\mathfrak{u}})
satisfies the differential system (
46
) and (
47
). Then, it follows by Equation (
34
) that the family of curvings
b_{\mathfrak{u}}
can be written as follows:

b_{\mathfrak{u}}=b+\mathrm{d}_{Y}k_{\mathfrak{u}}

for a given curving
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
and a family of one-forms
k_{\mathfrak{u}}\in C^{\infty}(Y)
. Hence, there exists a
\mathfrak{u}
-dependent family of gauge transformations of
(\mathcal{P},Y,A_{\mathfrak{u}})
such that the transformed curving, denoted by
b
, is
\mathfrak{u}
-independent. We define the new variable:

\mathcal{H}=\bar{\mathcal{H}}e^{\mathcal{F}}\,.

Plugging this expression into the first equation of (
46
) we obtain:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}|H_{b}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}})=0\,.

Combining this equation with the second equation in (
46
), we obtain
\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathcal{H}}=0
, and thus the Laplacian equations for
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathcal{F}
decouple. In particular,
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
is harmonic on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
and
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F})
satisfies the differential system (
48
).

Conversely, let
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F})
be a solution of the differential system (
48
) on
N
for some bundle gerbe
(\mathcal{P},Y,A)
, whose pull-back to
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times N
will be denoted
(\bar{\mathcal{P}},\bar{Y},\bar{A})
, and let
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
be a harmonic function on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
. We set:

g=\bar{\mathcal{H}}e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{h}\,,\qquad\bar{b}=b+\mathfrak{u}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\pi^{\ast}(\mu e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b})\,,

which implies that the curvature of
\bar{b}
is given by:

H_{\bar{b}}=H_{b}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\mu e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}\,.

Tracing back the computations that lead to the reduced system (
48
) shows that such a choice of
g
and
\bar{b}
is, by construction, a non-twisting solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity.
∎

9.4.
Families of solutions to six-dimensional minimal supergravity

To go beyond the supersymmetric Example
9.19
, we consider
N=\mathcal{I}\times X
for an interval
\mathcal{I}
with Cartesian coordinate
r
, where
X
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with an Einstein metric
h_{X}
with Einstein constant
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We assume that
h
is given by the following warped metric:

\mathfrak{h}=e^{K}(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+h_{X}),

where
K:=K(r)
is a smooth function of the
radial
coordinate
r
. We assume the three-form gerbe curvature is proportional to the volume form of
X
, that is:

H_{b}=\mathfrak{e}\nu_{h_{X}},

where
\nu_{h_{X}}
is the Riemannian volume form on
(X,h_{X})
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}
. The Hodge dual of the flux with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
takes the form:

\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=\mathfrak{e}e^{-K}\mathrm{d}r

and thus the Maxwell equation
\mathrm{d}(e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b})=0
in (
48
) is trivially satisfied for any choice of smooth functions
K(r)
and
\mathcal{F}(r)
. Substituting this ansatz into the remaining equations of reduced system (
48
), and noting that for the aforementioned choice of three-form
H_{b}
on a four-dimensional manifold we have the algebraic identity
H_{b}\circ_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=|H_{b}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}
on the transverse space
X
, the Einstein and dilaton equations reduce to the following set of coupled ordinary differential equations:

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}+K^{\prime}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2(\mathcal{F}-K)}\,,\qquad 3K^{\prime\prime}+2(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})^{2}=2\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2(\mathcal{F}-K)}\,,\quad K^{\prime\prime}+(K^{\prime})^{2}=2\lambda\,,

where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
r
. This system can be recast into the following coupled system of ordinary differential equations for
\mathcal{F}
and
K
:

(49)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}+K^{\prime}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2(\mathcal{F}-K)}\,,\qquad K^{\prime\prime}+(K^{\prime})^{2}=2\lambda

together with the
Hamiltonian constraint
:

(50)

C:=3(K^{\prime})^{2}-2(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})^{2}+2\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2(\mathcal{F}-K)}-6\lambda=0\,.

Assuming that
\mathcal{F}
and
K
satisfy the
evolution equations
(
49
), it follows that the derivative of the Hamiltonian constraint (
50
) satisfies:

C^{\prime}=-2K^{\prime}C\,.

Hence, if
C|_{r=0}=0
at
r=0
, it then follows that
C=0
everywhere
\mathcal{F}
and
K
are defined, and thus we have a well-defined initial data problem. The evolution equation for
K
in (
49
) clearly decouples. Defining
Z=e^{K}
, the equation
K^{\prime\prime}+(K^{\prime})^{2}=2\lambda
reduces to the following linear second-order ordinary differential equation:

(51)

Z^{\prime\prime}=2\lambda Z\,.

Plugging a solution to this equation into the remaining equation in (
49
), gives an explicit ordinary differential equation for
\mathcal{F}
, which can be studied by specific methods depending on the precise solution chosen for
Z
, which depends on the sign of
\lambda
. Finally, the harmonic function
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
required by Theorem
9.21
is determined by the equation
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}+K^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=0
, which yields:

\bar{\mathcal{H}}(r)=c_{1}\int e^{-K(r)}\mathrm{d}r+c_{2}

for integration constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. This provides a systematic construction of examples of
\mathfrak{u}
-independent non-twisting Kundt solutions to minimal six-dimensional supergravity.

We proceed to apply the previous formalism to the
\lambda<0
case, which we solve explicitly. As explained above, the first task is to solve Equation (
51
). Defining the characteristic frequency
k:=\sqrt{2|\lambda|}>0
, the linear equation (
51
) for the warp factor
Z=e^{K}
becomes
Z^{\prime\prime}=-k^{2}Z
, whose general solution with zero phase is given by:

(52)

e^{K(r)}=c\cos(kr)\,,

for a real non-zero constant
c\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
. To ensure a well-defined metric
\mathfrak{h}
, we restrict the radial coordinate to the interval
r\in(-\frac{\pi}{2k},\frac{\pi}{2k})
and hence we take
c>0
to be positive. Hence:

K(r)=\ln(c)+\ln(\cos(kr))

solves the second equation in (
49
), whereas the first equation in (
49
) becomes:

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}-k\tan(kr)\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2\mathcal{F}}}{c^{2}\cos^{2}(kr)}\,,

where we have used that
K^{\prime}=-k\tan(kr)
. In order to proceed further, it is convenient to introduce the coordinate
\rho
, defined via the differential equation
\mathrm{d}\rho=e^{-K}\mathrm{d}r
, which is integrated by:

\rho(r)=\frac{1}{ck}\ln\left|\sec(kr)+\tan(kr)\right|\,.

Note that as
r\to\pm\frac{\pi}{2k}
, the coordinate
\rho\to\pm\infty
, and hence
\rho
spans the whole real line
\mathbb{R}
. In this coordinate, the previous equation for
\mathcal{F}
reduces to the following one-dimensional Liouville equation:

\partial_{\rho}^{2}\mathcal{F}=\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2\mathcal{F}}\,.

On the other hand, substituting the specific profiles for
K^{\prime}=-k\tan(kr)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=e^{-K}\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F}
into the Hamiltonian constraint (
50
), we obtain:

3k^{2}\tan^{2}(kr)-2e^{-2K}\left[(\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F})^{2}-\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2\mathcal{F}}\right]+3k^{2}=0\implies E=\frac{3}{2}c^{2}k^{2}=3c^{2}|\lambda|>0\,,

where
E:=(\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F})^{2}-\mathfrak{e}^{2}e^{2\mathcal{F}}
is the constant, necessarily positive, Liouville energy. Since
E>0
, it follows that
\mathcal{F}
belongs to the hyperbolic branch of the Liouville solution, that is, we have:

e^{-\mathcal{F}(\rho)}=\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\sqrt{E}}\sinh(\sqrt{E}(\rho-\rho_{*}))

for a real integration constant
\rho_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
. Reconstructing the components for the six-dimensional Kundt metric, we find:

\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\rho)=m_{1}\rho+m_{2}\,,\qquad\mathcal{H}(\rho)=\frac{c\sqrt{3|\lambda|}\left(m_{1}\rho+m_{2}\right)}{\mathfrak{e}\sinh\big(c\sqrt{3|\lambda|}(\rho-\rho_{*})\big)}

for real constants
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. The resulting solution describes a wave-front that topologically pinches off as
\rho
approaches the limits
\rho\to\pm\infty
.

The cases
\lambda>0
and
\lambda=0
are solved analogously. Hence, we obtain the following.

Corollary 9.23
.

Every Einstein three-manifold admits a conformal embedding into a
\mathfrak{u}
-independent non-twisting solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity.

Remark 9.24
.

Non-supersymmetric solutions of minimal six-dimensional supergravity have been considered previously in
[
43
]
using a construction based on a general notion of metric contact structure in three dimensions that allows the Reeb vector field to be isotropic. It would be interesting to compare the solutions obtained in this section with the formalism and solutions obtained in
opere citato
.

9.5.
On quasi-supersymmetric wave-fronts

In this subsection, we relate the six-dimensional minimal supergravity solutions that are constructed via solutions of the reduced system presented in Theorem
9.21
with the notion of
quasi-supersymmetry
. That is, by applying Lemma
8.4
, we immediately obtain a key necessary condition for such a solution to be quasi-supersymmetric. This necessary solution is strong enough that we can conclude that solutions of the reduced system (
48
), and in particular the class of solutions obtained in Subsection
9.4
, will typically lead to non-quasi-supersymmetric solutions of minimal six-dimensional supergravity.

Hence, we take
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times N
for an orientable four-dimensional manifold
N
, and we consider the six-dimensional minimal supergravity determined on
M
by a reducible bundle gerbe with connective structure. As explained in Theorem
9.21
, given such a triple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
, the corresponding six-dimensional solution
(g,\bar{b})
has metric
g
given by:

g=e^{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\odot\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v})+e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{h}\,,

whereas the curvature of the curving
\bar{b}
reads:

(53)

H_{\bar{b}}=H_{b}+\mu e^{2\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{v}\wedge\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}\,.

Such a class of six-dimensional solutions may admit various types of skew-torsion parallel spinors with torsion
H_{\bar{b}}
. Given a tuple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
, we say that an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S^{\mu})
on
(M,g)
is
adapted
if its Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu*_{g}u
satisfies
u=e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}
. If such a solution is quasi-supersymmetric with respect to an adapted spinor, then we will say that it is
adapted quasi-supersymmetric
.

Proposition 9.25
.

A solution
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
of the reduced system (
48
) defines an adapted quasi-supersymmetric solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity only if:

\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=-\mu e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\,.

In particular,
\mathfrak{h}
is Ricci-flat.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
8.4
, it follows that a tuple
(h,b,\mathcal{F})
is an adapted quasi-supersymmetric configuration only if:

\nabla^{g}_{w}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u})=\frac{1}{2}H_{\bar{b}}(w,\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}),\qquad w\in\Gamma(TM)\,,

where
H_{\bar{b}}
is given as in Equation (
53
). The symmetric projection of this equation is automatically satisfied. On the other hand, its skew-symmetric projection gives:

e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{u}+H_{\bar{b}}(\partial_{\mathfrak{v}})=0\,,

which, upon use of Equation (
53
), reduces to:

\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=-\mu e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\,.

Assuming this equation, we compute:

\displaystyle(H_{b}\circ_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b})(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{-2\mathcal{F}}\langle\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\wedge w_{1}^{\flat_{\mathfrak{h}}}),\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\wedge w_{2}^{\flat_{\mathfrak{h}}})\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=e^{-2\mathcal{F}}(|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|_{\mathfrak{h}}^{2}\mathfrak{h}-\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F})(w_{1},w_{2})\,,

which plugged into the first equation in (
48
) gives
\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{h}}=0
.
∎

Elaborating on Proposition
9.25
, we obtain the following strict characterization of tuples
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
satisfying
\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=-\mu e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}
and defining
\mathfrak{u}
-independent, non-twisting, solutions of six-dimensional supergravity as explained in Theorem
9.21
.

Proposition 9.26
.

A tuple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
satisfying
\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=-\mu e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}
defines a
\mathfrak{u}
-independent, non-twisting, solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity if and only if
\mathfrak{h}
is Ricci-flat and both
\smash{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}
and
e^{-\mathcal{F}}
are harmonic functions on
(N,\mathfrak{h})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.25
, the Einstein equation of the reduced system (
48
) evaluated on a tuple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
such that
\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b}=-\mu e^{-\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}
simplifies to the condition
\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{h}}=0
. The Maxwell equation in (
48
) is clearly satisfied automatically, since:

\mathrm{d}(e^{2\mathcal{F}}\ast_{\mathfrak{h}}H_{b})=-\mu\,\mathrm{d}(e^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F})=0\,.

For the remaining equation, we observe:

0=\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+e^{2\mathcal{F}}|H_{b}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}=\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}+|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{F}|^{2}_{\mathfrak{h}}=-e^{\mathcal{F}}\nabla^{\mathfrak{h}\ast}\mathrm{d}e^{-\mathcal{F}}

and thus we conclude.
∎

Hence, every tuple
(\mathfrak{h},b,\mathcal{F},\bar{\mathcal{H}})
determining a quasi-supersymmetric solution of minimal six-dimensional supergravity via Theorem
9.21
has conformally Ricci-flat wave-front
(N,h)
, which implies the following.

Corollary 9.27
.

The class of solutions constructed in Subsection
9.4
with Einstein constant
\lambda\neq 0
is not quasi-supersymmetric.

Appendix A
The Kähler-Atiyah model for the Clifford algebra

Let
V
be an oriented
d
-dimensional real vector space equipped with a non-degenerate metric
h
of signature
(p,q)
and let
(V^{*},h^{*})
be the quadratic space dual to
(V,h)
, where
h^{*}
denotes the metric dual to
h
. Let
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
be the real Clifford algebra of the quadratic vector space
(V^{*},h^{*})
, viewed as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded associative algebra with decomposition:

\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathrm{Cl}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})\oplus\mathrm{Cl}^{\mathrm{odd}}(V^{*},h^{*}).

In our conventions the Clifford algebra satisfies:

\theta^{2}=h^{*}(\theta,\theta),\qquad\theta\in V^{*}.

We identify the real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
with the
Kähler-Atiyah algebra
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
, which we denote by
(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
(see
[
10
,
11
]
). The map
\diamond\colon\wedge V^{*}\times\wedge V^{*}\to\wedge V^{*}
denotes the
geometric product
determined by
h
. This is given by the linear and associative extension of the following expression:

(54)

\theta\diamond\alpha=\theta\wedge\alpha+\iota_{\theta^{\sharp}}\alpha,\qquad\theta\in V^{*},\,\alpha\in\wedge V^{*},

where
\theta^{\sharp}\in V
denotes the
h
-dual vector of the one-form
\theta
.

In order to do computations with the geometric product, it is convenient to introduce the
generalized products
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. These are the bilinear operators:

(55)

\triangle_{k}\colon\wedge^{i}V^{*}\times\wedge^{j}V^{*}\to\wedge^{i+j-2k}V^{*},

where
k=0,\ldots,d
, defined through the expansion:

\alpha\diamond\beta=\sum_{k=0}^{d}(-1)^{\binom{k+1}{2}+jk}\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta,\qquad\alpha\in\wedge^{j}V^{*},\,\beta\in\wedge V^{*}.

Choosing a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\}
of
V
we can express the generalized products as:

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=\tfrac{1}{k!}h^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h^{i_{k}j_{k}}(\iota_{e_{i_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{i_{k}}}\alpha)\wedge(\iota_{e_{j_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{j_{k}}}\beta).

Below, we collect some useful properties of the generalized products that we will use in the computations.

Proposition A.1

(
[
33
,
31
,
32
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in\wedge^{a}V^{*}
and
\beta\in\wedge^{b}V^{*}
. Then:

•

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=0
if
k>a
or
k>b
.

•

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=(-1)^{(a-k)(b-k)}\beta\triangle_{k}\alpha
. In particular
\alpha\triangle_{k}\alpha=0
if
a-k
is odd.

•

\alpha\triangle_{0}\beta=\alpha\wedge\beta
and
\alpha\triangle_{a}\beta=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
if
b=a
.

•

\alpha\triangle_{a}(*\beta)=*(\beta\wedge\alpha)
if
a+b\leq d
.

Proof.

The first three properties are clear. To prove the last property we use
\iota_{v}(*\beta)=*(\beta\wedge v^{\flat})
for
v\in V
and
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}
, and
(e_{j})^{\flat}=h_{jk}e^{k}
:

\displaystyle\alpha\triangle_{a}(*\beta)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a!}h^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h^{i_{a}j_{a}}(\iota_{e_{i_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{i_{a}}}\alpha)\wedge(\iota_{e_{j_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{j_{a}}}(*\beta))

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a!}h^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h^{i_{a}j_{a}}\alpha_{{i_{a}}\cdots{i_{1}}}*(\beta\wedge(e_{j_{a}})^{\flat}\wedge\cdots\wedge(e_{j_{1}})^{\flat})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a!}h^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h^{i_{a}j_{a}}h_{j_{a}k_{a}}\cdots h_{j_{1}k_{1}}*(\beta\wedge\alpha_{{i_{a}}\cdots{i_{1}}}e^{k_{a}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{k_{1}})

\displaystyle=*(\beta\wedge(\tfrac{1}{a!}\alpha_{{i_{1}}\cdots{i_{a}}}e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{a}}))

\displaystyle=*(\beta\wedge\alpha).

∎

As a unital and associative algebra, the Kähler-Atiyah algebra
(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
is isomorphic to the Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
through the
h
-dependent
Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism
(see
[
12
,
31
,
32
]
), which we denote by:

(56)

\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)\to\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*}).

We denote by
\pi
the
parity automorphism
of the Kähler-Atiyah algebra, which is defined as the unique unital algebra automorphism which acts as minus the identity on
V^{*}\subset\wedge V^{*}
, and by
\tau
the
reversion anti-automorphism
, defined as the unique unital algebra anti-automorphism which acts as the identity on
V^{*}
. For
\alpha\in\wedge^{a}V^{*}
we have:

\pi(\alpha)=(-1)^{a}\alpha,\qquad\tau(\alpha)=(-1)^{\binom{a}{2}}\alpha.

Note that
\tau\circ\pi=\pi\circ\tau
and
(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=(-1)^{\binom{a+1}{2}}\alpha
.

Let
\nu\in\wedge^{d}V^{*}
be the pseudo-Riemannian volume form on
(V,h)
. Then we have:

\nu\diamond\nu=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{p-q}{2}}&\mbox{ if $d$ is even},\\
(-1)^{\frac{p-q-1}{2}}&\mbox{ if $d$ is odd},\end{cases}

and:

\nu\diamond\alpha=\pi^{d-1}(\alpha)\diamond\nu,\qquad\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}.

Moreover, the volume form
\nu
satisfies the following properties:

Proposition A.2
.

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
and dimension
d
. Then the following identities hold for all
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}
:

(57)

\alpha\diamond\nu=*\tau(\alpha),\qquad\nu\diamond\alpha=*(\pi^{d-1}\circ\tau)(\alpha).

Proof.

Since multiplication by the volume form
\nu
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear, it suffices to consider homogeneous elements
\alpha=e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}
with
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d
, where
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. Set
\varepsilon_{i}:=h^{*}(e^{i},e^{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let us start with
\alpha=e^{i}
. On one hand we have:

e^{i}\diamond\nu=(-1)^{i-1}\varepsilon_{i}e^{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{i-1}\diamond e^{i+1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{d}.

On the other hand we have
e^{i}\diamond(*e^{i})=e^{i}\wedge(*e^{i})=h^{*}(e^{i},e^{i})\nu=\varepsilon_{i}\nu
, thus:

*e^{i}=\varepsilon_{i}(e^{i})^{-1}\diamond\nu=(-1)^{i-1}\varepsilon_{i}e^{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{i-1}\diamond e^{i+1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{d}

and
e^{i}\diamond\nu=*e^{i}=*\tau(e^{i})
. Arguing analogously for
\alpha=e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}
we get on one hand:

\alpha\diamond\nu=(-1)^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-k}\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}e^{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{i_{1}-1}\diamond e^{i_{1}+1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{i_{k}-1}\diamond e^{i_{k}+1}\diamond\cdots\diamond e^{d},

and on the other hand:

(-1)^{\binom{k}{2}}*(e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}})=\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}(e^{i_{1}})^{-1}\diamond\cdots\diamond(e^{i_{k}})^{-1}\diamond\nu=\alpha\diamond\nu.

Hence
\alpha\diamond\nu=(-1)^{\binom{k}{2}}*\alpha=*\tau(\alpha)
. Using
\nu\diamond\alpha=(-1)^{k(d-1)}\alpha\diamond\nu=\pi^{d-1}(\alpha)\diamond\nu
we conclude.
∎
